Hitopadesa, or “Good instructions”, is famous for its wisdom and is one of the most popular books on ethics and polity from niti-sastra. Many verses can be traced to the Puranas and Itihasas. It uses the story-within-a-story-format, with animals as the main characters. The first book, Mitralabha, or Acquisition of friends, defines the characteristics of real friends and shows the importance of having them. It is the most relevant in this present age, Kaliyuga, where shams thrive in the guise of love and good-will.

# The Hitopadesa (SNd)

Satya Narayana Dasa

## Preface

The Vedas explain four principal goals — religious practice (dharma), economic development (artha), regulated sense enjoyment (kama), and liberation from the cycle of birth and death (moksa). They ultimately teach about a fifth goal (pancama purusartha), love of God, prema, but this is not commonly known. The Vedas assert that to achieve any of these goals, integrity is required.

Different Vedic literatures are classified as dharma, artha, kama, or moksa sastra, according to the particular goal promoted. Thus Hitopadesa, which gives moral instructions (niti), falls within the category of artha-sastra, advocating economic development within the parameters of morality. The main character in the book is asked to educate the simple-minded sons of a king and train them as rulers. The foolish princes are not inclined to study, so the ingenious teacher tells engaging fables to capture their attention, simultaneously weaving in ethics, morality, and other character-building principles to elevate them.

Hitopadesa is popular with children because of these fables, in which animal characters are used to personify certain traits found in humans. The book is also popular with adults and even scholars because of its wisdom. Those who may have other editions of Hitopadesa should note that there are variant readings in different Sanskrit editions. The basis for this present translation is the Chaukhambha Sanskrit Series edition printed in 1982. Scholarly readers will appreciate the Devanagari script, transliteration, and word-for-word translation; and all readers will enjoy the translation and extensive commentary.

It will be obvious to readers that Sri Satya Narayana Dasa is not pursuing economic gain (artha), but is dedicated to achieving the pancama purusartha, or fifth and ultimate goal of life, prema-bhakti, or love of God. His commentary demonstrates how the predominating desire of the reader determines how the wisdom of Hitopadesa is applied. His explanations are especially valuable, for they demonstrate how these ethical principles can aid one to achieve the ultimate goal of life.

The crucial task for one striving for the ultimate goal is finding a bona fide teacher. Finding such a guide is not accidental; it requires integrity, sincerity of purpose, and good discrimination. The importance of recognizing a bona fide spiritual master is emphasized in several places. In the beginning of Bhagavad-gita Arjuna asks, “What are the symptoms of one who is fixed in transcendence?” The first chapter of Srimad- Bhagavatam defines the symptoms of one qualified for the post of spiritual teacher. The first chapter of the Caitanya-caritamrta minutely explains the different functions and qualifications of the various spiritual masters. The first verse in Sri Upadesamrta tells who is fit to accept disciples. Hitopadesa, though an artha-sastra, begins with the story called “The Tiger and the Traveler”, which teaches how to distinguish between a pseudo religionist and a mahatma, or a great soul, thus establishing the utility of the book for those seeking a guide to the ultimate goal of life.

A knife in the hand of a dacoit can take a life; the same knife in a doctor’s hand can save a life. Similarly, Hitopadesa can be used to exploit others, or to assist in accomplishing the four common goals mentioned, or the topmost goal, love of God.

The basic principles of morality in Hitopadesa offer a firm foundation for developing character as well as spiritual advancement, as one who is dishonest, deceitful, duplicitous, violent, envious, lusty, greedy, and so on cannot make tangible spiritual progress. Practice of its moral principles helps one develop the mode of goodness and keen skill in discriminating, which is essential for dealing with the frauds in modern- day society, who are like the jackals of these fables.

Our sincere hope is that the commentary of this edition will help the reader realize the superiority of the pancama purusartha. Kali-yuga is characterized by laziness, hypocrisy, and quarrel, consequently modern man is uninterested in the ultimate goal of life. For this reason, Srila Vyasadeva wrote the Mahabharata, filled with fascinating stories which attract the attention of the common man. Yet at the climactic moment before the great Battle of Kuruksetra began, he presented Bhagavad- gita, which points to the actual goal of life.

Sri Narayana Pandita, while tutoring his frivolous students, whose goal is economic success, used the same technique as Vyasadeva. Inspired by the same compassion Vyasa showed, Narayana Pandita nestles the most essential teaching within his instructions on morality:

*saṁsāra-viṣa-vṛkṣasya dve hy eva rasavat phale*  
*kavyāmṛta-rasāsvādaḥ saṅgamaḥ saj-janaiḥ saha*

**The poisonous tree of the material world yields only two succulent fruits — nectarean literature, and saintly association. (146)**

*sat-saṅgaḥ keśave bhaktir gaṅgāmbhasi nimajjanam*  
*asāre khalu saṁsāre trīṇi sārāṇi bhāvayet*

**In this useless material world there are only three valuable things: saintly association, devotion to Lord Kṛṣṇa, and bathing in the Ganges (147).**

The Publishers

## Introduction

About 1, 500 years ago the King of Iran got a book with the secret of how to raise the dead by means of a rasayana, an elixir of life. The book explained how the elixir was extracted from herbs and trees growing on the high mountains of India. Eager to sample this elixir, the King sent his chief minister on a quest for the prescribed herbs and trees.

In India, the minister was well-received and aided by sages, he scoured the mountains for the herbs and trees to make the elixir. No mixture they made, however, could bring the dead back to life. Finally, the disappointed minister concluded that the information was false.

Greatly distressed about returning empty-handed and disappointing his king, the minister asked his hosts what he should do. They led him to a famous philosopher, who once searched in vain for the same elixir, and in the end discovered that the elixir was actually a book. The philosopher explained that the story about the elixir was allegorical. The high mountains in the story represented the wise and learned men of lofty intellect; the trees and herbs, which are products of the mountains, indicated the various writings of those sages; the elixir indicated the wisdom extracted from the sages’ writings, which revived the dead intelligence and buried thoughts of ignorant materialistic men.

Relieved and elated, the minister begged a copy of the book from the philosopher, translated it, and returned to his king. That book we know today as the Pancatantra of Visnu Sarma; it is the basis of the Hitopadesa. As Visnu Sarma is the narrator in both books, he was thought the author of Hitopadesa. Study of old hand-written manuscripts reveal, however, that Narayana Pandit, who lived in Fourteenth Century Bengal, wrote the book on the request of King Dhavalacandra, drawing heavily from Pancatantra. Traditionally it was taught to beginning students in gurukula.

Hitopadesa, or “Good Instructions,” being famous for its wisdom, is one of the most popular books on ethics and polity from Niti-sastra. Many verses can be traced to the Puranas and Itihasas. It uses the story- within-a-story-format, with animals as the main characters. It has four books or chapters: Mitralabha, Suhrdbheda, Vigraha, and Sandhi.

This edition presents book one, Mitralabha (Acquisition of Friends), which defines the characteristics of real friends and shows the importance of having them. It is most relevant in this present age, Kali-yuga, where shams thrive in the guise of love and good will. The other chapters are useful for monarchs. Consequently their practical value under democracy is limited, though not obsolete.

The Prologue stresses the importance of education. “Just as designs carved on a clay pot can never be changed after firing, so impressions created on the minds of young boys remain for the rest of their lives (8).” This bit of wisdom is the source of the modern adage that what is learned in the cradle is carried to the grave. Thus intelligent and responsible leaders should take great care what they impress on the minds of the youth.

It is common knowledge that beginning students learn more effectively by singing rhymes, since this evokes emotions and aids memorization. Though this method is employed today using nursery rhymes, these are generally inane and of no practical value. In contrast, ancient Vedic teachers taught simple, melodious Sanskrit verses. These hold invaluable wisdom, which blossomed as the child matured; indeed, as the years passed, the verses became more meaningful.

It should be noted that Sri Narayana Pandita uses a dialectic technique called purva-paksa — presenting a piece of conventional wisdom, which is actually detrimental to spiritual progress, and is the antithesis to what he wants to establish. After giving the antithesis, he systematically refutes it and presents the actual conclusion (siddhanta). This method is used in the Srimad-Bhagavatam and other Vedic literatures. I have pointed out these instances in the commentary so the reader may not mistake purva-paksa statements for Siddhanta.

Niti is constructed from the root nin prapane, “to carry” by applying the kt suffix. It means “guidance” or “that which carries one to his desired goal.” Kautilya Arthasastra states that niti is a body of knowledge which offers guidance, direction, ethics, polity, science of morality, and so on.

Hitopadesa was specifically studied by kings and is thus also known as Raja-niti or Dharma-niti. Though it is especially relevant for kings or government heads, it is useful for everyone, since we are all leaders in some capacity, even if only in the family unit. Hitopadesa gives realized knowledge, not theoretical speculation. If followed in good faith, one will realize its validity, and experience the ultimate good. Careful study of Hitopadesa enlightens one regarding human nature and makes one competent.

Lord Kṛṣṇa says, nitir asmi jigisatam, “I am morality of those desiring victory” (Bg. 10. 38). Of course, Lord Kṛṣṇa knows how to use Niti-sastra best, and His pastimes practically demonstrate the rules to be followed. At the conclusion of Bhagavad-gita (Bg. 18. 78), Sanjaya declares:

*yatra yogeśvaraḥ kṛṣṇo*  
*yatra pārtho dhanur-dharaḥ*  
*tatra śrīr vijayo bhūtir*  
*dhruvā nītir matir mama*

**Wherever there is Kṛṣṇa, the master of all mystics, and wherever there is Arjuna, the supreme archer, there will also certainly be opulence, victory, extraordinary power, and morality (niti). That is my opinion.**

Generally, good instructions are like an arrow in the heart, because they put us in conflict with our desires, and only a true well- wisher will risk offering beneficial but unappealing advice. The wise Vidura says, “Oh Maharaja Dhrtarastra, flatterers who speak sweetsounding words are abundant. Rare are those who utter bitter but helpful words, and rare are those who listen. Only sincere persons heed such advice. Others are offended.” (Vidura-niti 5. 16)

Hitopadesa is an especially helpful tool in decision-making, as it teaches one the valuable art of discrimination. During moments of decision fate is written. Even after studying vast literature, one may fail to make sound decisions; yet decisions are what change man’s fate. In moments of indecision and doubt, Hitopadesa can illuminate one’s intelligence, inspire one to face daily problems, as well as help one overcome obstacles on the path of enlightenment.

Some principles of Hitopadesa may not appeal to the modern intellect, as in this day people are blind to the superior goals of life. The average person aspires for material prosperity, power, and popularity. Indeed possession of material objects, name, and fame are accepted as the hallmarks of success. Modern man considers economic equality, along with political and social freedom, the ultimate achievements. These are not bad goals, but even a casual reading of the Vedic literature shows that they are not the ultimate aims of human life.

Another misconception is that all knowledge is acquired by the scientific method, based on direct perception and inference. Unfortunately this is far from the truth. The Vedic sages rely on the foremost process for acquiring knowledge, sabda pramana, or study of the Vedas and Vedic literature, for they offer knowledge which cannot be gained from any other process. The seventh century Vedic scholar, Kumarila Bhatta confirms this in Sloka-varttika:

*pratyaksenanumitya va yas tupayo na vidyate*  
*enam vidanti vedena tasmat vedasya vedata*

**That solution which can neither be attained through direct perception, nor by inference, is known through the Vedas: Indeed, this is the special characteristic of the Vedas.**

Vedic sages lived by a different set of norms and values than what guides us today. Vedic society aimed at achieving goals beyond economic development and political freedom. Material necessities were not neglected, but the social system was oriented towards achieving a spiritual reality not accessible by sense perception and inferential reasoning.

Contrary to the misconception that gross and subtle sense gratification is the ultimate source of pleasure and peace, Vedic seers offer happiness via renunciation, tyagac chantir anantaram. The import is that unless one gives up material desires, there is no possibility of peace:

**A person who has given up all desires for sense gratification, who lives free from desires, who has given up all sense of proprietorship and is devoid of false ego — he alone can attain real peace. (Bg.** [**2.71**](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/2.71)**)**

And such a state can be attained by recognizing the Supreme Person:

**A person in full consciousness of Me, knowing Me to be the ultimate beneficiary of all sacrifices and austerities, the Supreme Lord of all planets and demigods, and the benefactor and well- wisher of all living entities, attains peace from the pangs of material miseries. (Bg.** [**5.29**](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/5.29)**)**

Thus we see that Vedic society is based on a different paradigm than contemporary society. Even today, those who observe Vedic tenets are more satisfied and peaceful than graduates of the educational system, which, in the name of creating a secular society, makes no attempt to train one in the higher principles of life. As explained at the end of the Preface, such training is the purpose of Hitopadesa. Therefore, though it may not agree with modern ideals, the reader is advised, after due consideration, to accept the wisdom of Hitopadesa.

## Prologue

### Text 1

*siddhiḥ sādhye satām astu prasādāt tasya dhūrjaṭeḥ*  
*jāhnavī-phena-lekheva yan-mūrdhni śaśinaḥ kalā*

**Перевод:**

**By the grace of Lord Siva, whose head is adorned with a crescent moon appearing like a streak of foam on the Ganges, may virtuous students attain their ideals.**

**Комментарий:**

*vande'ham śrī-gurum śrī-yuta-pada-kamalam śrī-gurūn vaiṣṇavāṁś ca*  
*śrī-rūpaṁ sāgrajātaṁ saha-gaṇa-raghunāthānvitaṁ taṁ sa-jīvam*  
*sādvaitaṁ sāvadhūtaṁ parijana sahitaṁ kṛṣṇa-caitanya-devam*  
*śrī-rādhā-kṛṣṇa-pādan saha-gaṇa-lalitā-śrī-viśākhānvitāṁś ca*

In Vedic culture every endeavor begins with a mangalacarana, which literally means “auspicious act.” It invokes the Lord’s blessings to overcome all obstacles so the work may be successful. No endeavor can be successful unless sanctioned by the Supreme Lord. Vedic philosophy teaches that even the efforts of atheists, when successful, must be owing to their pious deeds performed in the past.

Mangalacarana can be performed orally or mentally. One might wonder why, then, it is included as part of the book. By seeing it, readers may learn this Vedic etiquette. Here the author, Sri Narayana Pandita, invokes the blessings of Lord Siva, a great Vaisnava. One gets the mercy of the Lord through the Vaisnavas, and as Sukadeva Gosvami recommends, vidya-kamas tu girisam (Bhag. [2.3.7](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-2/3.7)), it is customary for one desiring education to pray to Lord Siva.

Lord Siva sustains the Ganges on his head, indicating that he has the blessings of Lord Visnu, for the Ganges water has washed the Lord’s lotus feet. Just as the Ganges flows from Lord Visnu, so all knowledge flows from Him. Hitopadesa, which means “good instruction”, is based on the Pancatantra and other similar works derived from the Vedic literature. Hence the knowledge in Hitopadesa ultimately comes from Lord Visnu, the source of Vedic knowledge.

The reference to the Ganges and the moon in conjunction with Lord Siva hints at the qualities of a bona fide student. Both have a cooling effect. Similarly, to acquire knowledge, the student must have a cool head. The moon further indicates that knowledge is brilliant and luminous, and Lord Siva bearing the Ganges on his head indicates his humility and respect. A proud student cannot progress very far in learning. The name of Lord Siva used in this verse, Dhurjati, means (“one who has matted hair. ’’ It indicates his austere nature. The student must also be austere, minimizing other interests and giving his full attention to the subject at hand.

Satam, as used in this verse, refers to gentle, well-behaved students. The author does not bless students who lack this qualification. Hitopadesa teaches one to develop character, and at the onset, the author knows that only the gentle and well-behaved will fully benefit from his good instruction. Sadhya (goal, ideal) refers to education, and siddhi (perfection) refers to acquiring proficiency in niti. The author is saying, “By the grace of Lord Siva, may the properly motivated students of this book achieve their goals. ’’ He describes these goals in the next verse.

### Text 2

*śruto hitopadeśo 'yaṁ pāṭavaṁ saṁskṛtoktiṣu*  
*vācāṁ sarvatra vaicitryaṁ nīti-vidyāṁ dadāti ca*

**Перевод:**

**After studying Hitopadesa, one will be expert in using Sanskrit idioms, his speech will always be wonderful, and he will be learned in niti-vidya, the conduct of human affairs.**

**Комментарий:**

Traditionally Hitopadesa is taught to children. It is a psychological fact that knowledge attained in verse in childhood is retained longer in our memory. For this reason Vedic teachers wrote in verse form. These slokas are linguistically simple, like nursery rhymes, yet they have deep meaning. Unlike nursery rhymes, which are generally meaningless songs that lose all utility once the student matures, Hitopadesa increases in utility as the young students become sophisticated, endowing them with wisdom for lifelong use.

In Vedic education language is given much stress as it is the tool for communicating, and childhood is the proper time to instill the fundamental language skills. According to Patanjali Muni, author of the Mahabhasya commentary on the Panini Sutras: ekaḥ śabdaḥ suprayuktaḥ samyag-jñātaḥ svarge loke ca kāmadhug bhavati, “Even if only one word is learnt and used properly, it can fulfill all of one’s desires.” This is because sabda is a form of Lord Visnu, sabda murti-dhara (Visnu Purana [1.22.84](https://vedarama.com/en/books/vp/1.22.84)).

One of the indispensable characteristics of a great man is good speech. Two popular sayings are, “A bird is known by its song, a man by his words”, and “A well-dressed fool goes unrecognized until he speaks.” Study of Hitopadesa makes one’s speech wonderful (vacam sarvatra vaicitryam), and simultaneously offers knowledge of niti sastra. It must be understood, however, from a bona fide teacher — that is the implication of srutah (heard).

By explaining what specifically is to be gained from studying Hitopadesa — expertise in idiomatic Sanskrit, eloquent speech, and wisdom in human affairs — the reader’s interest is heightened. Next, in five verses, the author glorifies education.

### Text 3

*ajarā’maravat prājño vidyām arthaṁ ca cintayet*  
*gṛhīta iva keśeṣu mṛtyunā dharmaṁ ācaret*

**Перевод:**

**An intelligent person should strive for education and wealth as though free from old age and death; but he should perform religious acts as if death has caught him by the hair.**

**Комментарий:**

Some fortunate souls have a natural inclination to study without any encouragement — they are like a hungry man who needs no dietary aids to whet his appetite — but they are very rare. In Kali-yuga especially, students are generally not keen to study. They cheer when a public holiday falls on a school day; indeed they feel relieved at the close of the school day. To offer needed encouragement, the author extolls the importance of education.

Kali-yuga conditions us to seek instant gratification and the resulting consumerist society offers so many gadgets for bodily comfort and distractions for entertainment that most have become averse to any course of activity that takes time to perfect. Consumerism is the result of our attempt to avoid austerity — ease is progress. Hence we are often averse to the austerity of studying, which yields fruit only after a prolonged period of time.

The original and main purpose of education is to gain wisdom and to develop good character. Nowadays, however, the purpose has declined to learning moneymaking skills. We find few who are interested in true education, since it requires the austerity of delaying gratification. When one wants to justify avoiding his studies, his mind rationalizes like this: “Why study? It will all be lost at death. Why work hard for 15 or 20 years to become learned? Why not enjoy instead? In old age one cannot enjoy, and at death all is lost, so better to enjoy now.”

Carvaka Muni, the epicurian philosopher, sums it up:

*yāvaj jīvet sukhaṁ jīvet ṛṇaṁ kṛtvā ghṛtam pibet*  
*deha-bhasmī-bhūtasya punar āgamanaṁ kutaḥ.*

**As long as one is alive one should live happily. Wealth is required for enjoyment. So if one is poor, he should beg, borrow, or steal to enjoy delicacies made with ghee. This body is burnt to ashes after death and there is no rebirth (so one should fully enjoy in this life).**

The wise author of Hitopadesa counters such short-term thinking by encouraging his readers to assume the attitude of immortality (amaravat), while undergoing the quest for knowledge. He correctly points out that the attitude of mortality will not serve one well while pursuing either wealth or education; it will not help one maximize his potential. The mind might protest, “Being immortal is one thing, but what about old age? When our senses are feeble we cannot enjoy.”

Sri Narayana Pandita says that we should not only think of ourselves as immortal, but as eternally youthful (ajara). This is the optimum spirit in which wealth and education should be pursued. Whereas death and old age are bodily changes, the soul is immortal. Hence the author is indirectly asking the reader to be conscious of the soul, to adopt spiritual vision.

Even if our goal is sensual enjoyment, wealth is necessary, which we generally cannot obtain without education. Sometimes uneducated persons achieve vast wealth by some good fortune, but it does not last long, for a fool and his money are soon parted. Indeed the two were lucky to get together in the first place. In America, for instance, studies have shown that the great majority of those who become instant multi-millionaires by winning the lottery, often return to their former financial status within two to five years. Several take only one year to lose their immense fortune, because these lucky winners lack the wisdom to handle wealth in a long-term way.

So wealth and education go together, but of the two the author hails education first, vidyam artham ca. It is the greatest wealth. The hedonist, to make the most of his opportunities, requires vidya, and the spiritualist, interested in salvation, also needs vidya. The Vedas say rte jnanan na muktih, “One cannot get liberation without acquiring knowledge.” Lord Kṛṣṇa states that knowledge is the most purifying element, na hi jnanena sadrsam pavitram iha vidyate.

Study, however, is best done during youth when the memory is strong and the mind is not burdened with anxieties. Later in life, it is much more difficult to study, especially if one has not practiced in his youth. Thus in the Vedic culture, brahmacari life, the first twenty-five years, is exclusively devoted to study. The primary meaning of brahmacari is “celibate student.” The practice of celibacy is a requirement for being a good student, as after marriage one’s attention is diverted by many other responsibilities.

A doubt may be raised: One who considers himself immortal may not hesitate to act sinfully as he will not fear punishment after death. The author writes the second half of the verse to negate this idea — we should act religiously, considering that we may die at any moment. We should not commit sins, knowing well that we will suffer the reaction. When we witness death, a temporary sense of religiosity and renunciation take hold, called smasana-vairagya, or crematorium renunciation. If we continuously remember that death is imminent, however, we can actually be renounced and religious-minded always. Materialistic people think this outlook is morbid, but many saintly persons advocate such realistic thinking as a means to attain optimal spiritual consciousness; and contrary to expectations, saintly persons are not morose by this practice, but are joyful.

A story illustrates this point. A man once approached a saint and said, “My mind always hankers for sense pleasure. I'm never happy, never satisfied. I'm always morose and miserable. Please help me control it.”

The saint closed his eyes and said, “I’ll definitely help you, but first hear this important message. You are going to die within a week, so you must quickly finish all your business and return here within two days.” Startled, the man immediately went home and told his family of the saint’s prophesy. He removed himself from his business, mindfully visited temples, gave charity, and studied scripture, which left him no time for sense gratification. In this way two days passed very quickly and he returned to the saint, who asked, “While you were preparing to meet your death, did your mind hanker for sense pleasure?”

“Not at all,” the man replied. “I had no time to even think of sense gratification. I used all my time for pious activities with the hope of achieving a good birth in my next life. I also realized I cannot take my wealth with me, so I used it for religious purposes.”

The saint said, “I have satisfied your request to help you control your mind. Always remember you may die at any moment and your mind will not hanker for sense pleasure.”

This made a deep impression on the man, and though he didn’t die within a week, from that day on he controlled his mind and led a pious life. Sri Narayana Pandita here applies similar wisdom and advises that we should think ourselves immortal in the quest for wealth and knowledge, yet while executing religious acts we should think that death has caught us by the hair. As in the story, this outlook will give us the optimum result from our life, regardless of how long we live.

Nevertheless, one may conclude that nothing can be accomplished without wealth. He may thus dedicate himself to its accumulation from a very young age, thinking that even if he does not earn much at first, he will acquire valuable experience, which will lead to success later. The author responds to this attitude in the next verse.

### Text 4

*sarva-dravyeṣu vidyaiva dravyam āhur anuttamam*  
*ahāryatvād anarghatvād akṣayatvāc ca sarvadā*

**Перевод:**

**The best wealth is knowledge — it cannot be stolen, it is priceless and imperishable.**

**Комментарий:**

Here are three reasons why vidya is the best wealth. Knowledge cannot be stolen (aharyatvat). A rich man is always anxious that his wealth may be plundered or embezzled, lost in speculative business or gambling, or that he or his relatives will be held for ransom. Today’s rich man can easily become tomorrow’s pauper. As told in the Mahabharata, Yudhisthira Maharaja tost his kingdom in a day and Dhrtarastra lost all his sons in eighteen days along with the kingdom they had acquired by intrigue against their cousins, the Pandavas. Material wealth and happiness is compared with the instability of a drop of water on a lotus leaf. A short story is told to illustrate the precarious nature of riches.

Once two brothers left their village to seek their fortune. One brother entered a gurukula and studied. The other became an apprentice to a rich merchant. After some years they decided to visit their parents and and offer whatever they had earned. On the way, dacoits attacked and seized all the wealth of the brother who was the merchant’s apprentice. The other brother, who had only books, was left untouched.

Knowledge cannot be taken away. It is never burdensome to carry, and it causes no anxiety. Rather, it alleviates one’s anxiety. Therefore, knowledge is the most stable wealth. Furthermore, spiritual knowledge is never lost, even after the demise of the body. It is a permanent asset.

Education is priceless (anarghatvat). Although worldly affluence is limited, vidya is unlimited, for no one can estimate the extent or value of his knowledge. With knowledge, one can earn any amount of wealth. Consultants in different fields earn millions, while their knowledge remains intact and even increases. Knowledge is also priceless in the sense that it cannot be purchased like other commodities; it has to be earned by individual effort.

Education is imperishable (aksayatvat). Worldly riches diminish when distributed, thus most people are not enthusiastic to give charity. Vidya, however, increases when shared with others. This is especially true with spiritual knowledge. A man enriched with transcendental knowledge can distribute his wealth unlimitedly, yet his wealth of realization increases. This can be practically tested when one tries to explain the philosophy of Vedic literature, which presents the path of pure devotional service to Lord Kṛṣṇa as the true culminaton of all knowledge. The more one speaks on the Vedic teachings, the more one’s store of transcendental knowledge increases. This experience is available to anyone who does not adulterate the true spirit and intent of the Lord’s instructions to Arjuna.

Kṛṣṇa says in Bhagavad-gita, svalpam apy asya dharmasya, “In this endeavor there is no loss or diminution.” When one dies, his worldly wealth is left behind, but spiritual knowledge is never lost. It is carried with his subtle body to his next life. In this way, spiritual knowledge is superior to worldly opulence.

In this connection, there is a story about a proud king who was attached to worldly opulence. A saintly person once called on him, and noticing the king’s strong attachment, suddenly became very serious. “Why have you become so grave?” asked the King.

“I can foresee,” replied the saint, “that very soon you will die; but don’t worry. Your pious deeds will promote you to the heavenly sphere. There you will enjoy far greater opulence than now. There is one problem, however. Your heavenly palace will be infested with mosquitoes, and though you will have a nice mosquito net, it will have a hole in which the mosquitoes will enter and bite you.”

Though the king liked the saint’s message, the bit about the mosquitoes disturbed him. for he hated them. However he assured the saint that he’ll simply have the net mended.

The saint replied, “That’s alright, except for the fact that in heaven there are no needles. So when you die, be sure to take a needle with you.”

“But how can I take a needle with me?”

“I have no idea,” said the saint, “but if you cannot even take a needle with you, then why are you so proud of your earthly opulence?”

Hearing this, the king realized the futility of his attachment and gave up his false pride. Therefore, among all types of wealth, knowledge is the best. Sri Sukracarya says (Sukra-niti, 3.180):

*vidyā-dhanam śreṣṭhataraṁ tan mulaṁ itarat-dhanam*  
*dānena vardhate nityam na bhārāya na nīyate*

**Vidya is superior to material wealth as it is the cause of earning all other wealth; it always increases when given in charity; it is never burdensome to carry; and no one can ever forcibly take it away.”**

Sri Narayana Pandita next explains how one born in a low family can achieve association with the rich if he is learned.

### Text 5

***saṁyojayati vidyaiva nīcagā'pi naraṁ sarit***  
***samudraṁ iva durdharṣaṁ nṛpaṁ bhāgyamataḥ param***

**Перевод:**

**As it river carries a blade of grass down to the great ocean, so knowledge, though possessed by a common man, can carry one to a king, who is generally unapproachable and whose audience brings good fortune.**

**Комментарий:**

Knowledge is respected everywhere, and one who possesses it can even attract the attention of world leaders, who are generally inaccessible to common men. Heads of state may be respected in their own country, but a scholar is honored worldwide, svadese pujyate raja vidvan sarvatra pujyate.

The life of the great poet, Sri Harsa, demonstrates how good education can elevate one to associate with the king. Once he traveled to Kashmir to have darsana of the deity of Sarasvati, the goddess of learning. There he sought audience of the king but a guard, considering him an ordinary man, denied his request.

Sri Harsa went to the riverbank for his evening meditation. Two women carrying clay pots for fetching water got into a quarrel as the poet attentively listened. Eventually a fight started and family members from both sides joined in. The fight became a melee.

The incident was reported to the king, who despite listening to the versions of both parties, could not determine who was at fault. He asked if there had been anv witnesses at the river, and the women told him of the brahmana who quietly observed the whole incident. The king summoned the poet to his court as a witness. When asked to give an account of the fight, Sri Harsa said, “I do not understand the local language, but I can repeat everything that was said,” and to the king’s amazement, he narrated every word spoken by each person during the incident.

Honored to have a scholar of such caliber in his kingdom, the king gave Sri Harsa a royal reception. Soon thereafter, he became very popular in Kashmir. Although previously denied the king’s audience, when recognized as a man of great learning, Sri Harsa was given royal honor. As told in Srimad-Bhagavatam, Sukadeva Gosvami was also not appreciated by ordinary people, yet in the. assembly of learned sages he was offered great respect.

One who has acquired spiritual knowledge is most fortunate as he can contact Lord Kṛṣṇa, the greatest person. Lord Kṛṣṇa says that anyone who understands His transcendental birth and activities attains Him (Bg. [4.9](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/4.9)). He also accepts a man of transcendental knowledge as His very self, jnani tv atmaiva me matam.

An uneducated man, on the other hand, even if given the opportunity to meet a king, will not be able to take full advantage. Sometimes an ignorant devotee approaches Lord Kṛṣṇa for fulfilling some material desire. Although Kṛṣṇa can mitigate all suffering, the devotee foolishly asks for something that will prolong his stay in the material world. This is like going to a great king to ask for a handful of ashes. An intelligent devotee who gains darsana of Lord Kṛṣṇa only asks for continuous engagement in His devotional service, knowing that will completely satisfy his heart and nothing else.

On their own strength, pebbles and straw are incapable of reaching the ocean, but can do so if carried by a river. The word samudram (ocean) literally means that which is full of gems. Thus by the mercy of a river even worthless pebbles can contact valuable gems. Similarly, the insignificant living entity is incapable of reaching the ocean of Kṛṣṇa unless he is carried by the river of spiritual knowledge, which flows through the parampara. The ability to acquire knowledge is the unique quality of human birth. If a person does not utilize this opportunity, his life is futile.

Materialistic persons addicted to sense enjoyment may ask, “How can vidya give us happiness?” This is explained in the next verse.

### Text 6

***vidyā dadāti vinayaṁ vinayād yāti pātratām***  
***pātratvād dhanam āpnoti dhanād dharmaṁ tataḥ sukham***

**Перевод:**

**Learning makes one humble and that makes one qualified. Being qualified, one attains wealth to perform religious acts such as charity and sacrifice, which lead to happiness.**

**Комментарий:**

Humility is the fruit of a good education. We cannot get knowledge from a bona fide guru without humbly submitting ourselves, making genuine inquiries, and offering submissive service. Even Lord Kṛṣṇa studied under a guru and performed menial service. Questions should not be presented in a challenging spirit, however, or with the intention of testing the caliber of the teacher. There should be intrinsic faith. In Bhagavad-gita (4. 34) Lord Kṛṣṇa recommends three things: pranipatena, paying obeisances; pariprasnena, asking questions; and sevaya, performing service. Thus by surrendering his body, words, and mind respectively, the disciple or student achieves complete humility.

Sometimes disciples make a show of surrender but inwardly maintain pride. They harbor a subtle defiance which, unless dealt with, deprives them of the actual fruit of knowledge. Sometimes they envy the spiritual master, who is the source of their knowledge and whom they have to serve selflessly. Unless one serves the spiritual master with the proper attitude, he will be prone to misuse the knowledge he has received; or the true meaning of the knowledge will not appear in his heart.

Those who neglect to render submissive service to their guru or who study without proper guidance become proud and misuse their learning to argue with and insult others, vidya vivadaya. This attitude is seen in modern students who not only lack respect for their teachers, but sometimes even threaten them. It is said that learning in the breast of a bad man is like a sword in the hand of a madman. Sri Kṛṣṇa says that a proud man does not reap the real results of vidya (Bhag. [9.4.70](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-9/4.70)):

***tapo vidyā ca viprāṇāṁ niḥśreyasa-kare ubhe***  
***te eva durvinītasya kalpete kartur anyathā***

**For a gentle scholar, austerity and learning are certainly auspicious, but when acquired by a person who is not gentle, such austerity and learning are most dangerous.**

One may argue: “People without humility also earn money, so why should one acquire learning?” Without proper education, the proud misuse their wealth, and even when they perform religious duties, they do it for an improper motive, for name and fame instead of selfpurification.

A person decorated with the quality of humility uses his hard-earned wealth in devotional service, knowing well that it does not belong to him. One who uses wealth for sinful activities ultimately gains only misery, whereas one who properly engages his wealth in religious activities, specifically devotional service, gains happiness. According to the law of karma, sins fructify as misery.

One may also argue that one can be happy without engaging his money in religious duties. That is not happiness. It is called maya- sukhaya, “the illusion of happiness”, because such happiness is temporary. The foreknowledge that our pleasure is temporary ruins our ability to enjoy it. We condition ourselves to forget this reality, but mere forgetfulness does not change the truth. Lord Kṛṣṇa says therefore, “That happiness which is derived from contact of the senses with their objects and which appears like nectar at first but poison at the end is said to be in the nature of passion.”(Bg. [18.38](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/18.38)) Such temporary pleasures “have a beginning and an end, and so the wise man does not delight in them.” (Bg. [5.22](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/5.22)).

Of all knowledge, scriptural knowledge is best for it leads to lasting happiness. Therefore one should acquire it by all means.

### Text 7

***vidyā śastrasya śāstrasya dve vidye pratipattaye***  
***ādyā hāsyāya vṛddhatve dvitīyādriyate sadā***

**Перевод:**

**Among all types of knowledge, martial arts (sastra-vidya) and scriptural knowledge (sastra-vidya) are famous for conferring honor. Of these two, the former causes ridicule in old age, but the latter is ever-respected.**

**Комментарий:**

It is said that the pen is mightier than sword. To control society by force is unhealthy as it results in excessive repression, which inevitably leads to revolution. Gentle persuasion, appealing to reason, encourages the masses to follow willingly. A young, agile fighter is respected, but will be ridiculed in old age if he demands honor befitting a great hero. Yet one who is learned in scripture is ever-respected. As he matures in experience and realization, his reputation is enhanced, and even after his death his teachings are followed. Thus persuasion through knowledge and reason is superior to force or martial knowledge. This was especially demonstrated by Lord Caitanya, who, unlike other incarnations, did not kill demons, but killed their demoniac mentality by teaching Srimad- Bhagavatam (prema-bhakti) and the chanting of Hare Kṛṣṇa.

Hundreds of mighty warriors can be controlled by one scholar. When Bhisma was lying on the bed of arrows awaiting death, the Pandavas approached to hear about scriptural knowledge, not about martial arts. Though Bhisma was a great warrior, it was his scriptural realizations that attracted eminent sages from all over the universe to his deathbed to hear him. Being incapacitated, he could not teach military science, but spoke for many days on sastra-vidya, while everyone listened intently.

### Text 8

***yan nave bhājane lagnaḥ saṁskāro nānyathā bhavet***  
***kathācchalena bālānāṁ nītis tad iha kathyate***

**Перевод:**

**As designs carved on a clay pot can never be changed after firing, so impressions created in the minds of young students remain for life. On the pretext of storytelling, therefore, I will teach the young niti.**

**Комментарий:**

A potter easily creates designs in wet clay, which become permanent after firing. Similarly, habits and preferences learned during childhood become an intrinsic part of one’s nature, and are increasingly difficult to change as one matures. Psychologists, various theories or methods notwithstanding, agree that our childhood influences shape and pervade our adult life. Many mental health professionals first examine one’s childhood to determine the source of adult traumas and neurosis. Therefore, education must especially aim to train the impressionable youth.

In Hitopadesa, the author will weave valuable moral instruction into fascinating stories involving animal characters, which are especially attractive to children. Although the child may not understand the moral of the story, he will enjoy the story, remember it in adulthood, and be able to apply its wisdom. This same technique is used in the Puranas and Itihasas to convey spiritual knowledge. Narayana Pandita wrote for children, but as we are all like children in subjects we have not yet’ learned, at any age we can benefit from studying Hitopadesa.

In this verse, the importance of early *Samskaras* is stressed. Samskara means purification. Here it indicates designs or impressions. Just as designs on a clay pot are made before firing, so in Vedic culture three *Samskaras,* or Vedic rites, are performed for the child before birth. These remove impurities in the semen and ovum of the parents and create good impressions on the mind of the unborn child, which he retains after birth.

Lord Kapiladeva instructs in the Srimad-Bhagavatam that the child in the womb becomes conscious from the seventh month of his conception and is influenced by his mother’s experiences. As described in the Vedic literature, while in the womb, Abhimanyu learned martial arts by hearing Arjuna speak to Subhadra, and Prahlada learned transcendental knowledge while hearing Narada Muni instruct his mother. Modern studies prove that the child learns in the womb and psychologists agree that the mother’s mentality affects the unborn child.

During the time of the British Empire, the King of Punjab, Maharaja Ranjit Singh, was dedicated to protecting cows and *brahmanas.* Once a businessman in his kingdom built a water trough for cows outside his mansion. One day a cow came to drink, and seeing some grains stored behind an iron grate, stuck her head through it to reach the grains and her horns got stuck in the grate. Seeing the cow’s dilemma, many Hindus tried to free her. One man suggested the wall holding the grate be demolished, but another said that would be too costly and suggested that the horns be cut instead. The crowd scoffed at this idea. Instead they broke the wall and freed the cow unharmed.

A spy reported the incident to the king. On hearing that a Hindu had suggested cutting a cow’s horns, he was very concerned and summoned the man. After confirming the story, the king asked, “How dare you suggest this ill treatment of a cow?”

Realizing his mistake, the man bowed his head and asked for forgiveness, but this did not pacify the king, “You are not a real Hindu. There must be some defect regarding your birth.” The king called the man’s mother and questioned her about her son’s birth. She testified that the man was her legitimate son, but the king was not convinced. He said, “Your son is not born of a Hindu.”

Terrified, the mother said, “I am a chaste lady and have never contacted anyone other than my husband — not even in dreams. But after my son was conceived, the first person I saw in the morning was a butcher sitting nearby on the roof of his house, and the thought of his abominable lifestyle entered my mind. This must be the reason the sinful thought entered my son’s mind.

This explanation satisfied the king, for he knew the importance of *Samskaras.* He passed a law requiring every Hindu woman to wear a ring with a small mirror in it, and every morning upon awakening they were to gaze at the reflection of their own face before seeing anyone else. The custom continues in some parts of Punjab although its purpose is forgotten. This story illustrates the value of *Samskaras,* which have been largely forgotten in today’s society. Next the author explains the source material for his book and names the chapters in it.

### Text 9

***mitralābhaḥ suhṛd bhedo vigrahaḥ sandhir eva ca***  
***pañcatantrāt tath-nyasmād granthādḥākṛṣya likhyate***

**Перевод:**

**This book includes stories from Pañcatantra and other such books. There are four sections named Mitralābha, Suhṛdabheda, Vigraha and Sandhi.**

**Комментарий:**

Many of the verses of Hitopadesa can be traced to older works, the Mahabharata, Puranas and the Pancatantra, by Visnu Sarma. Because it is based on sastra, one should study this book with faith. While Pancatantra is more expanded and complex, Hitopadesa, especially written for young students, is simpler and shorter. The first chapter, Mitralabha, explains how to make friends; the second, Suhrdbheda, how to divide them; the third, Vigraha, how to make war; and finally, Sandhi, how to make a truce.

*Atha katha-mukham: asti bhāgīrathī-tīre-pāṭaliputra-nāmadheyaṁ nagaram. Tatra sarva-svāmi-guṇopetaḥ sudarśano nāma narapartirāsīt. Sa bhūpatir ekadā kenāpi pāṭhyamānaṁ śloka-dvayaṁ śuśrāva —*

**Introduction: On the bank of the Ganges, in the city Pataliputra (presently known as Patna), lived Sudarsana, a king endowed with all royal qualities. Once the king heard two verses:**

### Text 10

***aneka-saṁśayocchedi parokṣārthasyaādarśakaṁ***  
***sarvasya locanaṁ śāstraṁ yasya nāsty andha eva saḥ***

**Перевод:**

**Sastra is the illuminating eye for everyone as it removes various doubts and reveals what lies beyond our vision. One who lacks knowledge of sastra, therefore, is surely blind.**

**Комментарий:**

Here the essence of Hitopadesa is revealed. We are born ignorant and gain the greater part of our knowledge by hearing. Direct perception by our five knowledge acquiring senses only gives knowledge about current events. Knowledge of events beyond our sense perception is acquired through sastra. Moreover, sastra explains objects we perceive yet do not understand. Sastra is our real eye, therefore, and is considered a flawless authority.

Originally, the Lord, who is free from all defects, imparted the Vedas to Brahma, the first person in the universe. Later, the sages compiled other scriptures called *Smrtis,* based on Vedic knowledge. The Vedas and smrtis combined make up the body of knowledge called sastra.

Knowledge based only on observation and inference is unreliable, as every conditioned soul suffers from four defects: imperfect senses, being subject to illusion, tendency to make mistakes, and the propensity to cheat. While burdened with these handicaps, a person who has not studied sastra can never attain knowledge of the Absolute Truth.

For a person lacking sastric knowledge, trying to progress is like a blind man attempting to walk. He may not harm others if he stays alone, but if he attempts to lead people, they will surely meet with disastrous results. If a doctor, teacher, or lawyer is not thoroughly learned in his field, his patients, students, or clients will be doomed. Similarly, sastric knowledge is an essential qualification of a leader.

### Text 11

***yauvanaṁ dhana-sampattiḥ prabhutvam avivekitā***  
***ekaikam apy anarthāya kim u yatra catuṣṭayam***

**Перевод:**

**Youth, wealth, power, and lack of discrimination are individually sufficient in themselves to cause misery, and especially disastrous if combined together.**

**Комментарий:**

This world’s most desirable possessions become dangerous for one devoid of scriptural knowledge. In the intoxication of youth, our discrimination is often covered as we try to enjoy the senses with abandon. And indiscriminate youth combined with wealth and power, inevitably leads to pride. On the spiritual path, pride is often the cause of falldown. As it is said, “Pride precedes a fall; wealth is the root of all evil; and power corrupts, while absolute power corrupts absolutely.” A person possessing wealth and power, combined with humility is rare.

One who is triply intoxicated by youth, wealth and high position is far more dangerous than one who is weak, poor, and lowly. Furthermore, if he lacks discrimination, he is bound to be tyrannical, leading to his ultimate misfortune. Srimad-Bhagavatam describes King Vena, who had youth, wealth, and power, but used them only to inflict misery on others. Due to his pride he was later destroyed. The real dangers of youth, wealth, and power lie in their tendency to cover one’s discrimination. Otherwise, they are certainly coveted assets. The only antidote to indiscrimination is knowledge.

The next thirteen verses stress the importance of educating one’s children, especially to insure noble leadership in society.

*ity ākarṇyātmanaḥ putrāṇām anadhigata-śāstrāṇāṁ nityam unmārga-gāmināṁ śāstrānanuṣṭhānenodvigna-manāḥ sa rājā cintayāmāsa —*

**Hearing the above two verses, the king was concerned about his sons, who were uneducated, always misbehaved, and ignored sastra. Thus he thought to himself:**

**Commentary**

The intelligent king understood how these two verses applied to his delinquent sons and was concerned about them. He demonstrated the qualities of a good father or guru who takes a personal interest in the upliftment of his sons and/or disciples. In this age it is not uncommon to see gurus initiate disciples far and wide, without attending to their spiritual growth. They are like children cast into the water and left to fend for themselves. The actual role of guru is to train his disciples. By extending himself on behalf of others, the guru grows more expansive as a person, and the disciple in turn learns from the role model of the guru how to be guru himself. The sastra enjoins, therefore, that one should not have too many disciples. The next twenty-two verses explain the king’s concern about his sons.

### Text 12

***ko 'rthaḥ putreṇa jātena yo na vidvān na dhārmikaḥ***  
***kāṇena cakṣuṣā kiṁ vā cakṣuḥ pīḍaiva kevalam***

**Перевод:**

**What is the use of a son who is neither scholarly nor religious? He is like a painful, useless, blind eye.**

**Комментарий:**

A son is like one’s own self for it is said that a man is born from the womb of his wife as his son. Thus the wife is called jaya, or from whom one takes birth, jayayas tad hi jayatvam yad asyam jayate punah (Manu Smrti 9. 8). The attached father naturally feels satisfaction when his son attains the goals he himself did not achieve. A son who is uneducated, however, or of bad character, only distresses his father, like an ugly, useless blind eye.

An irreligious son is considered useless because he neglects the Vedic practices such as pinda-dana which gives liberation to one’s forefathers. Vedic culture teaches that every man carries five debts from birth, one of which is pitr-rna, or one’s debt to his forefathers. One who marries and has a religious son gains relief from this debt and delivers his forefathers from distress. Arjuna was concerned that as a result of his fighting, many women would become widows, thus making them vulnerable to illicit sex, creating unqualified sons, which would lead to the ruination of society (Bg. 1. 41):

*saṅkaro narakāyaiva*  
*kula-ghnānāṁ kulasya ca*  
*patanti pitaro hy eṣāṁ*  
*lupta-piṇḍodaka-kriyāḥ*

**An increase of unwanted population certainly causes hellish life both for the family and for those who destroy the family tradition. The ancestors of such corrupt families fall down, because the performances of offering them food and water are entirely stopped.**

In the varnasrama system good family life is stressed. The purpose of marriage is to produce a son to continue the family tradition and perform oblations to the forefathers, putrarthe kriyate bharya putram pinda- prayojanam.

In the Mahabharata is the story of Jaratkaru, who engaged in severe austerities, and was uninterested in marriage. One day, while wandering in the forest he saw some weak, emaciated people tied and hanging upside down over a deep pit. A rat had nibbled through the rope holding them until only one strand remained. “Who are you who are about to fall into the pit?” Jaratkaru asked. “What can I do to help?”

“We are forefathers who are suffering because our dynasty has been lost. Our only descendant is Jaratkaru, who has decided not to marry. Thus we have fallen from the heavenly sphere and are hanging from this rope which represents our dynasty. The rat represents Time and the one remaining straw represents Jaratkaru. The day Jaratkaru dies, our dynasty will be lost! ”

Hearing this, Jaratkaru introduced himself, assured them of his help, and later married the sister of Vasuki to produce a son.

This story does not oppose renunciation, but illustrates the importance of having a religious son to continue the family dynasty. The path of renunciation is not possible for most people, so they are advised to marry and produce qualified children to uplift society.

Not realizing the benefit of spiritual life, materialistic parents sometimes become alarmed if their son becomes interested in religious studies. Being ignorant of life’s ultimate purpose, they remain attached to the fleeting happiness obtained from sense pleasure in the atmosphere of family life. The varnasrama principle, however, while allowing for family life, helps uplift one to eternal happiness.

### Text 13

***ajāta-mṛta-mūrkhāṇāṁ varam ādyau na cāntimaḥ***  
***sakṛd duḥkha-karāv ādyāv antimas tu pade pade***

**Перевод:**

**Unborn or dead sons are preferable to foolish ones, for the first two give misery once, but the fool at every step.**

**Комментарий:**

If a couple does not conceive a son, or if the son dies prematurely they suffer temporarily, but a foolish son causes his parents lifelong misery.

One may question why misery results from not conceiving a son. The importance of a son in Vedic culture has been already described. Not having a son is a great misfortune, because there is no one responsible to carry on the family traditions and perform the necessary rituals for the departed forefathers. Since sense pleasure is not the central purpose of Vedic culture, husband and wife united only for the purpose of procreation (putrarthe kriyate bharya).

At present, however, people generally unite only for sensual pleasure, and children are often considered a regrettable accident. Abortion being accepted as standard practice, people conveniently forget that life begins at conception, by using such euphemisms like “tissue” when referring to the fetus. Although subconsciously they know that they are killing the baby in the womb, they prefer to say that they are “terminating the pregnancy,” or “removing the tissue” so they may feel free of the guilt for murder, thus deluding themselves psychologically. Little do they know that they cannot escape the punishment for murder given by the laws of karma, and that in their next lives they will have to suffer the misery of repeatedly being aborted.

Childless parents, on the other hand, may not agree with this verse due to their strong desire for a son, good or bad. Yet there are several instances where a son has given so much distress to his parents that they would prefer never to have given birth to him.

In the Bhagavata Mahatmya, which is part of Padma Purana, is the story of a brahmana named Atmadeva, who devoutly followed the Vedic principles. His wife Dhundhuli was cruel, talkative, lazy, miserly, and quarrelsome. Because they had no children, Atmadeva was unhappy. Once, feeling frustrated, he went to the forest to commit suicide. On the way he met a Sannyasi to whom he revealed his mind. Although the *Sannyasi* advised him on the futility of having an ill-behaved son, it had no effect on Atmadeva.

Seeing Atmadeva’s determination, the *Sannyasi* gave a sacred fruit for his wife to eat. His wife, not wanting to undergo the pains of pregnancy and delivery, fed the fruit to their cow and told her husband that she had eaten it. Feigning pregnancy, Dhundhuli made an agreement with her pregnant sister to get her child. When her sister delivered a son, Dhundhuli tricked Atmadeva into believing that it was her baby. Overwhelmed with ecstasy, Atmadeva named the boy Dhundhukari. Simultaneously, the cow which had eaten the sacred fruit also delivered a human child who had ears like a cow. The *brahmana* was overjoyed and named this boy Gokarna.

Gokarna grew into a gentle, obedient, and learned boy while Dhundhukari grew into an irreligious, proud, quarrelsome rogue, fond of drinking, prostitution and gambling. Being a cruel, irate thief and liar, he misused all his father’s wealth. When his parents would no longer supply money for his sinful activities, he would beat them, and when disciplined, the boy’s anger increased. Unable to tolerate the suffering caused by his son, Atmadeva went to the forest out of frustration, and Dhundhuli committed suicide by jumping into a well. As this verse rightly advises, therefore, it is better to have no son than a foolish son who brings misery at every step.

### Text 14

*varaṁ garbha-srāvo varam api ca naivābhigamanaṁ*  
*varaṁ jātaḥ preto varam api ca kanyāvajanitā*  
*varaṁ bandhyā bhāryā varam api ca garbheṣu vasatir*  
*na vāvidvān rūpa-draviṇa-guṇa-yukto 'pi tanayaḥ*

**Перевод:**

**Moreover, better a miscarriage, or no union at all; better the child is stillborn, or better a girl is born; better the wife is barren, or the child remains in the womb. All these are preferable to a beautiful, wealthy son who is a fool.**

**Комментарий:**

Beauty and wealth without good character are like ornaments on a dead body. They may also inflate the false ego. Ignorant people, deeply attached to the material body, considering it their very self, give utmost importance to wealth and beauty, though these are lost at death. Real beauty is found in a person’s knowledge and good character. Bhatrhari says (Niti-satakam 62):

*śrotraṁ śrutenaiva na kuṇḍalena*  
*dānena pāṇirna tu kaṅkaṇena*  
*vibhāti kāyaḥ karuṇāparāṇāṁ*  
*paropakārairna tu candanena*

**The bodies of the compassionate are beautified, not by sandalwood pulp (or by cosmetics), but by acts performed for others’ benefit; their ears are decorated, not with earrings, but by hearing scriptures; and their hands are ornamented, not by golden bracelets, but by giving in charity.**

Those attracted to a handsome man who has demoniac character, are like flies attracted to garbage.

### Text 15

*sa jāto yena jātena yāti vaṁśaḥ samunnatim*  
*parivartini saṁsāre mṛtaḥ ko vā na jāyate*

**Перевод:**

**One’s birth is successful if he brings honor to his dynasty; otherwise one is born and dies uselessly in this changing world.**

**Комментарий:**

A life misspent has no real value. Better a short life properly lived than a long life wasted in the vain pursuit of sense gratification, ending in death. Actually, a long self-centered life is worse than the life of a tree, because at least trees serve humanity by purifying the atmosphere, supplying wood, fruits, flowers, honey, and medicines. A life of self- sacrifice brings fame both in this life and the next: yasya kirtih sa jivati, one lives on even after death through his good name.

Real fame, however, results from the performance of devotional service. The saintly devotee of the Lord conquers his senses and brings them under control, using them in devotional service to the Lord and service to others. His achievement is greater than the man who conquers the world, but whose senses are uncontrolled. Therefore, while many world conquerors live on in the pages of history, great saintly persons live on in the hearts of men. In the Caitanya-caritamrta, Ramananda Raya says, “That person who is reputed to be a devotee of Lord Kṛṣṇa enjoys the utmost fame and glory.” Of course, the devotee is not motivated by the pursuit of fame and glory.

A devotee of the Lord is an asset to his family for he delivers his ancestors by his devotional service. After killing Hiranyakasipu, Lord Nrsimhadeva offered Prahlada a boon. Prahlada requested the deliverance of his father, and the Lord assured him that twenty-one generations of his family members had been delivered because of his pure devotional service.

### Text 16

***guṇi-gaṇa-gaṇanā'rambhe***  
***na patati kaṭhinī sa-sambhramād yasya***  
***tenāmbā yadi sutinī***  
***vada bandhyā kīdṛśī bhavati***

**Перевод:**

**When the pious are counted and history doesn’t respectfully record the son’s name first, yet she who has borne him is called mother, then who is considered barren?**

**Комментарий:**

The hard labor of childbirth and subsequent toil of motherhood is worthwhile only if the son becomes pious and learned. It is better to be barren than to have a son who neither brings glory to the family nor cares for the parents. Why perform hard labor for no good result? Parents should, therefore, take great care to build their sons’ character by educating them properly.

*Api ca —* And also....

### Text 17

***dāne tapasi śaurye ca yasya na prathitaṁ manaḥ***  
***vidyāyām artha-lābhe ca mātur uccāra eva saḥ***

**Перевод:**

**Further, one who is not enthusiastic to offer charity, perform austerities, engage in valorous deeds, or acquire knowledge or wealth, is indeed the excrement of his mother.**

**Комментарий:**

By nature’s arrangement the body naturally rejects excrement. No one is attached to their stool, but parents consider even a useless son as their very self, although in reality, he is no better than the stool of the mother. Even stool is useful as fertilizer, but a son who is unenthusiastic about the above activities, being deeply immersed in the mode of ignorance, is simply a useless burden.

*Aparan ca* — Furthermore,

### Text 18

***varam eko guṇī putro na ca mūrkha-śatair api***  
***ekaś candramas tamo hanti na ca tārā-gaṇair api***

**Перевод:**

**Moreover, it is better to have one learned son than hundreds of foolish ones. One moon is able to dispel the darkness, not millions of stars.**

**Комментарий:**

Quality is preferable to quantity. Hundreds of plastic roses cannot compare to a single real one, nor can millions of names of God chanted offensively be compared to one name chanted without offense. A full moon not only dispels darkness, but it eclipses the twinkling stars.

A moonlike pure devotee can deliver unlimited numbers of fools. Srila Prabhupada wanted to create a society of brahmanas, men of ideal character, who could lead the world in spiritual life. He said, “If I create one moon, that is sufficient. I don’t want many stars. What is the use of having numbers of fools and rascals. If one man understands rightly, he can deliver the whole world.”

To attract followers one should not compromise the philosophy. Purity is the force, purity of the philosophy and purity of character. Srila Prabhupada said that we should not be duplicitous. “If we show one thing externally and something else internally, it will not be successful. We have to present an ideal institution — not that we make compromise with everybody. We don’t want stars. What is the use of millions of stars? Get one moon.”

### Text 19

*puṇya-tīrthe kṛtaṁ yena tapaḥ kvāpy atiduṣkaram*  
*tasya putro bhaved vaśyaḥ samṛddho dhārmikaḥ sudhīḥ*

**Перевод:**

**One who has performed severe austerities in a holy place will conceive a son who is obedient, wealthy, religious, and intelligent.**

**Комментарий:**

Nothing happens by accident. Out of ignorance of the invisible and intricate laws of material nature, people think that a pious son is born by chance. Although speculative philosophers have debated over fate and free will for centuries, some favoring one or the other, both parties are wrong. Fate and free will exist side by side. Our actions from our previous life determine the circumstances we encounter in this life. We decide what path we follow in response to the fated circumstances. Therefore it is said that between stimulus (fate) and response is a decision. That decision is because of our free will. Fate and endeavor combined give the result of any attempt. A pious son is born owing to past pious activities (fate) and by conception according to the Vedic rule called garbhadhana samskara.

Vedic scriptures offer many prescriptions for conceiving a good child. One should perform garbhadhana samskara on a proper day. Purnima, astami, and ekadasi are forbidden. The husband should not approach his wife during the first four days of her monthly period, or during daytime, or brahma-muhurtta. A child conceived on even-numbered days, after the menstruation has stopped, will be a boy; on odd days, a girl.

Other recommendations are to take special ayurvedic herbs, hear stories of great devotees during menstruation, and not procreate when the mind is disturbed.

Srimad-Bhagavatam narrates how two great demons were born to Kasyapa and Diti because they were conceived at an improper time and without the garbhadhana samskara. Kamsa is another example of a demon who appeared in a good family. Although thought to be a son of King Ugrasena, he was actually conceived by the trick of a gandharva named Dramila.

According to the Garga-Samhita, while Ugrasena’s queen was visiting her father, she went to the forest with her friends. In that remote place she suddenly heard someone singing who sounded just like her husband. She followed the voice and was overjoyed to find her young husband sitting under a tree singing songs expressing his great longing for her. The king confessed that he was unable to tolerate her absence, and not wanting to appear henpecked, avoided visiting her father’s house. Evading embarrassment, he pursued her in that solitary forest. After their union the Queen understood the man to be an imposter, and to her great dismay learned he was a gandharva named Dramila. Being attracted by the Queen’s beauty, Dramila seduced her and as a result of their union Kamsa was born.

By neglecting the Vedic Samskaras, today’s society has produced disobedient and irreligious children, who delight in creating turmoil and distress in the family.

### Text 20

*arthāgamo nityam arogitā ca priyā ca bhāryā priya-vādinī ca*  
*vaśyaś ca putro 'rtha-karī ca vidyā ṣaḍ jīva-lokasya sukhāni rājan*

**Перевод:**

**O King, in the material world, pleasure comes from six things: wealth, good health, a beautiful wife, a wife who speaks sweetly, an obedient son, and knowledge.**

**Комментарий:**

Money is sweeter than honey, as it can buy happiness in this world and satisfy one’s family and friends. The socialist philosopher Karl Marx has expressed the power of money:

That which exists for me through the medium of money, that which I can pay for (i. e. which money can buy), that I am, the possessor of the money. My own power is as great as the power of money. The properties of money are my own (the possessor’s) properties and faculties. What I am and can do is, therefore, not at all determined by my individuality. I am ugly, but I can buy the most beautiful woman for myself. Consequently, I am not ugly, for the effect of ugliness, its power to repel, is annulled by money. As an individual I am lame, but money provides me with twenty-four legs. Therefore, I am not lame. I am a detestable, dishonorable, unscrupulous and stupid man, but money is honored and so also is its possessor. Money is the highest good, and so its possessor is good. Besides, money saves me the trouble of being dishonest; therefore, I am presumed honest. I am stupid, but since money is the real mind of all things, how should its possessor be stupid? Moreover, he can buy talented people for himself, and is it not that he who has power over the talented is more talented than they? I who can have, through power of money, everything for which the human heart longs, do I not possess all human abilities?

Such a man appears to be a great man, not because of his natural qualities, good character, etc., but because he is a powerful man. The rich can buy or intimidate others. Thus they “possess” fame and admiration. Thus money is a source of pleasure to both the pious and impious. It can make the impious appear pious by levelling all his bad qualities, as Marx observed.

Of course, without good health, money is futile. And to enjoy the topmost pleasure of association with the opposite sex, one needs a beautiful woman, who money can buy, but she must also speak sweetly. Health, wealth, and a beautiful but quarrelsome wife give one misery. Then again, one may have wealth, good health, and a beautiful loving wife, but if the sons are disobedient, one will still be miserable. To find one who is rich, healthy, with a loving wife and an obedient son is very rare, and impossible to achieve without strictly following scriptures and having pious merit from previous lives.

However, even if one has good health, a nice wife, and an obedient son, he may have difficulty at his place of work. An educated man, skilled in acquiring wealth, is not subservient to anyone. When one has all the above and does not depend on others for money, he realizes happiness.

Even so, this happiness is relative, because in this world disaster strikes without warning and our happiness can be lost in a moment. Therefore even in the midst of the utmost pleasure there is always a worm of anxiety. Even Indra, the lord of heaven, is not free from misery.

### Text 21

***ko dhanyo bahubhiḥ putraiḥ kuśūlāpūraṇāḍhakaiḥ***  
***varam ekaḥ kulālambī yatra viśrūyate pitā***

**Перевод:**

**A house full of many useless sons is like a pot filled with husk. Better to have one intelligent son who can support the family and bring his father fame.**

**Комментарий:**

Parents rejoice at the birth of each son, but they do not know the destiny of those sons. If they all turn out to be vagabonds, it is a great disappointment. In hindsight the parents may prefer that they had no sons at all. And certainly one son that brings fame to the family is preferred to a half dozen mediocrities.

### Text 22

*ṛṇa-kartā pitā śatrur mātā ca vyabhicāriṇī*  
*bhāryā rūpavatī śatruḥ putraḥ śatrur apaṇḍitaḥ*

**Перевод:**

**These are natural enemies: a father in debt, an unchaste mother, a beautiful wife, and an illiterate son.**

**Комментарий:**

Whoever causes us distress is an enemy. If the father borrows money and suddenly dies, the son is obliged to repay the debt. An unchaste mother is an enemy in three ways. To her husband she is an embarrassment. She will conceive illegitimate children, who will be rebellious and disturb her legitimate children. She is also an enemy to her illegitimate son, who will feel inferior in the presence of gentlemen.

If one’s wife is beautiful, other men will try to entice her and if she is not devoted to her husband, she will either abandon him or accept a paramour. Sometimes an unchaste wife even arranges for the murder of her husband. Of course, if a wife is beautiful and chaste, she may not cause trouble, but still, she is a source of worry because others will be attracted to her. Sita Devi was very chaste, but because Ravana became enamored and abducted her, Rama had to undergo much trouble to free her.

If a son is not learned, he makes the father miserable, especially if a father is learned and the son is a dunce. Feeling ashamed, he dares not bring his son into the presence of learned men.

### Text 23

*yasya kasya prasūto 'pi guṇavān pūjyate naraḥ*  
*dhanur vaṁśa-viśuddho 'pi nirguṇaḥ kiṁ kariṣyati*

**Перевод:**

**A man endowed with good qualities is respected regardless of his high or low birth; indeed, even if made of the finest bamboo, what good is a bow without a string?**

**Комментарий:**

Here, the author makes a pun on vamsa and nirguna. Vamsa means either “dynasty” or “bamboo”. Dhanu-vamsa-visuddho’pi means a bow made from good vamsa. Nirguna means “without good qualities,” and also “without a string.” As a bow made of good bamboo (vamsa) lacking a string (nirguna) cannot perform its function, a son even if born in a good family (vamsa) is useless if lacking good qualities (nirguna).

For one of bad character, a high birth is like a decoration on a dead body. A person of impeccable character, though born in a low family, is far superior. Narada Muni and Vidura were sons of maidservants, and Vyasa was born of a fisherwoman, yet they are worshipped, owing to their excellent character. On the other hand, Hiranyakasipu, Ravana, and Kamsa, although born in good dynasties, are condemned as their character was despicable. Rather than his birth, therefore, a man should be appreciated for his good character.

### Text 24

*hā hā putraka nādhītaṁ gatāsv etāsu rātriṣu*  
*tena tvaṁ viduṣāṁ madhye paṅke gaur iva sīdasi*

**Перевод:**

**Alas, my son, all these nights you have not studied. Thus in an assembly of scholars, just as a cow sinks in marshy land, you will have to lower your head in shame.**

**Комментарий:**

Time and tide wait for no man. Successful people have realized that time is the most precious possession, as it can never be regained even in exchange for money. Therefore, they are always alert not to waste even a single moment. Maharaja Pariksit, for example, upon learning that he had only seven days to live, continuously engaged in hearing the glories of Lord Kṛṣṇa without wasting time even for eating or sleeping. One’s time in youth is best spent in acquiring knowledge. Otherwise, when old age attacks, one will lament his wasted life; but then it is too late.

*tat katham idānīm ete mama putrā guṇavantaḥ kriyantām? Yataḥ*

**Then how can my sons become glorious? Because —**

### Text 25

***āhāra-nidrā-bhaya-maithunāni sāmānyam etat paśubhir narāṇām***  
***jñānaṁ narāṇām adhiko viśeṣo jñānena hīnāḥ paśubhiḥ samānāḥ***

**Перевод:**

**Eating, sleeping, fearing, and mating are common among animals and humans; but religion is unique among humans. Without it they are no better than animals.**

**Комментарий:**

Because human beings have the unique ability to speak and acquire knowledge, they are considered superior to all other species. They can strive for superior goals like spiritual knowledge. Animals cannot inquire into the meaning of life. Without cultivating spiritual knowledge, people remain on the animal platform. Animals, being completely controlled by their senses, are governed by the above four natural tendencies. A human being is expected to distinguish himself from animal life by rising above these tendencies to achieve self-realization and knowledge of the Supreme Lord. The Lord appears again and again to educate human beings. In this regard, Sri Sukadeva Gosvami states (Bhag. 5. 19. 5):

*martyāvatāras tv iha martya-śikṣaṇaṁ*  
*rakṣo-vadhāyaiva na kevalaṁ vibhoḥ*

**O Lord, You incarnate in this material world not just to kill demons, but to educate mortal beings.**

Misusing the opportunity of human life, people live just to satisfy their animalistic desires. This only binds them more tightly to the wheel of repeated birth and death. Animals deplete the results of their past karma without accruing new karma, and thus do not degrade themselves further. Rather, the more their karma is expended, the more they progress. Human beings, however, who live only for the sake of eating, sleeping, defending, and mating increase their bad karma. Such human beings are worse than animals.

Thanks to modern technology, men may now accumulate enough karma in this lifetime to provide themselves with millions of animal births in the future. Misusing their intelligence, they have become expert in exploiting others. In killing animals to satisfy their tongues or to decorate their bodies with skins, or using them for laboratory experiments or in making cosmetics, they inflict untold suffering for which they must endure the reactions. Such persons are the worst enemies of all beings, including themselves.

In contrast, animals do not make extensive plans to exploit and enslave others, hoard excessively, or encroach upon another’s rights, but are satisfied when their basic needs are met. Materialistic people, however, are never satisfied. Although here it is said that they are equal to animals, actually they are worse than animals.

### Text 26

*dharmārtha-kāma-mokṣāṇāṁ yasyaiko 'pi na vidyate*  
*ajāgala-stanasyeva tasya janma nirarthakam*

**Перевод:**

**If one does not attain any of the goals of religion, economic development, regulated sense-gratification, and liberation, one’s life is useless, like the milkless nipples hanging from a goat’s neck.**

**Комментарий:**
