According to Vedic culture, the purpose of human life is to strive for the above-mentioned goals and the Supreme Lord offers four types of sastra for guidance. Moksa-sastra, which describes liberation, includes the Upanisads and Vedanta-sutra. Dharma-sastra (Manu-smrti, Hari-bhakti- vilasa) explains a person’s religious duties according to varnasrama. Artha-sastra, (Pancatantra, Hitopadesa, Kautiliya-artha-sastra) which includes niti, or ethics, explains the process of acquiring wealth by fair means; advises how a king should manage his kingdom; and offers instructions on proper conduct. Kama-sastra (Kama-sutra and Rati-manjari ) or erotic science, should not be mistaken to encourage promiscuity; it describes the process of gratifying one’s desires without transgressing religious principles. The author of Kama-sutra, Vatsyayana, writes (1. 2. 1):

*śata-āyur vai puruṣo vibhajya kālam anyonya-anubaddhaṁ parasparasya-anupaghātakaṁ trivargaṁ seveta*

**A man’s life-span of one hundred years should be divided into the various asramas (brahmacarya, grhastha, vanaprastha, and Sannyasa), and he should practice dharma, artha and kama in such a way that the three become neither obstacles to nor independent of one another.**

Vatsyayana does not prescribe unrestricted sense pleasure, nor is he opposed to it. One who excessively indulges in sense enjoyment cannot expect to live a hundred years. Therefore, Sutra 1. 2. 6 prescribes that one should remain celibate during student life. Vatsyayana includes moksa within the category of dharma as is evident from the fourth sutra in the same chapter. Later he explains the importance of trivarga (dharma, artha and kama) and concludes that artha is superior to kama, and dharma is superior to artha, eṣāṁ samavāye pūrvaḥ pūrvo garīyān (2. 14).

Amazingly enough, Vatsyayana was a lifelong brahmacari and he wrote the Kama-sutra to encourage sense control, not sex pleasure. He declares this in the concluding verses \[Kama-sutra 7. 2. 57, 58\]:

*tad etad bramacaryeṇa pareṇa ca samādhinā*  
*vihitaṁ lokayātrāyai na rāgārtho'sya saṁvidhiḥ*

**This kama-sutra has been compiled by me, while observing celibacy and trance to teach proper human conduct, not attachment to sex.**

*rakṣan dharma-artha-kāmānāṁ sthitiṁ svāṁ loka-vartinīm*  
*asya śāstrasya tattvajño bhavaty eva jitendriyaḥ*

**One who knows the essence of kama-sutra and who protects dharma, artha, and kama by his dealings in society, will certainly become jitendriya, a master of his senses.**

Therefore, one should not doubt that kama is one of the principal goals in human life. Lord Kṛṣṇa says dharmaviruddho bhutesu kamo’smi bharatarsabha, “I am kama that is not against religious principles, O Arjuna.”

An intelligent human being must pursue one or more of these four goals according to their respective sastra. Otherwise, his life is compared to the useless nipple-like appendages hanging from the goat’s neck. The pursuit of artha and kama while ignoring scripture will not lead one to the ultimate goal of life. Manu-smrti declares, pravrttir esa bhutanam nivrttis tu maha-phala: The living entities are naturally attracted to sense enjoyment, but the real goal is to become free from it.

The purpose of sanctioned sense pleasure is nivrtti, renunciation When artha and kama are pursued according to sastra, they can be counted as purusarthas, as they will ultimately detach one from the body and its by-products. Actually, there is only one purusartha — moksa. The other three enable one to realize the fourth by dovetailing one’s natural human propensities.

Lord Caitanya taught that beyond these four goals there is a fifth, which is the highest goal of human life — prema, or love of God. Prema brings peace in this life and the next, while the other goals simply cheat us out of real happiness, because in the next life we must start all over again. Everyone is searching for the perfect person to love and be loved, but, forgetting Kṛṣṇa, the source of love, we are creating so many false objects of affection. Without a loving relationship with someone, we cannot be happy. Our propensity to love, however, can only be completely satisfied when we have a loving relationship with Kṛṣṇa and His devotees. This love is gained by chanting the Lord’s names without offence under the guidance of a bona fide guru.

The followers of Lord Caitanya wrote many books to explain the philosophy of love, such as Bhakti-rasamrta-sindhu, Ujjvala Nilamani, and Sat-sandarbhas. Those who are serious to achieve the ultimate goal of life should take advantage of them.

### Text 27

***āyuḥ karma ca vittaṁ ca vidyā nidhanam eva ca***  
***pañcaitāni hi sṛjyante garbhasthasyaiva dehinaḥ***

**Перевод:**

**While still in the womb these five are decided — longevity, occupation, amount of wealth, degree of education, and cause of death.**

**Комментарий:**

This verse defends the students desiring to avoid study. They say that if they are fated to be learned, it will happen automatically. Why should they work hard for it?

The verse indicates that a man’s fate is fixed before birth. Api (also) implies that as the limbs of the body are fashioned within the womb, the above five items are also decided there. It is said, “He who is born to be hanged shall never be drowned”, indicating that fate is immutable. Some philosophers say that everything is predestined and one cannot escape his karma, nabhuktam ksiyate karma kalpa-koti-satair api / avasyam eva bhoktavyam krtam karma subhasubham (Brahma vaivarta Prakrti 37. 16). Others stress the importance of work, or prescribed duties, over fate. The author will present both views and give his conclusion.

Kinca — Moreover,

### Text 28

***avaśyaṁ bhāvino bhāvā bhavanti mahatām api***  
***nagnatvaṁ nīlakaṇṭhasya mahāhi-śayanaṁ hareḥ***

**Перевод:**

**Lord Siva is naked for want of clothes and Lord Hari sleeps on a snake for want of a bed; whatever is destined will occur even to great persons.**

**Комментарий:**

Man has the tendency to rationalize his beliefs to suit himself. Thus people who believe in destiny think that there is nothing beyond fate and that it guides everyone. Ignorant people, upon hearing that Lord Siva goes naked and that Lord Hari sleeps on the snake Ananta Sesa, think that these great persons are in the same situation as themselves, that They lack the bare necessities of life owing to fate.

Those who regard fate as supreme substantiate their case with sastra. They cite the incident of Vasistha, who had fixed a suitable time for Rama’s coronation, but later saw Him banished to the forest. They say Rama was banished owing to fate and to prove their point narrate many stories such as this.

Once Yamaraja came to visit Lord Rama in Ayodhya, and while entering the palace, he smiled to see a pigeon sitting atop the gate. The terrified pigeon asked Yamaraja why he was smiling. “Soon you will be killed,” answered the Lord of Death.

While the disturbed pigeon was wondering what to do, Garuda, the carrier of Lord Visnu, arrived. On hearing of the pigeon’s plight, he carried him to an uninhabited region in Lokaloka mountain where no one could kill him, promising to retrieve him in due course. Garuda assured the pigeon that Yamaraja’s prediction would fail.

Returning to Ayodhya, Garuda saw Yamaraja, who asked him why he appeared fatigued, and Garuda revealed his plan to save the pigeon. Yamaraja smiled and said, “That pigeon has already been eaten by a cat. When I saw him sitting on the gate I smiled, because I wondered how he would ever reach that remote place thousands of miles away where he was destined to die. I knew he would not be able get there on his own strength even in years. How wonderful it is that you carried him there! Surely one’s destiny is inevitable.” Hearing this, Garuda was awestruck by the power of fate.

Anyac ca — Also...

### Text 29

***yad abhāvi na tad bhāvi bhāvi cen na tad anyathā***  
***iti cintā-viṣa-ghno 'yam agadaḥ kiṁ na pīyate***

**Перевод:**

**Drink this antidote to mitigate the poison of life’s anxieties: That which is not destined to be, will never be, and that which is predestined, cannot be otherwise.**

**Комментарий:**

When tragedy strikes, people in India often console themselves by tossing their hands up to inevitable fate. One must, however, guard against using fate as an excuse for laziness. To avoid work, lazy men claim, “Whatever will be, will be.” They think if they are destined to be rich, wealth will come effortlessly; and if wealth is not in their stars, why work for it? This attitude shows ignorance of the co-existence of fate and free will.

Lord Kṛṣṇa says we must work without attachment for the fruits, and not be attached to inaction, ma te sango’stv akarmani (Bg. 2. 47). Even animals work for their food, so why shouldn’t human beings? In Vedic culture it is a practice to gaze at one’s hands upon arising to remember they are God’s gifts, and that man is the creator of his own fate as depicted in the lines on his palms. We should always remember that man is the architect of his own fortune.

The above verse may console those who have just suffered a calamity, but we should not conclude that fate is supreme and action is futile. The author establishes the correct understanding in the next seven verses.

*etat kāryākṣamāṇāṁ keṣāṁcid ālasya-vacanam*  
*These are the words of the lazy who are incapable of working.*

### Text 30

*yathā hy ekena cakreṇa na rathasya gatir bhavet*  
*tathā puruṣakāreṇa vinā daivaṁ na siddhyati*

**Перевод:**

**As a chariot cannot move on one wheel, so fate cannot yield results without endeavor.**

**Комментарий:**

Success in life is obtained neither by fate nor endeavor alone, but by their alliance. Both indicate action. The results of activities done in the past are called fate, and actions performed at present are called endeavor. Yet, of the two, endeavor is superior, as the result (fate) is dependent on the cause (endeavor). Moreover, we can control our endeavor, but not our fate. Struggle precedes greatness, which cannot be achieved by passive dependence upon fate.

According to scripture, a man desiring to achieve heaven should perform the fire sacrifice, svarga-kamo yajeta. If by fate one could achieve heaven, then why perform sacrifice at all? If fate reigned supreme, all sastric injunctions would be futile. Sri Sukracarya says (Sukra-niti 1. 47):

*prak-karma-vasatah sarvam bhavaty eveti niscitam*  
*tadopadesa vyarthah syuh karyakarya-prabodhakah*

**If everything is predestined according to one’s karma, then scriptural injunctions and prohibitions become meaningless.**

Scriptural instructions are given for the guidance and upliftment of human society. A person would have no option to follow or neglect the scriptural injunctions, if everything is predestined. And how can the words of God and saints be futile? As stated in Agni Purana:

*svam eva karma daivakhyam viddhi dehantararjitam*  
*tasmat paurasam eveha srestham ahur manisinah*

**Deeds performed in past lives are called fate. Therefore the wise say endeavor is superior.**

Since fate is dependent on endeavor, one who acts in the present according to scripture, creates his future fortune.

### Text 31

*pūrva-janma-kṛtaṁ karma tad daivam iti kathyate*  
*tasmāt puruṣakāreṇa yatnaṁ kuryād atandritaḥ*

**Перевод:**

**Activities performed in past lives arc called fate. Throw off laziness, therefore, and endeavor with enthusiasm even now.**

**Комментарий:**

Although our destiny is fixed, we should not be discouraged by ill fortune. We should act enthusiastically in the present to build our future good fortune. Specifically, as per the advice of Lord Kṛṣṇa, we should act without being attached to the fruits of action (Bg. 2. 47):

*karmaṇy evādhikāras te*  
*mā phaleṣu kadācana*  
*mā karma-phala-hetur bhūr*  
*mā te saṅgo 'stv akarmaṇi*

**You have a right to perform your prescribed duty, but you are not entitled to the fruits of action. Never consider yourself the cause of the results of your activities, and never be attached to not doing your duty.**

The Lord also advises that we perform our duty despite happiness or distress, victory or defeat, or loss or gain. When problems arise, we should not resign ourselves to the inevitable, but should act with intelligence to solve them. Since animals have the instinct to protect themselves, why should humans, having superior intelligence, leave everything to fate?

A story in the fourth section of Hitopadesa illustrates this point. In a pond lived three fish, Anagata-vidhata (one who anticipates problems and quickly acts before they arrive), Pratyupanna-mati (one who has sharp intelligence and solves problems as they arise), and Yad-bhavisya (one who procrastinates, simply depending on fate). One day they heard that a fisherman was approaching, so Anagata-vidhata immediately left for another residence. When the fisherman came, he easily caught the other two. Using his sharp intelligence, Pratyupanna- mati feigned death and was thrown back into the pond. The fisherman ate Yad-bhavisya, who had resigned himself to his fate. Thus we see the end of one who passively depends on fate.

### Text 32

***na daivam api saṁcintya tyajed udyogam ātmanaḥ***  
***anudyogena tailāni tilebhyo nāptum arhati***

**Перевод:**

**As one cannot get oil from sesame seeds without pressing them, even if fate is favorable, one should not give up hard work.**

**Комментарий:**

Anyac ca — Also...

### Text 33

***udyoginaṁ puruṣa-siṁham upaiti lakṣmīr***  
***daivena deyam iti kāpuruṣā vadanti***  
***daivaṁ nihatya kuru pauruṣam ātma-śaktyā***  
***yatne kṛte yadi na sidhyati ko 'tra doṣaḥ***

**Перевод:**

**Wealth approaches an industrious lion among men. Lazy men say that there is no need to work hard; fate supplies everything. Dismissing such lazy ideas, one should endeavor according to one’s capacity, and if one is unsuccessful, what is the loss? (Or one should determine what went wrong. )**

**Комментарий:**

Here is the author’s verdict on fate. Though one may be fortunate, still he must endeavor. By good fortune one may get sesame seeds, but effort is required to extract the oil. Complete dependence on fate is symptomatic of the mode of ignorance. Arjuna was certainly born with a great ability to fight, but still he practiced — even at night. The purusa simha is not one who is born with a silver spoon in his mouth, but one who has struggled. Only such persons make history.

Even if one is unsuccessful after endeavoring for a worthwhile goal, what is the loss? Better to have tried and failed than never to have tried at all. And if one avoids acting, what is the gain? It is not laudable that one has never failed at anything because one never dared to try.

For one who perseveres, failures are the stepping stones to success. As one gains valuable experience for future endeavors, one’s thinking capacity increases and one’s character is strengthened. Success is rarely seen without experiencing failure, and success without failure is not as relishable. Without a hard struggle there cannot be a glorious victory.

The phrase ko’tra dosah (what is the loss? ) can also mean that one should determine what went wrong, improve, and try again. Being unsuccessful is no reason to give up. One must pinpoint the cause of failure and renew the attempt. Unfortunate men cannot learn from their experiences, whereas intelligent men see life as a learning experience. For them, failure is simply another step on the path toward the goal. The great inventor Thomas Edison failed ten thousand times in his attempt to invent the light bulb. When someone pointed this out to him, he remarked that he had not failed ten thousand times but had learned ten thousand ways how not to make a light bulb. Success is not accidental; it is the result of positive effort.

Sometimes devotees have the misconception that being surrendered means that Kṛṣṇa or their guru will do everything for them and they can just sit back and relax. They say, “I'm so lucky to have such a wonderful guru to take care of me. Now I don’t have to think.” Such inaction in the name of surrender to the Supreme Lord is nothing but spiritual lethargy. Actually, it is a sign of impotency, inability, ignorance, and ultimately cheating.

Lord Kṛṣṇa spoke the Bhagavad-gita just to drive out such confusion in the name of surrender and religion. After hearing the Bhagavad- gita, Arjuna surrendered to Kṛṣṇa and agreed to fight. He did not sleep on the chariot and let Kṛṣṇa do everything, but fought vigorously on behalf of Kṛṣṇa. God helps those who help themselves. He has given us the human body replete with hands, legs, mind, and intelligence, and if we utilize it properly, we can achieve the mercy of the Lord.

### Text 34

***yathā mṛt-piṇḍataḥ kartā kurute yad yad icchati***  
***evam ātma-kṛtaṁ karma mānavaḥ pratipadyate***

**Перевод:**

**Just as a potter can make anything he desires from a lump of clay, one can create good or bad results according to his activities.**

### Text 35

***kākatālīyavat prāptaṁ dṛṣṭvāpi nidhim agrataḥ***  
***na svayaṁ daivam ādatte puruṣārtham apekṣate***

**Перевод:**

**Even if by coincidence one finds some treasure before him, fate will not place it in his pocket. Some effort is required to pick it up.**

**Комментарий:**

Here is the defect of completely depending on fate. Without endeavor, even fate cannot be fruitful. Therefore one should never be discouraged. In the case of devotional service to the Lord, which is not governed by fate, effort is of the utmost importance. Srila Rupa Gosvami says that success depends on six things (Upadesamrta):

There are six principles favorable to the execution of pure devotional service: (1) being enthusiastic, (2) endeavoring with confidence, (3) being patient, (4) acting according to regulative principles \[such as Sravanam kirtanam visnoh smaranam — hearing, chanting and remembering Kṛṣṇa\], (5) abandoning the association of nondevotees, and (6) following in the footsteps of the previous acaryas. These six principles undoubtedly assure the complete success of pure devotional service.

These six principles apply to success in material or spiritual goals. The general meaning of items four, five, and six is to do whatever is required to achieve the goal; to give up association of persons obstructing the goal; and to learn from successful people in the desired field. All successful men have these characteristics and modus operandi.

An enthusiastic person attracts success, whereas one lacking enthusiasm is like a walking corpse. Lord Kṛṣṇa advised Arjuna to fight without lethargy, yudhyasva vigata-jvarah (Bg. 3. 30). Fighting indicates performance of one’s duty and vigata-jvara implies enthusiasm. One who acts with determination, free from doubt and hesitation is sure to achieve success. Enthusiasm, however, must be tempered with patience and flexibility. We must always be prepared to adapt, adjust, or change until the goal is achieved. For most people this is difficult, as they prefer to cling to the status quo in safety, rather than take the risk of change. Fanaticism or narrow-mindedness also hampers our progress.

Of course, we need not associate with those whose association is detrimental. Rather, we can emulate the character and habits of successful people. To act as a role model for humanity, Lord Kṛṣṇa appears in the material world. If we follow Kṛṣṇa’s example and that of great souls, we can achieve success, mahajano yena gatah sa panthah.

### Text 36

***udyamena hi sidhyanti kāryāṇi na manorathaiḥ***  
***nahi suptasya siṁhasya praviśanti mukhe mṛgāḥ***

**Перевод:**

**Only by hard work can one attain success, not simply by wishful thinking. Animals do not enter the mouth of a sleeping lion.**

**Комментарий:**

Wishful thinking is insufficient for success. Some people dream of being a millionaire, or a leader, a pure devotee, and so on, but lack the necessary faith to carry it through. Undoubtedly, thinking is the seed of all activity, but it must propel us into action. As a tree comes from a seed, gross things come from the subtle, but positive thinking alone will not lead to the goal. One must take positive action with perseverance, determination and faith, following a practical plan. It is said that man proposes and God disposes, but it is also said that the Lord helps those who help themselves.

Some sadhus like to follow ajagara-vrtti-vrata, lying down like a python without endeavoring for food. Why should a human act like a python? If one behaves like a python, he becomes one in his next life. Lord Kṛṣṇa says, svadharme nidhanam sreyah para-dharmo bhaya-vahah, one should not follow another’s path, but can achieve perfection by following one’s own prescribed duty. We cannot imitate the behavior of persons like Rsabhadeva who neglected rules and regulations.

Srimad Bhagavatam states, “Who is that mystic yogi who can follow the example of Lord Rsabhadeva even with his mind? Lord Rsabhadeva rejected all kinds of yogic perfection, which other yogis hanker to attain. Who is that yogi who can compare to Lord Rsabhadeva?” (Bhag. 5. 6. 15). The verse states that those who try to imitate the activities of an avadhuta like Rsabhadeva will plunge into hell, “Low-class people, due to their gross ignorance, introduce a system of religion that deviates from the Vedic principles. Following their own mental concoctions, they automatically fall down into the darkest regions of existence.” (Bhag. 5. 6. 11)

Impotent persons rationalize their inability to act by referring to examples like Rsabhadeva. In this regard Sukrararya says, (Sukra-niti 1. 148):

*dhimanto vandyacarita manyate paurusam mahat*  
*asaktah paurusam kartum kliba daivamupsate*

**The intelligent, who have glorious character, consider endeavor supreme. Weaklings, who are impotent, worship fate.**

### Text 37

***mātā śatruḥ pitā vairī yena bālo na pāṭhitaḥ***  
***na śobhate sabhā-madhye haṁsa-madhye bako yathā***

**Перевод:**

**Those parents who do not educate their son are enemies. As a crane is not respected among swans, so in an assembly of learned persons an uneducated son is not respected.**

**Комментарий:**

Earlier, the author praised learning and explained the importance of having a learned son. He also stressed how one should not depend on fate, but must endeavor, specifically for knowledge. Often, out of laziness, young students give up their education, concluding it is not for them. Herein the author refutes this mentality.

The great poet Kalidasa was born in the brahmana community but was a simpleton, uninterested in study. Preferring to tend goats, he would cut branches from trees so the goats could feed on the leaves. Once some brahmanas watched Kalidasa fall out of a tree after cutting off the very branch he sat on. Seeing his folly, they decided to trick him.

Nearby lived a learned brahmana woman who had defeated many learned scholars in debate, thereby insulting them. It was known that she would only marry a man who could defeat her in debate. These mischievous brahmanas decided to diminish her pride. Dressing Kalidasa nicely, they told him they would arrange his marriage to a very beautiful girl if he would agree to pose as an important pandita observing a vow of silence. Kalidasa was overjoyed, as he never expected that any beautiful girl would marry a dunce like him.

The brahmanas arranged for a debate between the two and informed the woman that pandita Kalidasa was their guru, and since he had taken a vow of silence, the debate would have to be conducted by hand gestures. She consented and began the debate by raising one finger to indicate that the ultimate reality is one. The foolish Kalidasa thought she was threatening to poke him in the eye. So in retaliation he raised two fingers to let her know that if she tried, he would poke his fingers into both of her eyes.

The brahmana judges immediately clapped and exclaimed, “Our guru has won this point as the ultimate reality is dual. There is atma and Paramatma and they can never be one.” In this way Kalidasa scored the first point.

Next the woman lifted her hand showing all five fingers, indicating that the body is made of five elements. Kalidasa took it that she was threatening to slap him in the face, so in response he raised his hand and made a fist. The brahmanas cheered in ecstasy, saying, “The body is indeed made of five elements, but only when they are mixed together. This has been perfectly and eloquently signified by the fist. Our guru has defeated you.”

The woman accepted defeat and agreed to marry Kalidasa. Now a fool can go unrecognized until he opens his mouth. Soon after the wedding she realized he was a dullard and locked him out of the house, saying he could not return until he was educated. Dejected, Kalidasa pondered what to do.

He began wandering, and one day, feeling thirsty, went to a well where village women were drawing water with buckets tied with rope. When he saw that the ropes from the buckets had made deep grooves in the stones around the top of the well, it dawned on him that if hard stone could be cut by soft ropes, his dull brain could also absorb knowledge by persistent drilling. From this incident, he gained determination to study, and ultimately became one of India’s greatest poets.

Students should not be discouraged, therefore, and parents should not neglect their responsibility to arrange for the education of their sons. As explained here, uneducated sons are like ugly cranes in an assembly of swans; and parents responsible for this plight are like enemies.

### Text 38

***rūpa-yauvana-sampannā viśāla-kula-sambhavāḥ***  
***vidyā-hīnā na śobhante nirgandhā iva kiṁśukāḥ***

**Перевод:**

**Although handsome, young, and born in a famous family — like a kimsuka flower, which looks beautiful but has no fragrance — an uneducated man is useless.**

**Комментарий:**

Kimsuka flowers, also known as Palasa, are red and grow in big trees, and although very beautiful, have no fragrance. Thus they are not used for any purpose. Similarly, one may be young and beautiful, but without proper education he is useless and may only cause disturbance to others. Learning, therefore, is the essence of all good qualities.

### Text 39

***pustakeṣu ca nādhītaṁ nādhītaṁ guru-sannidhau***  
***na śobhate sambhā-madhye jāra-garbha iva striyāḥ***

**Перевод:**

**Just as an illegitimate child is not respected, a man who has not studied under a teacher nor from books is not respected.**

**Комментарий:**

We receive knowledge from books, which must be studied under a bona fide teacher. As an illegitimate child is unfit for religious functions within the varnasrama system and is not respected by the learned, so one who has not studied under a bona fide guru will not have proper understanding and is not fit to sit in an assembly of learned men. Similarly, at present, if one lacks academic credentials, his competence is questioned. Therefore, Vedic literature stresses acquiring knowledge from a bona fide guru.

According to Mahabharata, a brahmana named Devadatta neglected his studies during his youth. As he matured and realized the importance of learning, he decided to acquire knowledge by performing penance, without approaching a teacher. He thought, tapasa kim na sidhyate, “By penance everything can be acquired,” so he went to the forest to perform austerities. Although Lord Indra had advised him to study under a teacher, proud Devadatta did not listen.

One day Devadatta went to bathe in the river and noticed an old brahmana sitting on the bank while dumping sand with his hands into the river. Devadatta asked, “What are you doing?”

“I’m building a bridge over the river.”

Laughing, Devadatta remarked, “How can you build a bridge with sand? Just see how the water is carrying it away.”

The brahmana, who was Indra in disguise, replied, “If you can acquire knowledge without approaching a teacher, surely I can build a bridge with sand.”

On hearing this, Devadatta realized his mistake, subdued his pride, gave up his penance, and sought a teacher.

*etac cintayitvā rājā paṇḍita-sabhāṁ kāritavān rājovāca-bho bhoḥ paṇḍitāḥ! śrūyatāṁ mama vacanam asti kaścid evambhūto vidvān yo mama putrāṇāṁ nityam unmārga-gāminām anadhigata-śāstrāṇām idānīṁ nīti-śāstropadeśena punar janma kārayituṁ samarthaḥ?*

**Thinking in this way, the king assembled some learned scholars and said, “O learned men, please listen. Can any of you give second birth to my ill-behaved and illiterate sons by educating them in Niti-sastra?”**

Yatah — Because,

### Text 40

***kācaḥ kāñcana-saṁsargād dhatte mārakatīr dyutīḥ***  
***tathā sat-sannidhānena mūrkho yāti pravīṇatām***

**Перевод:**

**As a piece of glass appears like a jewel when held near gold, so a fool gains proficiency through learned association.**

**Комментарий:**

Literally, the verse says that a piece of glass appears like an emerald (marakata-mani) when held near gold. Green glass appears like an emerald, and plain glass like a yellow sapphire when held near gold. The point is that elevated association promotes the lowly and thus a fool can be transformed into a learned man. Although glass cannot permanently acquire the emerald’s qualities, a fool can actually become scholarly by associating with learned men, because, unlike glass, man is conscious and can retain knowledge. For his sons who were like worthless pieces of glass, the king desired a teacher like gold.

### Text 41

***hīyate hi matis tāta hīnaiḥ saha samāgamāt***  
***samaiś ca samatām eti viśiṣṭaiś ca viśiṣṭatām***

**Перевод:**

**One’s intelligence is degraded by associating with low class people, remains the same by associating with equals, and is improved by associating with superiors.**

**Комментарий:**

Like a mirror, a person’s mind reflects nearby objects. A mirror, however, does not acquire the qualities of the objects it reflects; whereas the mind is influenced by the characteristics of persons with whom we associate. It is said, therefore, that a man is known by the company he keeps:

*yadi santaṁ sevate yadyasantaṁ*  
*tapasvinaṁ yadi vā stenameva*  
*vāso yathā raṅga vaśaṁ prayāti*  
*tathā sa teṣāṁ vaśamabhyupaiti*

**One is influenced by his company, be he a saint, a sinner, an ascetic, or a thief. A man is like a cloth which absorbs the dye it’s soaked in. (MB. Santi Parva 299. 33)**

Dvivida was a devotee of Lord Rama who fought against Ravana's army, yet later developed demoniac qualities by the association of Narakasura. As a result, he was killed by Lord Balarama for his abominable behavior. In contrast, Narada was the son of a maidservant who became a great sage by holy association.

The mirror as a metaphor for the mind indicates that the effect of association is instantaneous, but people are generally reluctant to believe the almost instantaneous effect of association on the mind of another. Contemporary students of human nature are aware, however, that there is no exaggeration at all. For example, after studying human nature for more than fifty years, Erich Fromm made this observation about bad company:

Bad company, on the other hand, is not only the company of merely trivial people but of evil, sadistic, destructive life-hostile people. But why, one might ask, is there danger in the company of bad people, unless they try to harm one in one form or another?

In order to answer this question it is necessary to recognize a law in human relations: There is no contact between human beings that does not affect both of them. No meeting between two people, no conversation between them, except perhaps the most casual one, leaves either one of them unchanged — even though the change may be so minimal as to be unrecognizable except by its cumulative effect when such meetings are frequent.

The king is exhibiting wisdom, therefore, in arranging for his dull sons to have good association. One may argue that the boys’ association will negatively affect the learned brahmana. While it is generally true that association influences both parties, there is no mutual exchange of influence in the case of one who is not weak-willed, but fixed in his own values. Otherwise saints would never grant their association to materialistic people. It is to be expected, therefore, that a learned brahmana will uplift the ignorant princes, while their company will have no ill effect on him.

*atrāntare viṣṇu-śarma-nāmā mahā-paṇḍitaḥ sakala-nīiti-śāstra-tattva-jño bṛhaspatir ivābravīt-deva mahākula-sambhūtā ete rājaputrāḥ tat mayā nītiṁ grāhayituṁ śakyante yataḥ:*

**Hearing the king’s speech, a great scholar named Visnu Sarma, who was well-versed in Niti-sastra, spoke like Brhaspati, “O King, these princes, descendants of an exalted race, can learn Niti-sastra from me. Because...**

### Text 42

***nādravye nihitā kācit kriyā phalavatī bhavet***  
***na vyāpāra-śatenāpi śukavat pāṭhyate bakaḥ***

**Перевод:**

**Just as by hundreds of attempts one cannot teach a crane to speak like a parrot, unfit persons can never be trained.**

**Комментарий:**

Different types of knowledge suit different natures. After assessing the ability and interest of students, the teacher must educate them accordingly. As it is easier to sail with the wind than against it, it is easier to train a student according to his nature. Bad teachers fixate on the deficiencies of the student, but an expert teacher knows how to make the student soar with his strengths. This is the basic principle of varnasrama culture — that one is educated in terms of one’s nature.

In spiritual life, the ability of the student is measured in two ways — his capacity to understand the subject matter and his ability to use it properly. If a teacher suspects that a student will misuse what he is teaching, he should not educate him in that subject. An example of misuse is in Mahabharata where we find that Asvatthama was unqualified to learn the art of throwing the brahmastra weapon as he could not control his anger. Yet Dronacarya made the mistake of teaching him, resulting in unnecessary danger to the Pandavas.

Knowledge is power and more so is spiritual knowledge, but when given to unqualified students, spiritual knowledge is degraded. One should, therefore, avoid instructing unqualified persons, who will definitely abuse spiritual knowledge.

Anyac ca — Furthermore,

### Text 43

***asmiṁs tu nirguṇaṁ gotre nāpatyam upajāyate***  
***ākare padya-rāgānāṁ janma kāca-maṇeḥ kutaḥ***

**Перевод:**

**As glass is not produced in the mine of Padmaraga (topaz), useless and unfit sons do not take birth in this royal dynasty.**

**Комментарий:**

*ato 'haṁ ṣaṇ-māsābhyantare bhavat-putrān nīti-śāstrābhijñān kariṣyāmi rājā sa-vinayaṁ punar uvāca*

**Within six months, therefore, I will make your sons proficient in Niti- sastra.**

The king again spoke humbly...

### Text 44

***kīṭo 'pi sumanaḥ-saṅgād ārohati satāṁ śiraḥ***  
***aśmāpi yāti devatvaṁ mahadbhiḥ supratiṣṭhitaḥ***

**Перевод:**

**A worm in a flower can reach the heads of saints, and a stone can become divine if installed by devotees.**

**Комментарий:**

How can a worm reach the head of a saint? If nestled in a flower within a garland worn by a saint, a lowly worm can achieve the impossible. Similarly, by good association a low class person can attain rare heights. Marble that is generally used for flooring, if carved into a Deity and installed by a great devotee is worshiped as God. The marble acquires divinity on the spiritual potency of the devotee.

In this and the following two verses the king glorifies the virtues of good association. He praises Visnu Sarma, who has kindly volunteered to take charge of his sons, thus encouraging him to put his heart into teaching the princes. When saintly persons are praised, they become enthusiastic and dutiful, not wanting to disappoint one’s expectations, thinking that now they must exhibit these qualities for which they are being praised. Praise brings out the best in them. Low class people, on the other hand, become proud, thinking that they naturally possess all these qualities. Thus they want to assume a position loftier than their actual level of attainment. Praise brings out the worst in them.

Some recommend that one should be lavish in praising others, but actually, praise as a motivator is only for intelligent persons, and even then, it should be used judiciously, for it can quickly turn to poison. Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Saraswati Gosvami, understanding the treacherous nature of praise, used to instruct his disciples, “One who praises me is my enemy, one who chastises me is my friend.” Wise words, but difficult to practice. Visnu Sarma was above the negative effects of praise and as we shall see, he responds positively to the kings words by instructing the foolish princes with keen wisdom and insight.

### Text 45

*yathodaya-girer dravyaṁ sannikarṣeṇa dīpyate*  
*tathā sat-sannidhānena hīna-varṇo 'pi dīpyate*

**Перевод:**

**As a lusterless stone of Udayagiri shines in the rays of the rising sun, so a lowborn person becomes brilliant in saintly association.**

**Комментарий:**

Udayagiri is a mountain in Eastern India which shines like gold when bathed by the morning sun. Similarly, the intelligence of a person born in a low family shines in good association. The word vama means “color” as well as “caste” and dipyate means “shines” as well as “attains bright intelligence.”

### Text 46

***guṇā guṇajñeṣu guṇā bhavanti***  
***te nirguṇaṁ prāpya bhavanti doṣāḥ***  
***āsvādya-toyāḥ pravahanti nadyaḥ***  
***samudram āsādya bhavanty upeyāḥ***

**Перевод:**

**As sweet river water becomes unpalatable upon reaching the sea, good qualities possessed by the virtuous are beneficial, yet when possessed by the impious they become defects.**

**Комментарий:**

If unqualified persons attain something of value, they simply misuse it. An unfit student cannot learn properly. As rain water flows from high mountains into lakes below, knowledge descends to the student who is humble and obedient. Knowledge given to an arrogant disciple, who is envious of his teacher, will be misused. It is compared to feeding milk to a snake, which only increases its poison. In this regard the author says,

*payaḥ-pānaṁ bhujaṅgānāṁ kevalaṁ viṣa-vardhanam*  
*upadeśo hi mūrkhāṇāṁ prakopāya na śāntaye /*  
*anyac ca-vidvān evopadeṣṭavyo nāvidvāṁs tu kadācana*

**Feeding milk to a snake increases its venom; similarly giving good instructions to a fool only angers him. One should instruct a sober man and not an upstart.**

After receiving boons from Lord Brahma, Hiranyakasipu became so powerful that he created havoc in the three worlds. Lord Nrsimhadeva had to rebuke Lord Brahma: “My dear Lord Brahma, O great lord born from the lotus flower, just as it is dangerous to feed milk to a snake, so it is dangerous to give benedictions to demons, who are; by nature ferocious and jealous. I warn you not to give such benedictions to any demon again.”

*tad eteṣām asmat-putrāṇāṁ nīti-śāstropadeśāya bhavantaḥ pramāṇam ity uktvā tasya viṣṇu-śarmaṇo kare bahumāna-puraḥsaraṁ putrān samarpitavān*

**Therefore you are qualified to instruct my sons in Niti-sastra. Saying this, with great respect, the king placed his sons in the hands of Visnu Sarma.**

Iti Prastavika — Thus ends the introduction.

Good education is the foundation of a cultured society and should be administered in youth when the mind is sharp and easily molded. Poor students are often motivated to study so they can improve their economic condition, while wealthy students, like these royal princes, may lack motivation. When the students are not properly motivated, there is every chance they will fall prey to bad association and be corrupted. Thus the author stresses the importance of good association, especially among the ruling class. At present, the leaders of society are poorly trained, and consequently misuse their administrative position for self-aggrandizement and keeping the status quo.

The mass media reaches into every corner and makes it practically impossible for anyone to remain unaffected by the advertising that so effectively disrupts everyone’s concentration. Indeed, those who want to pursue spiritual life must be very determined to avoid this polluting association. Careful study and application of the teachings of Hitopadesa will help combat this degrading influence and help the student develop good character so he may one day become a great soul, a mahatma. The prologue to Hitopadesa ends here.

## Мitra-lābhaḥ Acquisition of Friends

*atha prāsāda-pṛṣṭhe sukhopaviṣṭānāṁ rājaputrāṇāṁ purastāt prastāva-krameṇa paṇḍito 'bravīt-bho rāja-putrāḥ śṛṇuta — ”*

**Перевод:**

**Thereafter, as an introduction, Pandita Visnu Sarma spoke to the princes who were comfortably seated on the roof of the palace, “O Princes, listen..**

### Text 1

*kāvya-śāstra-vinodena kālo gacchati dhīmatām*  
*vyasanena tu mūrkhāṇāṁ nidrayā kalahena vā*

**Перевод:**

**While the foolish waste time in vices, in sleeping or fighting, the intelligent relish scripture and poetic literature.**

**Комментарий:**

Our lifespan is limited, because time devours all. In old age our memory, intelligence, senses, and bodily strength weaken, depriving us of our ability to perform rigorous physical or mental labor. Therefore, intelligent persons avoid wasting time, as it cannot be increased, and once it passes, it is forever lost.

The foolish, being lazy, prone to sleeping too much and quarreling, are expert in misusing time. Srimad-Bhagavatam explains the proper use of time: “Both by rising and setting, the sun decreases the duration of life of everyone, except one who utilizes the time by discussing the topics of the all-good Personality of Godhead.” (Bhag. 2. 3. 17).

In Kali-yuga, however, the majority of people lack interest in spiritual topics, being addicted to intoxication, quarreling, and illegal activities. Srimad-Bhagavatam was especially compiled by Vyasa to remedy the problems of this age, and enlightened people relish hearing Srimad-Bhagavatam, because it establishes love of God as the highest goal. When one learns to love God and His creation, the darkness created by Kali-yuga is destroyed, and a spiritual atmosphere of perfect harmony is created.

When Pariksit Maharaja learned that he had only one week to live, he immediately gave up his empire to hear Srimad-Bhagavatam. He did not think, “Life is short, so let me enjoy.” He understood the value of hearing spiritual topics. Even today, there are many holy places such as Vrndavana, where saintly persons regularly discuss scripture. The greatest benefit derived by visiting a holy place is to obtain the association of devotees, for without spiritual knowledge one lives in vain. Canakya Pandita says (Canakya-niti 7. 19):

*śunaḥ puccham iva vyarthaṁ jīvitaṁ vidyayā vinā*  
*na guhya-gopane śaktaṁ na ca daṁśa-nivāraṇe*

**An uneducated man’s life is as useless as a dog’s tail, which neither covers its private parts nor drives away insects.**

Visnu Sarma hints to the princes that, being intelligent, they should relish studying literature and poetry. Otherwise they will be considered fools.

*tad bhavatāṁ vinodāya kāka-kūrmādīnāṁ vicitrāṁ kathāṁ kathayiṣyāmi rāja-putrair uktam-ārya! kathyatāṁ viṣṇu-śarmovāca-śṛṇuta yūyam samprati mitra-lābhaḥ prastūyate yasyāyam ādyaḥ ślokaḥ"*

**“Thus, for your pleasure I will recite the wonderful story of the crow, turtle, and others.”**  
**The princes said, “Sir, please tell us.”**  
**Sri Visnu Sarma said, “Listen. This is the first verse of the chapter Mitralabha....”**

### Text 2

***asādhanā vitta-hīnā buddhimantaḥ suhṛn-matāḥ***  
***sādhayanty āśu kāryāṇi kāka-kūrma-mṛgākhuvat***

**Перевод:**

**As shown by the crow, tortoise, deer, and mouse, intelligent persons, though bereft of wealth or means, can quickly accomplish difficult feats by affectionate cooperation.**

**Комментарий:**

In this chapter the author explains the importance of acquiring good friends. Even insignificant people can help wonderfully in an emergency. One desiring success should, therefore, keep cordial relations with others. The slogan, “Unity is strength,” is especially true in this age. This is confirmed in the Sanskrit saying; saṅghe śaktiḥ kalau yuge, in Kali-yuga strength lies in cooperation.

Visnu Sarma advises the princes to cooperate and this comes naturally when those involved have affection for each other. Love and affection are rare in this age of quarrel and hypocrisy, when cheating prevails even among intimates, like husband and wife, father and son, or guru and disciple. How, then, can there be happiness or success?

To counteract the polluted atmosphere of this age, Lord Caitanya preached the congregational chanting of the holy names of Kṛṣṇa, which is a natural expression of love for the Lord and His creation.

*rājaputrā ūcuḥ-katham etat? so 'bravīt-asti godāvarī-tīre viśālaḥ śālmalī-taruḥ tatra nānā-dig-deśād āgatya rātrau pakṣiṇo nivasanti atha kadācid avasannāyāṁ rātrau astācala-cūḍāvalambini bhagavati kumudinī-nāyake candramasi laghupatana-nāmā vāyasaḥ prabuddhaḥ kṛtāntam iva dvitīyam aṭantaṁ pāśa-hastaṁ vyāgham apaśyat tam ālokyācintayat-adya prātar evāniṣṭa-darśanaṁ jātam na jāne kim anabhimataṁ darśayiṣyati ity uktvā tad anusaraṇa-krameṇa vyākulaś calati yataḥ —*

**The princes said, “How was that?”**  
**Visnu Sarma said, “On the bank of the river Godavari is a big Salmali (silk cotton) tree wherein birds from all directions come to roost at night. Once, when the night was almost over and the moon, who is the lord of the kumudini flower (night lotus), was about to set, a crow called Laghupatanaka (swift flier) saw a hunter, appearing like death personified, carrying a net in his hand. Seeing him. the crow reflected, ‘This morning I have seen something inauspicious. Now what disaster will befall us?’**  
**“After saying this, the disturbed crow started following the hunter. For...”**

### Text 3

***śoka-sthāna-sahasrāṇi bhaya-sthāna-śatāni ca***  
***divase divase mūḍham āviśanti na paṇḍitam***

**Перевод:**

**Foolish people daily encounter thousands of occasions for lamentation and fear, but not the learned. (Thus the crow decided to find a solution for the anticipated problem).**

**Комментарий:**

A learned and wise man (pandita) is the best judge of time, place, and circumstance. His wisdom lies in his ability to detect portents of forthcoming problems. Thus he anticipates the future and is prepared to meet it. Not entrusting his life to the hands of fate, he creates his own destiny. Everyone has problems. Yet upon facing them, fools lament, complain, and sometimes even deny that problems exist, whereas the intelligent act to resolve them, accepting full responsibility for every situation. Where a fool sees a problem, an intelligent person. sees a challenge, an opportunity to learn and grow. For a fool, life is a struggle; for the intelligent, life is an adventure.

*anyac ca-viṣayiṇām idam avaśyaṁ kartavyam.*

**Moreover, worldly people must do the following —**

### Text 4

***utthāyotthāya boddhavyaṁ kim adya sukṛtaṁ kṛtam***  
***āyuṣaḥ khaṇḍam ādāya ravir astaṁ gamiṣyati***

**Перевод:**

**Daily upon arising one should consider that a great calamity is at hand. Which will befall him today — death, misery, or lamentation?**

**Комментарий:**

In industry there are two types of maintenance — crisis and preventive. Crisis maintenance entails repairing machines once they have become broken. Preventive maintenance means to service the machines so they don’t break down. Breakdown maintenance is uncertain, generally expensive, a cause of anxiety, and for the most part could be avoided with planned preventive maintenance. Similarly, one should be prepared to anticipate problems in one’s life and plan to resolve them before they come to the point of crisis. This is a sign of good intelligence.

*atha tena vyādhena taṇḍula-kaṇān vikīrya jālaṁ vistīrṇam sa ca tatra pracchanno bhūtvā sthitaḥ asminn eva kāle citragrīva-nāmā kapota-rājaḥ sa-parivāro viyati visarpaṁs taṇḍula-kaṇān avalokayāmāsa tataḥ kapota-rājas taṇḍula-kaṇa-lubdhān kapotān prāha-kuto 'tra nirjane vane taṇḍula-kaṇānāṁ sambhavaḥ tan nirūpyatāṁ tāvat bhadram idaṁ na paśyāmi prāyeṇānena taṇḍula-kaṇa-lobhenāsmābhir api tathā bhavitavyam”*

**Then the hunter scattered rice on the ground, spread his net and hid nearby. Along came the pigeon Citragriva (with a multi-colored neck) with his retinue. Seeing the rice, he said to the pigeons, who were hungry. “This is not auspicious. Think about it. How can rice be here in this lonely forest? I think by being greedy for these grains we will meet the same end (as the traveler who coveted the gold bracelet. )”**

### Text 5

***kaṅkaṇasya tu lobhena magnaḥ paṅke sudustare***  
***vṛddha-vyāghreṇa samprāptaḥ pathikaḥ sammṛtaḥ***

**Перевод:**

**Overcome by greed for a gold bracelet, the traveler sank in the mud and was captured and killed by the old tiger. (Similarly we will meet with death).**

**Комментарий:**

With the so-called advancement of civilization, it has become a big business to cheat the innocent. In the West, salesmen are trained to ensnare the gullible by exploiting their greedy mentality. Influenced by greed, the common man’s discrimination is covered and he becomes easy prey for intelligent crooks who are quick to exploit his weakness.

In India also, there are many cheaters who profess they can double one’s wealth by mystic power. Arriving at a village, they convince the simple illiterate women that they will multiply their jewelry for a very small price. Being greedy for opulence, the village women entrust their ornaments to the rogues, who trick them and steal their ornaments. An intelligent person would wonder why the so-called mystics waste their time going from village to village, if they actually had power? Rather, their immense opulence would attract everyone to their residence for guidance in increasing wealth. Blinded by attachment, however, discrimination is lost and one easily becomes a victim.

Consumer-oriented industry is largely responsible for the increase in crime in society as it produces so many desirable objects of enjoyment — cars, stylish clothes, electronic gadgets, etc. Then, with imaginative and alluring advertisements, businessmen try to convince people that they actually need these things, that without the latest in everything they will not be happy. Obsessed with obtaining various unnecessary conveniences, people plan how to get more money. Of course, in the West, with easy credit, people are deceived into thinking they have more money than they actually do. Then when their expenses exceed their income, they may take to illegal schemes to get rid of debts or to satisfy their desires.

The author, therefore, warns us to guard against greed. Lord Kṛṣṇa says that lust, anger, and greed are the gateways to hell (Bg. 16. 21). Although people are warned, they are still deceived because greed covers their intelligence.

Citragriva warned the hungry pigeons that rice is not usually found in a forest, since it does not grow wild. Therefore, somebody must have placed it there as bait. Yet the greedy pigeons did not listen.

*kapotā ūcuḥ-katham etat? so 'bravīt —*

**The pigeons said, “How was that? Citragriva said — ”**

## Story 1

***aham ekadā dakṣiṇāraṇye carann apaśyam eko vṛddho vyāghraḥ snātaḥ kuśa-hastaḥ saras-tīre brūte-bho bho panthāḥ! idaṁ suvarṇa-kaṅkaṇaṁ gṛhyatām tato lobhākṛṣṭena kenacit pānthena ālocitam-bhāgyena etat sambhavati kintu asmin ātma-sandehe pravṛttir na vidheyā yataḥ —***

**Перевод:**

**Once while wandering in a forest in the South (Dandakaranya), I saw an old tiger, who after bathing, would stand on the bank of a pond with some sacred kusa grass in his paw, saying to the passersby, “O traveler, please accept this gold bracelet in charity”. Hearing this, a greedy traveler thought, “This has occurred by good fortune; yet one should avoid activities which involve personal risk. Because — ”**

### Text 6

***aniṣṭād iṣṭa-lābhe'pi na gatir jāyate śubhā***  
***yatrāste viṣa-saṁsargo 'mṛtaṁ tad api mṛtyave***

**Перевод:**

**Although nectar is desirable, when mixed with poison it causes death. Similarly, to accept a desirable thing from a dangerous source is inauspicious. (So, I should not take it).**

**Комментарий:**

Before embarking on a venture one should carefully weigh the advantages and disadvantages, and if the latter are greater, the proposition should be abandoned. Intelligence means that we see both sides of a proposition, the advantages and disadvantages, the risks and the rewards. Unfortunately, greed can cover one’s discriminating power and blind one from seeing disadvantages.

A tranquil mind and the advice of well-wishers are the foundation of good planning. The next step is to determine if one is capable of executing the plan. The expected outcome may be desirable, but if one lacks the capacity to execute the plan, again, better to abandon it. For example, one may desire to be the president of a country, but if he is unqualified, why should he endeavor?

Another possible scenario is this: the goal is advantageous; one has the capability to achieve it; but the execution of the plan creates an negative side-effect which outweighs the positive aspect of the goal. In this case also, one should not proceed. As mentioned above, one may benefit from drinking nectar; he may have the capacity to digest it; but if it is mixed with poison, one should not accept. In this regard the author of Hitopadesa narrates a story in Book Four (Sandhi 10):

*upāyaṁ cintayan prājño hy apāyam api cintayet*  
*paśyato baka-mūrkhasya nakulair bhakṣitāḥ sutāḥ*

**An intelligent person, while finding a solution to a problem should also consider the disadvantages. Otherwise one will lament like the foolish crane, whose offspring were eaten by the mongoose.**

In a banyan tree lived some cranes whose young were being eaten by a snake who lived in the same tree. To alleviate the problem, one old crane suggested they catch fish and lay them in a line starting from a mongoose’s den leading up to the snake’s hole in the tree. The plan was that the mongoose would eat the fish and be led to the snake’s hole and kill it, since they are natural enemies. The cranes implemented this plan, and sure enough, the snake was killed. But when the mongoose discovered the nest of young cranes, he decided to feast on them as well. Thus the solution caused more problems than it solved.

This is the traveler’s dilemma: He needs gold. The tiger has a bracelet, but since tigers eat men, the traveler could get killed in the process. So better to abandon the venture. Yet we will see how greed covers his discrimination and he loses his life.

*kintu sarvatrārthārjana-pravṛttau sandeha eva tathā coktam —*

**But there is always danger when it comes to earning money. It is said —**

### Text 7

*na saṁśayam anāruhya naro bhadrāṇi paśyati*  
*saṁśayaṁ punar āruhya yadi jīvati paśyati*

**Перевод:**

**Good fortune eludes a person bewildered by doubts, which dampen the spirit of adventure. Abandoning all doubt, one must act to achieve his end, provided he survives.**

**Комментарий:**

Enthusiasm flourishes when one is free from doubt. Half-hearted attempts never succeed, for the rain of doubt dampens the fire of enthusiasm. However, one should not act rashly. Proper deliberation saves us trouble, time, and money. Decisions mold our destiny. Therefore we must be very careful in decision-making, and after the decision is made, we should never lose sight of the goal. It is wise to be flexible in approach, but determined in aim.

An important point in this verse is yadi jivati — if he survives. One should not embark on an adventure which will cost one his life. If life is lost, everything is lost; thus life must be protected by all means.

*tan nirūpayāmi tāvat prakāśaṁ brūte kutra tava kaṅkaṇam? vyāghro hastaṁ prasārya darśayati pāntho 'vadat-kathaṁ mārātmake tvayi viśvāsaḥ?*

**The traveler thought, “Let me first examine the bracelet,” then said, “Where is your bracelet?”**  
**The tiger streched out his paw and showed it to him.**  
**The traveler asked, “How can I trust a ferocious animal like you?”**

*vyāghra uvāca-śṛṇu re pāntha! prāg eva yauvana-daśāyām aham atīva durvṛtta āsam aneka-go-mānuṣāṇāṁ vadhād me putrā mṛtā dārāś ca vaṁśa-hīnaś cāham tataḥ kenacid dhārmikeṇāham upadiṣṭaḥ dāna-dharmādikaṁ caratu bhavān iti tad-upadeśādi-dānīm ahaṁ snāna-śīlo dātā vṛddho galita-nakha-dantaḥ na kathaṁ viśvāsa-bhūmiḥ? yataḥ —*

**The tiger said, “Listen, O traveler, in my youth, I committed many sins by killing cows and human beings. Consequently, my wife and sons have died and I am left completely alone. Some time ago, a wise saint instructed me to distribute charity and be religious. Following his instructions, I now bathe regularly and afterwards distribute charity. Now I am old and my claws and teeth are worn out. Why shouldn’t you have faith in my words? For — ”**

The tiger, representing a bogus godman, demonstrates how to cheat in the name of religion. Although a ferocious animal with bestial features, the tiger poses as a pious religionist. Deceptive spiritualists have human bodies and pose as saints, while inwardly possessing a tiger-like mentality. Thus they are more dangerous than the tiger, whose looks are not deceiving. Like the tiger, they confess their past misdeeds and speak eloquently, citing scriptures only to convince the foolish about their greatness. Very expertly, the tiger will convince the traveler how he has given up sinful life and being remorseful, is now engaged in welfare activities as atonement for his past misdeeds. But as a leopard does not change its spots, a tiger does not change its stripes.

If a pious man performs religious acts, people arc not very impressed, but if a rogue changes his appearance and performs religious rituals, they are amazed. To make an external show is easy, but to bring about a change in consciousness is next to impossible. An ass may don a tiger’s skin, but when he hears other asses braying, he will also bray. He can never acquire the qualities of a tiger. One must, therefore, discriminate between a person’s inherent qualities and his imagined ones. To protect ourselves from being cheated, we must distinguish between truth and illusion by looking beyond appearances.

### Text 8

***ijyā'dhyayana-dānāni tapaḥ satyaṁ dhṛtiḥ kṣamā***  
***alobha iti mārgo 'yaṁ dharmasyāṣṭa-vidhaḥ smṛtaḥ***

**Перевод:**

**Scripture explains eight types of religious acts: 1. performance of sacrifice; 2. scriptural study; 3. giving charity; 4. performing austerities; 5. speaking truthfully; 6. being patient; 7. practicing forgiveness; and 8. not being greedy. (Of these I practice charity, truthfulness, freedom from avarice, forgiveness, and I have scriptural knowledge, therefore have faith in me. )**

### Text 9

***tatra pūrvaś caturvargo dambhārtham api sevyate***  
***uttaras tu caturvargo mahātmany eva tiṣṭhati***

**Перевод:**

**Out of these, the first four (sacrifice, study of scripture, charity, and austerity) can be performed for show, but the second four (truthfulness, patience, forgiveness, and freedom from avarice) are found only in magnanimous souls. (Because I am truthful, forbearing, and not greedy, have faith in me).**

**Комментарий:**

Those who want to avoid being deceived in the name of religion, self- realization, mystic power, seeing God, receiving one’s siddha-svarupa, and so on, should carefully study this verse. Hearsay and superficial association are not a sound basis on which to build faith in someone. One must carefully study the character of anyone professing spiritual advancement, as even a cheater can temporarily display saintly behavior. History, both ancient and contemporary, is full of the activities of saints, as well as psychopaths in the guise of saints.

Expert pretenders pose as saints and attract plenty of followers by dazzling people with their appearance, movement, and speech. Actors in TV serials such as Mahabharata, Ramayana and Kṛṣṇa are treated with awe and reverence, as if their pious professional roles are part of their off-camera lives. People were appalled to see a movie where the heroine, who had previously played the part of Sita, was dancing in a bikini with the actor who had portrayed Laksmana. This shows that people are so taken in by outward appearances that they expect actors to only play religious roles. One reason for their wrongful expectations is that formerly in India, religious roles were played by pious actors, who were devotees of the Lord.

The charlatan tiger now lectures on how an imposter imitates religious activities, thus winning the faith of the traveler who represents the common man. The tiger implies that if he were an imposter, he would not be anxious to define the symptoms and thus expose himself. Thus he should be taken as a bona fide religionist. As the first four items can be imitated, he wants to show how he has the second four qualities, establishing himself as a bona fide religious man. As the qualities of truthfulness, patience, forgiveness, and freedom from avarice cannot be easily imitated for a long time, one should detect these qualities in a person before accepting him as saintly. Eloquent speech does not qualify one as a man of integrity. One’s thoughts, words and deeds must be in harmony and above suspicion.

*mama caitāvān lobha-virahaḥ yena sva-hasta-stham api suvarṇa-kaṅkaṇaṁ yasmai kasmaicid dātum icchāmi tathāpi vyāghro mānuṣaṁ khādatīti lokāpavādo durnivāraḥ yataḥ —*

**I have given up greed to the extent that I am offering my own bracelet to a stranger. Still, it is difficult for people to forget that tigers eat men, for...**

The tiger now gives evidence of his religiousness. Since charity and greed cannot coexist, he must be free from greed. Otherwise, how can he be charitable? The tiger is so charitable that, having donated all his other possesions, he is ready to donate his only bracelet. He is exemplifying the adage danena panir na tu kankanena, the beauty of the hand is enhanced by charity, not by a bracelet. Thus the tiger is performing a greater sacrifice than giving to family members or friends by offering his gold bracelet to a total stranger.

Self-praise is a sure symptom of a charlatan. Those who glorify themselves hardly do anything worthy of praise.

### Text 10

***gatānugatiko lokaḥ kuṭṭanīm upadeśinīm***  
***pramāṇayati no dharme yathā goghnam api dvijam***

**Перевод:**

**Blind followers will not respect a prostitute, though she gives sound religious advice, but will respect a brahmana, though a cow-killer.**

**Комментарий:**

To appear virtuous, cheaters generally pose themselves as learned. Scholarship alone, however, does not make a saint; rather, a saint is distinguished by his exemplary conduct. A cow-eater or a prostitute may pose as a saint and speak philosophy, but that does not make them authentic. The Manu-samhita (1. 108) declares, acarah paramo dharmah, the supreme religion is good behavior; and, acarah prabhavo dharmah, religion arises from good conduct. Thus a so-called learned or saintly person who lacks good conduct should never be trusted. Such persons use religion for self-aggrandizement, like the tiger, who wants food without work. The principle being stressed is that one should not accept anything blindly, especially in religious matters.

In itself, birth in a brahmana family does not automatically qualify one as a brahmana. As one can accept gold found lying in stool, one can accept a lowborn person displaying good conduct, as a saint. Vidura and Narada had low births, yet were the most qualified saintly persons. In contrast, the notorious demon Ravana was born in a brahmana family. The tiger implies that his birth as a ferocious animal does not disqualify him from having saintly qualities, although generally, tigers are not saintly. Fools, however, will not detect his good character, as they blindly follow the common belief that a tiger is ferocious. In this way the tiger subtly induces the traveler not to follow the common belief.

*mayā ca dharma-śāstrāṇi adhītāni śṛṇu —*

**The tiger further said, “I have also studied religious books, please listen — ”**

After establishing that he is charitable and free from greed, he now informs the traveler that he is also learned in scripture, thus possessing the second of the eight religious characteristics.

### Text 11

***maru-sthalyāṁ yathā vṛṣṭiḥ kṣudhārte bhojanaṁ tathā***  
***daridre dīyate dānaṁ saphalaṁ pāṇḍu-nandana***

**Перевод:**

**O Yudhisthira, just as rain is useful on dry land, and food is useful when given to the hungry, so charity is useful when given to the poor.**

**Комментарий:**

(Considering this, please accept this bracelet. )

### Text 12

***prāṇā yathātmano 'bhīṣṭā bhūtānām api te tathā***  
***ātmaupamyena bhūtānāṁ dayāṁ kurvanti sādhavaḥ***

**Перевод:**

**As one cherishes his own life, so do other living beings. Considering this, saintly persons bestow their mercy on all creatures.**

**Комментарий:**

aparaṁ ca — And also —

### Text 13

***pratyākhyāne ca dāne ca sukha-duḥkhe priyāpriye***  
***ātmaupamyena puruṣaḥ pramāṇam adhigacchati***

**Перевод:**

**One realizes the effect of offering and refusing charity, of happiness and distress, favorable and unfavorable behavior, by personal experience.**

**Комментарий:**

Experience is the best teacher, nanubhuya na janati puman visaya- tiksanata. A man who has not suffered cannot understand the misery caused by sense objects. There is a story illuminating this point.

Once a prince was sent to gurukula and upon completing his studies, his guru called for the king to take him home. The prince demonstrated his abilities, which pleased the king. Desiring to teach the prince a final lesson, the guru asked the king to ride his horse to a distant tree and back. As the king galloped away, the guru ordered the prince to try and touch the horse. When the king reached the tree and started back, he saw his son panting in pursuit. Furious, he demanded to know why the prince was made to do such hard labor. The guru said, “Now his education is complete. By this experience, he will be more empathetic with the laborer class and thus will not mistreat them.”

In the Bhakti-Sandarbha, Srila Jiva Gosvami discusses how Lord Kṛṣṇa, who is most merciful, does not give His mercy to a suffering man, since He has never undergone material miseries, nor can He experience them, being completely transcendental. Yet, the Lord bestows His mercy when He understands the misery of someone through a devotee. Therefore devotees are the carriers of His mercy. The tiger is implying that because he has himself suffered, he commiserates with the traveler who is also in a miserable condition. Therefore, out of compassion, he wants to help him.

anyac ca — And also —

### Text 14

***mātṛvat para-dāreṣu para-dravyeṣu loṣṭravat***  
***ātmavat sarva-bhūteṣu yaḥ paśyati sa paṇḍitaḥ***

**Перевод:**

**He is a learned scholar who treats all women, except his wife, like his own mother, considers others’ wealth like a clod of dirt, and treats other living entities like his own self.**

**Комментарий:**

In the last four verses, the tiger, who represents the pseudo religionist, convinces the traveler to trust him by posing as a scholar and saint. Quoting popular Puranic verses on the pretext of being merciful to the traveler, he tries to allure him. The tiger correctly establishes from sastra the essential religious principle that a sadhu is one who is merciful to others and a scholar is one who treats all other beings like his own self. However, he is using the sastra for his own ends. Externally, the tiger's heart appears to be melting out of compassion, seeing the traveler suffering from poverty, but actually, he can’t wait to eat the traveler.

Pseudo religionists sing the same tune. Exhibiting apparent renunciation, they portray themselves as the greatest welfare workers of suffering humanity. But, like the tiger, their real aim is to find some fools to exploit.

*tvaṁ ca atīva-durgataḥ tena tat tubhyaṁ dātuṁ sa-yatno 'ham tathā coktam —*

**I know you are very poor, so I offer this bracelet to you. As it is said —**

Anticipating that the traveler may wonder why the bracelet is being offered to him, the tiger begins to give the reasons.

### Text 15

***daridrān bhara kaunteya mā prayaccheśvare dhanam***  
***vyādhitasyauṣadhaṁ pathyaṁ nīrujasya kim auṣadhaiḥ***

**Перевод:**

**O Maharaja Yudhisthira, maintain the poor. There is no need to give to the rich. Medicine is useful for the diseased, not for the healthy.**

**Комментарий:**

anyat ca — Moreover —

### Text 16

***dātavyam iti yad dānaṁ dīyate'nupakāriṇi***  
***deśe kāle ca pātre ca tad dānaṁ sāttvikaṁ viduḥ***

**Перевод:**

**Charity given as a matter of duty, without expectation of return, to a proper person, at a proper place and time is called charity in the mode of goodness. (Since I don’t expect anything in return, please accept this bracelet. )**

**Комментарий:**

The above two verses establish that the tiger does not expect anything from the traveler. Posing as a compassionate person (para-duhkha- duhkhi), and considering the needy traveler to be a worthy recipient, the tiger demonstrated his desire to bestow his mercy. Hearing the tiger’s pleasing words, the traveler’s doubts were dispelled. Thus he decided to accept the invaluable gift, hoping that hi wandering would finally come to an end. Similarly, fools take initiation from a charlatan guru (they accept beads instead of a gold bracelet), thinking they will cheaply achieve liberation, and their ceaseless wanderings in the universe will come to an end.

*tad atra sarasi snātvā suvarṇa-kaṅkaṇam idaṁ gṛhāṇa tato yāvad asau tad-vacaḥ-pratīto lobhāt saraḥ snātuṁ praviṣṭaḥ, tāvan mahā-paṅke nimagnaḥ palāyitum akṣamaḥ taṁ paṅke patitaṁ dṛṣṭvā vyāghro 'vadat-ahaha mahā-paṅke patito 'si atas tvām aham utthāpayāmi ity uktvā śanaiḥ śanair upagamya tena vyāghreṇa dhṛtaḥ sa pāntho 'cintayat*

**“Therefore, after bathing in this pond, accept this bracelet in charity.” Hearing the tiger’s words, the traveler, overcome by greed, believed him. Upon entering the pond, he got stuck in deep mud and was unable to move. Seeing him in that condition, the tiger said, “O my god, you are stuck in the mud! Wait, wait, I will help you.”**  
**The tiger slowly came near the traveler and caught him. Then the traveler began reflecting....**

According to religious scriptures, charity should be given and accepted when one is in a clean state. Thus meticulously adhering to religious principles, the tiger asked the traveler to bathe before accepting the offering. Like the tiger, a high-class imposter never admits he is a cheater. Even when the traveler was stuck in the mud, the tiger did not prematurely show his glee, but continued to pose as a well-wisher of the hapless traveler.

Another point to be noted is that the traveler realized his mistake as soon as he got stuck. Greedy men generally realize their mistakes at the last moment. At that time their intelligence is uncovered, but alas, it is too late. After accepting initiation from a bogus guru, a disciple may realize his mistake, but it is rare that he can get free from his clutches. The guru will promise him help, but instill in him the fear that if he harbors doubts about his guru, it is a great offence. Thus the hapless disciple will remain ever confused. The tiger will kill the traveler and end his misery, while the disciple described above will slowly suffocate.

### Text 17

***na dharma-śāstraṁ paṭhatīti kāraṇaṁ***  
***na cāpi vedādhyayanaṁ durātmanaḥ***  
***svabhāva evātra tathātiricyate***  
***yathā prakṛtyā madhuraṁ gavāṁ payaḥ***

**Перевод:**

**Just as cow’s milk by nature is always sweet, an evil person’s nature will dominate, though he studies religious scriptures. Hence one should not put faith in him.**

**Комментарий:**

Lord Kṛṣṇa explains that it is difficult for one to transcend his nature (Bg. 3. 33):

*sadṛśaṁ ceṣṭate svasyāḥ*  
*prakṛter jñānavān api*  
*prakṛtiṁ yānti bhūtāni*  
*nigrahaḥ kiṁ kariṣyati*

**Even a man of knowledge acts according to his own nature, for everyone follows the nature he has acquired from the three modes. What can repression accomplish?**

A fortunate person, however, can transcend his acquired nature by taking shelter of the Lord’s pure devotee, who is akincana, without any material motivation. Unfortunately, in Kali-yuga pseudo gurus abound, who lack pure devotion, but, like the tiger, they learn enough scripture to allure and exploit others. While pretending to uplift their disciples, their heart’s desire is to have power over the disciples to enjoy their wealth. Lacking good character, they deceive others to fulfill their own ends.

The taste of fresh cow’s milk is naturally sweet and does not change when kept in a gold pot, silver pot, or glass jar. Similarly, a man’s nature does not change whether he dresses himself in saffron, yellow, white, or decorates himself with sastric knowledge. Even the devil can quote sastra. Although the tiger established himself as being free from greed, in knowledge of the scripture, charitable, compassionate, and so on, it was wrong to place faith in him, because he lacked truthfulness. If a man is untruthful, duplicitous or deceitful, all his religious qualities are nullified. How can a cheater be truthful? Unfortunately, the traveler accepted the tiger’s words at face value without observing his behavior. Overcome by greed, he was carried away by the tiger’s flowery words, which proved to be fatal.

### Text 18

***avaśendriya-cittānāṁ hasti-snānam iva kriyā***  
***durbhagābharaṇa-prāyo jñānaṁ bhāraḥ kriyāṁ vinā***

**Перевод:**

**The religious acts and scriptural study of an uncontrolled person are like the bathing of an elephant. As ornaments worn by a widow arc useless, knowledge possessed by one lacking good behavior is also useless.**

**Комментарий:**

The elephant’s habit is to throw dirt all over its body immediately after taking bath. In the same way, a man with uncontrolled senses engages in sinful activities immediately after performing religious ones. Although pseudo religionists make a show of controlling their senses, they constantly meditate on sense pleasure and secretly break the regulative principles. Lord Kṛṣṇa calls such people mithyacara (Bg. 3. 7).

Sense objects can be enjoyed in two ways — by the external senses or the mind. Both types of enjoyment create harmful impressions (samskaras) on the subtle body. Externally, one may refrain from sense enjoyment out of fear, shyness, or to deceive others. Actual renunciation, however, means to give up enjoyment even within the mind. Since there are no obstacles to mental enjoyment, one who strives to renounce it is a sincere sadhaka, or practitioner. A sincere sadhaka should analyze his dreams, for when one has given up sense enjoyment even in dreams, his mind is considered pure.

In Vedic culture, a woman dresses nicely only to please her husband; if he is away from home, or deceased, she does not dress attractively. A widow is especially expected to wear plain white clothes without ornaments. A widow’s ornaments are a burden as they attract lusty men who will degrade her. Similarly, that knowledge which is preached and not practiced is a burden, meant to entrap the innocent, and will drown both the preacher and his audience in the ocean of birth and death.

*tan mayā bhadraṁ na kṛtam yad atra mārātmake viśvāsaḥ kṛtaḥ tathā coktam —*

**I did not act properly as I put my faith in a ferocious animal. Therefore it is said —**

### Text 19

*nadīnāṁ śastra-pāṇīnāṁ nakhināṁ śṛṅgiṇāṁ tathā*  
*viśvāso naiva kartavyaḥ strīṣu rāja-kuleṣu ca*

**Перевод:**

**One should not put faith in a river, a person with weapons, an animal with claws or horns, a woman, or royalty. (Because their nature is fickle. )**

**Комментарий:**

The recommendation to avoid putting faith in the items listed above must be applied differently. Rivers are known to flood and change course; thus one should not build a house on the bank of a river. Although a river may appear calm, one should net swim across it, as one can get trapped in whirlpools which form suddenly.

One should be careful in the presence of an armed man as he may attack if agitated. Presently, life is taken very cheaply and people casually shoot others for a little money or even for no apparent reason, without consideration of future consequences.

Animals with claws and horns sometimes attack without provocation, so one should keep a safe distance from them. There are many stories of pets and trained animals going wild and killing or maiming their masters or bystanders.

Materialistic women, being absorbed in bodily identification, tend to be ruled by their emotions which cloud their intelligence, leaving them vulnerable to being exploited by unscrupulous men. Desiring the satisfaction of being loved when others find them attractive, they waste much time and money trying to improve their appearance by going to gyms, jogging, spending lavishly to reduce their weight, or by using costly cosmetics to hide their wrinkles. If someone flatters them with romantic words or gifts, they may be seduced, as a child is allured with sweets. To place trust in such a woman can be dangerous.

A Kṛṣṇa conscious woman, however, who has dedicated her life to her spiritual master, who is chaste and self-controlled, can be trusted. She associates with men only for service to Kṛṣṇa, being careful to understand that the real enemy of both the man and woman is lust. Srila Prabhupada engaged many women in preaching, Deity worship, and other devotional activities. Tolerating the urges of the mind and senses, they sacrificed their lives alongside the men, performing austerities to enlighten others in Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Srila Prabhupada once quoted this verse as follows: “Never trust the politician and woman. Of course, when a woman comes to Kṛṣṇa consciousness, that position is different. We are speaking of ordinary woman. So when we study things from a material point of view, these things are to be taken care. But when a man or woman becomes Kṛṣṇa conscious, he or she takes care of herself or himself.”

The word raja-kula, or politician, refers to those who have the absolute power to punish or harass others. One should be careful in dealing with them, because they can kill at slight provocation. The conclusion is that one should not trust those with a fickle mind, be they male, female or animal.

aparaṁ ca — Furthermore,

### Text 20

***sarvasya hi parīkṣyante svabhāvā netare guṇāḥ***  
***atītya hi guṇān sarvān svabhāvo mūrdhni vartate***

**Перевод:**

**Indeed one’s nature overshadows all acquired good and bad qualities. Therefore, one should note a person’s nature — not other qualities (such as education, taking bath, giving charity, and chanting mantras. )**

**Комментарий:**

Our nature or inner characteristics are understood by observing our preferred activities, food, clothes, speech, dealings, and association. Acting according to our nature gives us pleasure, while going against our nature distresses us. Since the soul is pleasure-seeking, we always seek to act within our nature. In conditioned life, however, our true nature, which is pure, unique, and pleasing to everyone, is covered. The nature we display is the one acquired by our past activities (karma) combined with the three modes of material nature, namely goodness, passion, and ignorance.
