Readers unfamiliar with the three modes of nature may consult Bhagavad-gita, wherein Lord Kṛṣṇa explains the fundamentals of their functions. Besides the character traits we carry over from our previous lives, the first few years of life are also crucial in molding our character. By age twenty, our core nature is practically fixed.

The basis of our nature is rooted in our subtle body, consisting of the mind, which is divided into conscious, subconscious, and unconscious parts. The function of the conscious mind is to process the data received through the senses, as well as to create new thoughts, and forget old ones. A major function, however, is supplying reasonable justifications (plausible lies) for the irrational things we do.

The subconscious mind stores impressions created by past actions until a contrary impression is created, and remembers past thoughts when required. It also houses our irrational desires, for which the conscious mind provides us with rationalizations (plausible lies). Because of this, psychologists have concluded that many seemingly insoluble problems could be readily solved, if we accept that our real motivations are not found in the conscious mind, but lie hidden in our subconscious.

Repeated activities and thoughts create deep Samskaras, or impressions, on the subconscious mind, which guide the thoughts of the conscious mind. A person habituated to smoking, for example, being reminded by the subconscious mind, feels the urge to smoke by smelling cigarette smoke. Unconsciously, he’ll reach for a cigarette while reading or talking. In this way, the conscious mind is influenced by the subconscious mind, also called citta, or heart. All our likes and dislikes come from the Samskaras lying in the subconscious mind. Deep impressions due to past experiences create a person’s nature and to change them, one has to attack them with determination, enthusiasm, and patience.

We live, act, think, and feel according to what is in our subconscious, and the conscious mind provides the plausible reasons for such thinking, feeling and so on. Therefore it is sometimes said that what we think determines who we are. If we train ourselves to have benign thoughts, we will display benign characteristics. If we have evil thoughts, we will display demonic natures.

Most people are somewhere inbetween, displaying divine or demonic natures from time to time. One reason for this is that people are not aware of the importance their thoughts play in shaping their character. Many people mistakenly think they are not responsible for the contents of their thoughts, which they believe occur automatically. They think they have no control over what thoughts come into their minds. In this way, they live life as victims of their random thoughts that are largely products of their emotional responses to stimuli from the external or internal world. Such people never achieve their potential for life, either as materialists or spiritualists.

One who has taken up bhakti-yoga is especially unfortunate if he neglects his responsibility to control his thoughts, as he has full opportunity to comprehend the workings of the conscious and subconscious by self-examination. By his daily practice of the symptoms of a sadhu, he can recondition the patterns of his thoughts and develop his saintly nature, eventually leading to a life of transcendence.

Unfortunately, due to inadequate guidance, many on the path of bhakti wistfully hope for improvement without taking responsibility for their lives and applying themselves effortfully to making progress. Many are hoping to progress effortlessly, and that hope is rationalized as surrender, dependency on the mercy of the Lord. This is an example of the plausible lies the conscious mind supplies us, supporting our desire to be lazy.

It is true that under extreme circumstances one may temporarily act in a way contrary to his nature, but that temporary change should not be taken as permanent. For example, one may display artificial humility in adverse circumstances or when seeking favors from a superior. When circumstances return to normal, the change disappears. It is like releasing a stretched rubberband; it naturally returns to its original shape.

Even in cases of prolonged exposure to unusual circumstances, people are often found to temporarily adjust, yet gradually revert to their basic nature. For instance, a disciple who is habituated to some vice may temporarily be rectified by performing personal service to his guru, but after some time he reverts to his old habits, perhaps with a bit more caution. Reconditioning our nature is difficult indeed. Thus Kṛṣṇa says we are forced to act according to our nature, so much so, He asks Arjuna, “What will repression accomplish?” He does not mean that it is impossible, or the Lord would not preach Bhagavad-gita at all; however, He wanted to stress how difficult it is to change one’s nature, that it takes effort. Bhakti-yoga is always described, therefore, as a gradual process (sanair sanair uparamed). This of course does not apply to one who has natural attraction.

To make the matter more complex, we find that it is easier to condition ourselves to bad habits than to good ones. Also, it is generally easier to alter the nature of a child — either for better or worse — than an adult.

Sometimes people develop a temporary mood of renunciation when confronted with distressing family problems, and consequently may join a spiritual organization and steadfastly follow sadhana practices. Since the effect (their distress) is related to the cause, when the cause disappears, so does the effect. Subsequently, as their distress diminishes, so does their mood of renunciation along with their practice of sadhana, which was motivated. Therefore leaders, especially gurus, must carefully study the character of prospective disciples before taking responsibility for them. Those who join a spiritual society on their own accord are more suitable candidates than those who join to avoid social problems or are coerced by circumstances.

Of course, sometimes such people become gurus themselves; therefore, prospective disciples must also scrutinizingly study the guru before approaching him for initiation.

The traveler was duped because he mistook the tiger’s temporary show of good character for his actual nature. Humans have the unique ability to mask their thoughts and behave completely contrary to their real feelings. They can smile when they are furious; they can be vicious behind their masks of affability and congeniality. A good example of this ability is that two people will be in a heated argument, when suddenly the phone rings, and one of the people will answer the phone with the most beguilingly pleasant voice that the caller has no idea of the tense atmosphere in the room. All humans have this capacity to hide their true feelings and even their motives. In this story the tiger represents the human being.

Anyac ca — And again,

### Text 21

***sa hi gagana-vihārī kalmaṣa-dhvaṁsa-kārī***  
***daśa-śata-kara-dhārī jyotiṣāṁ madhya-cārī***  
***vidhur api vidhi-yogād grasyate rāhuṇāsau***  
***likhitam api lalāṭe projjhitaṁ kaḥ samarthaḥ***

**Перевод:**

**The moon, the destroyer of darkness, has a thousand rays and is surrounded by stars, yet by the will of fate it is troubled by Rahu. Therefore, what is written on one’s forehead cannot be changed.**

**Комментарий:**

This verse says that fate is so powerful that despite exalted qualities, such as the moon’s splendor, still its destiny is to be troubled by Rahu. Similarly, according to the science of physiognomy, we have our destiny on our foreheads, and that cannot be changed.

When a person realizes he has made a mistake, therefore, he may console himself by blaming fate. Or he may blame Kali-yuga, the storehouse of all bad qualities. Typically in this age, when people often don't believe in karma or the Vedic version, they blame their parents, teachers, spouses and even their spiritual master for their misfortunes. To disown responsibility symptomizes pettiness of heart. Instead of blaming fate or time, we should take full responsibility for our circumstances and try to solve our problems. Our outlook should be that all the outcomes I have had in my life up to this moment are the result of my choices and decisions in the past. Therefore no one is to blame but myself. Henceforth I will take full responsibility for my choices and decisions.

Surprisingly, our tendency to blame others hints at the unsullied nature of the soul in the natural state. The pure soul is faultless. By identifying with the material body, however, bad qualities appear. In the conditioned state, the soul tries to realize his spiritual characteristics through the material body. For example, the soul is immortal, so in the conditioned state he tries to make the body immortal. Similarly, the soul is flawless and thus in the conditioned state, he tries to deny his mistakes and blame others or fate.

Fate, however, is insentient and cannot be superior to sentient beings, who are endowed with intelligence and the ability to act. Instead of lamenting, the traveler should have tried to escape, since nothing is worse than death.

*iti cintayann evāsau vyāghreṇa dhṛtvā vyāpāditaḥ khāditaś ca ato 'haṁ bravīmi-kaṅkaṇasya tu lobhenety ādi ata eva sarvathāvicāritaṁ karma na kartavyam iti yataḥ — ”*

**As he thought this, the tiger caught, killed, and ate him. Therefore I (Citragriva) say, ‘“Being greedy for the gold bracelet.. One should never act before fully deliberating, for — ”**

### Text 22

***sujīrṇam annaṁ suvicakṣaṇaḥ sutaḥ***  
***suśāsitā strī nṛpatiḥ susevitaḥ***  
***sucintya coktaṁ suvicārya yat kṛtaṁ***  
***sudīrgha-kāle'pi na yāti vikriyām***

**Перевод:**

**These don’t cause harm even after a long time: food well-digested, an intelligent son, an obedient wife, a king faithfully served, words spoken after proper deliberation, and work thoughtfully performed.**

**Комментарий:**

Most diseases result from unwholesome eating, overeating, eating at the wrong time, or bad combining of foods. In Sanskrit, undigested food is called ama and diseases caused by such foods are called amaya. Rich food, overeating and eating without chewing properly are the main causes of indigestion.

One must always think before speaking, as harsh speech can cause distress. Similarly, one should weigh the pros and cons of a particular enterprise before attempting it. One should not act rashly, especially if the action entails a risk to one’s life. As long as one lives, he can strive to achieve the goal of human life. The sixth century poet Bharavi gives the following advice (Kiratarjuniyam 2. 30):

*sahasā vidadhīta na kriyām*  
*avivekaḥ paramāpadāṁ padam*  
*vṛṇute hi vimṛśya kāriṇaṁ*  
*guṇa-lubdhāḥ svayam eva sampadaḥ*

**A man devoid of discrimination should not act rashly, as such action causes great misery. Opulence, which follows good qualities, comes to a person who acts only after careful deliberation.**

An incident from the poet’s own life illustrates the point of his verse. Although Bharavi had poetic genius, he was poverty-stricken. One day his wife became furious about him being always absorbed in poetry and neglecting his family. Bharavi left home by foot to seek his fortune in the royal court. Fatigued by his exhausting walk, he stopped near a scenic lake to rest. There he got inspired and wrote the first line of the above verse on a lotus leaf with his fingernail. By the will of providence the king was out hunting and stopped at the lakeside. He was delighted with the line on the lotus leaf and asked the poet to meet him in the court. However, the palace guard refused Bharavi entry because of his shabby appearance.

The king liked the verse so much that he had it engraved in gold in his bedroom so he could read it every morning upon arising. One day he went hunting and returned late at night a few days later. Upon entering his private chamber, he was astonished to see a young man lying on his bed by the side of the queen. In a rage, he drew his dagger to kill both of them. Just as he was about to thrust the dagger, his eyes caught the glittering golden letters of the verse. He paused and remembered how chaste his wife was and knew she would never sleep with another man, so he woke her up. With great joy, she informed the king that their son who had been stolen some years ago had been brought to the palace that very evening. Seeing how the verse saved the life of his wife and son, he sent for the poet, and later gave him royal honors.

*etad vacanaṁ śrutvā kaścit kapotaḥ sa-darpam āha-āḥ! kim evam ucyate? — ’*

**Hearing these words of Citragriva, one pigeon spoke proudly, “What are you saying?” (He tells the other pigeons not to listen to the words of Citragriva because — )**

### Text 23

***vṛddhasya vacanaṁ grāhyam āpat-kāle hy upasthite***  
***sarvatraivaṁ vicāre ca bhojane'pi pravartatām***

**Перевод:**

**One should take advice from elders only in an emergency. If we consult them in trivial matters, we will not even get food. (Therefore let us feast on the rice).**

**Комментарий:**

Youth are generally impudent, adventurous and like to take risks, ignoring the advice of seniors, considering them old-fashioned, ill- informed, and out of touch. They think, “Why be cautious? Life is meant for enjoying.” Considering themselves more avant-garde, intelligent, and in-the-know, they tend to reject any authority. Unfortunately, out of passion, they fail to discriminate between rashness and boldness.

Adults, on the other hand, are generally wiser owing to experience. They can be true well-wishers of the youth and try to give them kind advice, thereby saving them from the miseries of inexperience. Wisdom lies in learning from others’ experience.

Yatah — Because,

### Text 24

***śaṅkābhiḥ sarvam ākrāntam annaṁ pānaṁ ca bhūtale***  
***pravṛttiḥ kutra kartavyā jīvitavyaṁ kathaṁ na vā***

**Перевод:**

**In eating and drinking on earth there is always some danger. How then should we maintain our lives to perform our duties?**

**Комментарий:**

There is risk in every endeavor. Food may be poisonous or it may cause indigestion. Though most diseases result from food and water, still, no one refrains from eating. Crossing the street is risky, but one must go out to make a living. Yet we should not take foolish risks that surely end in death, which is precisely what the proud young pigeon is about to do, blinded by excessive greed. The greedy traveler had the same thought and chose to accept the bracelet.

An unintelligent person who considers his judgment infallible uses illogical arguments and even quotes scripture to justify his actions. Owing to attachment we cannot see our own defects, nor will we agree with anyone who may point them out. Of course, out of respect for a senior, we may sometimes externally agree, but within ourselves we maintain that our course of action is best. To avoid this syndrome, one should work with faith under the guidance of a bona fide teacher, who will train one to discriminate properly and make wise decisions.

### Text 25

*īrṣyī ghṛṇī tv asantuṣṭaḥ krodhano nitya-śaṅkitaḥ*  
*para-bhāgyopajīvī ca ṣaḍ ete nitya-duḥkhitāḥ*

**Перевод:**

**These six are always miserable: an envious person, one who detests others, a dissatisfied person, one who is irascible, one who is always doubtful, and one who depends on others. (So let us be bold and eat the rice).**

**Комментарий:**

The two previous verses are purva-paksa, or the antithesis, and this verse is being misused to support rash action. An envious person’s heart is always burning as he cannot tolerate another’s progress, aksantir irsya (Amarakosa 1. 7. 24). Always finding someone to envy, he can never be happy. Similarly, one who hates, who is dissatisfied, as well as one who is short-tempered, will suffer continuously. A doubtful person, being indecisive, is unsuccessful in achieving his goals. A person who depends on others cannot be happy as his happiness lies in the hands of others. Depending upon unqualified persons means getting exploited. But if one depends on a qualified person, who is a compassionate well-wisher of his dependents, he can achieve perfection in life.

*etac chrutvā taṇḍul-kaṇa-lobhena nabho-maṇḍalād avatīryas arve kapotās tatropaviṣṭāḥ yataḥ —*

Hearing these words and overcome by greed for grains, the pigeons landed near the rice (and got caught in the net). It is said —

### Text 26

***sumahānty api śāstrāṇi dhārayanto bahu-śrutāḥ***  
***chettāḥ saṁayānāṁ ca kliśyante lobha-mohitāḥ***

**Перевод:**

**Even those who have studied great literature and are thus well-informed and capable of dispelling doubts, also face misery, being bewildered by avarice.**

**Комментарий:**

Some people, though learned in scriptures and capable of giving good advice, make mistakes, as greed covers their intelligence. Modern psychology agrees with the author in this regard. According to Erich Fromm in the Art of Being: “Our reason functions only to the degree to which it is not flooded by greed. The person who is a prisoner of his irrational passions loses the capacity for objectivity and is necessarily at the mercy of his passions; he rationalizes when he believes he is expressing the truth.” As told in Srimad-Bhagavatam, although Sukracarya, was very learned and knew the truth, being overcome by greed, he rationalized in many ways, advising Bali Maharaja not to worship Lord Visnu.

A greedy person, overcome by personal ambition, desires to possess more than he needs. The more we have, the more we want. Greed can sway the minds of even learned men who know better, impelling them to act deviously to fulfill their personal ambitions. And for their hard labor, their only reward is misery. Generally, we rationalize our greed as some virtue.

anyac ca — Moreover it is said —

### Text 27

***lobhāt krodhaḥ prabhavati lobhāt kāmaḥ prajāyate***  
***lobhān mohaś ca nāśaś ca lobhaḥ pāpasya kāraṇam***

**Перевод:**

**From greed comes anger, lust, delusion, and destruction; therefore greed is the root cause of all sin.**

**Комментарий:**

Dhrtarastra and his sons were destroyed owing to greed for the kingdom of the Pandavas. Of all evils, greed is the most difficult to overcome; it is said that when all sins grow old, covetousness stays young. Of the many evils that result from greed, lust is the greatest. When lust is thwarted, anger results, which destroys intelligence and spoils accurate decision-making. To satisfy greed, one generally engages in sinful activities. Born of the mode of passion, greed leads to ignorance and misery, rajasas tu phalam duhkham. Indeed, Lord Kṛṣṇa has described it as a gate to hell (Bg. 16. 21).

Anyac ca — And again —

### Text 28

***asaṁbhavaṁ hema-mṛgasya janma***  
***tathāpi rāmo lulubhe mṛgāya***  
***prāyaḥ samāpanna-vipatti-kāle***  
***dhiyo 'pi puṁsāṁ malinā bhavanti***

**Перевод:**

**Although a golden deer does not exist, still Lord Rama greedily pursued one (in His attempt to please Sita Devi). Indeed, when calamity approaches, one’s intelligence is polluted.**

**Комментарий:**

Lord Rama is completely free from the modes of nature and corresponding inferior characteristics. Srimad-Bhagavatam explains that He descended to instruct human beings by His personal example (Bhag. 5. 19. 5):

*martyāvatāras tv iha martya-śikṣaṇaṁ*  
*rakṣo-vadhāyaiva na kevalaṁ vibhoḥ*  
*kuto 'nyathā syād ramataḥ sva ātmanaḥ*  
*sītā-kṛtāni vyasanānīśvarasya*

**It was ordained that Ravana, the chief of the Raksasas, could not be killed by anyone except a man, and for this reason Lord Ramacandra, the Supreme Personality of Godhead, appeared in the form of a human being. His mission, however, was not only to kill Ravana but to teach mortal beings that material happiness centered around sex life, or one’s wife, is the cause of misery. Lord Rama is the self-sufficient Supreme Personality of Godhead, and nothing is lamentable for Him. Therefore why else could He be subjected to tribulations by the kidnapping of mother Sita?**

While Lord Rama was in exile, Sita became enchanted by a golden deer and requested Him to capture it for her. In reality, the deer was the demon Marici sent by Ravana to distract Lord Rama and Laksmana so that he could abduct Sitadevi. When Ravana’s plan succeeded, Rama, greatly disturbed, went to war to rescue Sita. Although Rama knew that no golden deer existed, still He chased one. The golden deer represents a glittering sense object and Lord Rama played the part of a henpecked husband trying His best to please his wife. This pastime instructs us how avarice covers our intelligence.

*anantaraṁ te sarve jāla-nibaddhā babhūvuḥ, tato yasya vacanāt tatrāvalambitās taṁ sarve tiraskurvanti sma yataḥ.*

**After that, the pigeons were bound in the net. Later they criticized the proud pigeon, who had advised them to eat the rice. Thus it is said —**

### Text 29

***na gaṇasyāgrato gacchet siddhe kārye samaṁ phalam***  
***yadi kārya-vipattiḥ syān mukharas tatra hanyate***

**Перевод:**

**One should not shoulder leadership of an assembly; in success, everyone takes the credit, but in failure, the leader alone is blamed.**

**Комментарий:**

This verse informs us of one disadvantage of accepting leadership — one is bound to be criticized for a small mistake, and when one does something beneficial, hardly anyone acknowledges it. Leaders, therefore, should be prepared to face criticism. Human nature is such that we see faults in others and virtues in ourselves, even when they don’t exist. When there is failure, the tendency is to blame others, especially the leaders; but in success everyone clamors for the credit.

*tasya tiraskāraṁ śrutvā citragrīva uvāca-nāyam asya doṣaḥ, yataḥ —*

**Hearing the proud pigeon being criticized, Citragriva said, “It is not his mistake. (Do not blame him, )” Because —**

### Text 30

***āpadām āpatantīnāṁ hito 'py āyāti hetutām***  
***mātṛ-jaṅghā hi vatsasya stambhī-bhavati bandhane***

**Перевод:**

**At milking time the cow’s leg is used as a post to tie the calf; so for one in difficulty, even favorable things are the cause of distress.**

**Комментарий:**

At milking time the calf is allowed to start nursing and when the cow releases her milk for the calf, it is then pulled away and tied to a post while the milkman takes the bulk of the milk. Later the calf is allowed to drink what remains. Sometimes the front leg of the cow is used as the tying post for the calf. In this way, the cow, who is favorable to the calf, becomes an obstacle to its feeding.

Citragriva now exhibits the qualities of a noble leader. He avoids criticizing or chastising the proud pigeon whose bad advice got them in trouble, knowing that he is already embarrassed and that further criticism will only depress him. A magnanimous leader seeks solutions to problems, whereas one of low character takes pleasure in criticism. The purpose of chastisement or punishment is to make the offender realize his mistake and rectify him — not just to heckle him. The purpose of misery is to rectify the sinner.

### Text 31

***sa bandhur yo vipannānām āpad-uddharaṇa-kṣamaḥ***  
***na tu bhīta-paritrāṇa-vastūpālambha-paṇḍitaḥ***

**Перевод:**

**A friend is one who can uplift an afflicted person from difficulty, not one simply expert in taunting him while feigning protection.**

**Комментарий:**

When someone commits a mistake, people freely advise how the person should have acted, for it is easy to be wise after the event. A real friend, however, does not deride one in difficulty, but lends a helping hand. He knows that to err is human. Some men disguise themselves as friends, but derive subtle pleasure in deriding the afflicted person on the pretext of consolation. Such malicious persons should not be mistaken for friends. Citragriva who is the family head and the well- wisher of the other pigeons forbade them to criticize their proud companion. Constructive criticism is helpful, whereas malicious criticism is self-defeating. Sometimes incisive criticism is taken as malicious, but we must not be superficial in assessing criticism. The criticism has to be weighed. Even criticism offered in malice may have beneficial merit in it.

*vipat-kāle vismaya eva kāpuruṣa-lakṣaṇam tad atra dhairyam avalambya pratīkāraś cintyatām, yataḥ —*

**In times of distress, perplexity is a sign of cowardice. Therefore, be courageous and find a solution. For —**

One’s resolve is tested in times of calamity. Here is the difference between the traveler in the previous story and the pigeons. When faced with danger, the traveler consoled himself by blaming fate and died. For Citragriva it would have been easy to blame the proud pigeon, but that would have been a death sentence for the whole group. Blaming was not a rational solution to the problem at hand; it never is. Instead Citragriva, unshaken by his trying circumstances, exhibits the quality of a noble leader and encourages the pigeons to take heart and be solution-oriented. As we shall see, they will act with fortitude and survive.

### Text 32

***vipadi dhairyam athābhyudaye kṣamā***  
***sadasi vākya-paṭutā yudhi vikramaḥ***  
***yaśasi cābhirucir vyasanaṁ śrutau***  
***prakṛti-siddham idaṁ hi mahātmanām***

**Перевод:**

**These are the natural qualities of the magnanimous: fortitude in adversity, forgiveness in prosperity, eloquence in an assembly, chivalry in battle, taste for fame, and affection for scriptural study.**

**Комментарий:**

It’s easy to be peaceful in times of prosperity, to forgive when one lacks the power to chastise, to be a scholar in the presence of one’s wife, and be a great hero at home. The characteristics mentioned in this verse are found only in great persons.

Once the Kauravas attacked King Virata on two fronts simultaneously and stole his cows. At that time, the king’s youthful son, Uttara Kumara, boasted before the royal ladies that he alone would capture the great Bhisma and Kama and bring cloth from their turbans for his sister Uttara’s dolls. Arjuna, who was living incognito as the eunuch Brhannala, agreed to drive his chariot. When the prince arrived on the battlefield, however, and saw the great Kaurava heroes, he understood his death was imminent. Trembling in fear, he told Brhannala to drive the chariot back to the palace. In response, Brhannala proposed to fight on the prince’s behalf, if he would agree to drive the chariot.

Refusing his driver’s proposal, Uttara Kumara jumped off the chariot and ran. After catching him, Brhannala warned the prince that if he again tried to flee, he would personally shoot him. Then Brhannala (Arjuna), feeling enlivened for the opportunity to fight with great heroes, demonstrated chivalry in battle. Singlehandedly, he defeated all the Kaurava heroes and released King Virata’s cows. Thus he was famous for his heroic deeds.

That the magnanimous have a taste for fame means they are inspired to perform beneficial activities which bring sincere praise. Otherwise, everyone is desirous of name and fame. Vyasanam srutau means attachment for hearing scriptures from authoritative sources. Prakrti- siddham means these qualities exist naturally in the magnanimous. Others can obtain these qualities by associating with great souls, observing their actions, and imbibing their character.

### Text 33

*sampadi yasya na harṣo vipadi viṣādo raṇe ca bhīrutvam*  
*taṁ bhuvana-traya-tilakaṁ janayati jananī sutaṁ viralam*

**Перевод:**

**A mother rarely gives birth to a son, crest jewel of the three worlds, who does not rejoice in opulence, nor become morose in distress, nor fearful in battle.**

**Комментарий:**

People in general are influenced by external circumstances, but great souls remain fixed in mind and are not swayed by external changes. Such a quiescent state is achieved only by devotees whose intelligence is fixed in devotion to the Lord,

*duḥkheṣv anudvigna-manāḥ*  
*sukheṣu vigata-spṛhaḥ*  
*vīta-rāga-bhaya-krodhaḥ*  
*sthita-dhīr munir ucyate*

**One who is not disturbed in mind even amidst the threefold miseries or elated when there is happiness, and who is free from attachment, fear and anger, is called a sage of steady mind. (Bg. 2. 56)**

Anyone can achieve this state by associating with saintly persons. *Anyac ca* — Also

### Text 34

*ṣaḍ-doṣāḥ puruṣeṇeha hātavyā bhūtim icchatā*  
*nidrā tandrā bhayaṁ krodha ālasyaṁ dīrgha-sūtratā*

**Перевод:**

**A person desiring to progress in life should give up the following six defects: oversleeping, laziness, timidity, anger, lack of enthusiasm, and procrastination.**

**Комментарий:**

The defects listed here are all symptoms of the mode of ignorance, tamo-guna. Tandra refers to sluggishness of the body and alasyam to lack of enthusiasm in the mind. Tandra appears when there is insufficient rest, overeating, or exhaustion, and induces one to lie down, whereas alasya induces general apathy. Dirgha-sutrata is akin to alasya and appears when one is forced to act involuntarily. Although lacking a taste for performing his duty, still one does it unwillingly. Nidra, or sleep, is necessary for maintaining the body, so here it implies oversleeping. Anger arises when one’s desires are obstructed, under the threat of being wrongly punished, or when one’s life or position is in danger.

*idānīm api evaṁ kriyatām-sarvair ekacittībhūya jālam ādāya uḍḍīyatām yataḥ —*

**Even in this situation we should act. Let’s take the net and fly away, for...**

### Text 35

*alpānām api vastūnāṁ saṁhatiḥ kārya-sādhikā*  
*tṛṇair guṇatvam āpannair badhyante matta-dantinaḥ*

**Перевод:**

**As straws twisted into a rope can bind even a mad elephant, so the unity of the weak can accomplish great tasks.**

**Комментарий:**

There is strength in unity. A single straw cannot bind a mouse, but when united with others and formed into a rope, it can restrain even an elephant. Similarly, no one can achieve any great task singlehandedly. As mentioned several verses earlier, in this age especially, our strength is in unity. Disunity brings the downfall of a family, a community, or a nation.

The disappearance of the Yadu dynasty shows how disunity causes destruction. The Yadus were the most powerful beings on the planet and could not be subjugated by anyone, yet owing to internal quarrels, they were annihilated. Similarly, a society or organization can be destroyed if the members fight among themselves. Generally, outside enemies cannot destroy an organization, family, or country. Often an outside enemy serves to unify those under attack. Even though Ravana’s army was more powerful than Rama’s, Ravana lost the battle because his brother Vibhisana turned against him, whereas Lord Rama was victorious because the monkeys were united.

### Text 36

*saṁhatiḥ śreyasī puṁsāṁ svakulair alpakair api*  
*tuṣeṇāpi parityaktā na prarohanti taṇḍulāḥ*

**Перевод:**

**As rice without the husk cannot sprout, so union is beneficial even with insignificant family members.**

**Комментарий:**

Unity in the family is vital. Sharing common past karma and having a natural affinity for each other, members of the same family can be more helpful and trustworthy. Even after taking to the path of devotion, one can take support from his family members. Breaking all bonds with a favorable family is dry renunciation and does not help one on the path of devotion. Rupa, Sanatana, and Jiva Gosvamis cooperated together in the same family to build a very strong foundation for the movement of Lord Caitanya.

Sukracarya advises not to create friction with one’s own kinsmen, svajanair na viruddhyeta, (Sukra-niti 3. 53). Enmity within a family brings about destruction, as demonstrated by the Yadus at Prabhasa, and the fratricidal war between the Kauravas and Pandavas at Kuruksetra. Hiranyakasipu conquered the three worlds, but met his end because of enmity with his own son. Ravana also met his demise for having insulted his brother Vibhisana, who then joined the enemy. Often a country is subjugated owing to traitors. India fell to Moghul rule because Jayachandra conspired against his brother-in-law, Prthviraja Chauhan, the Emperor of Delhi. Similarly a spiritual society can be ruined by the quarreling of even insignificant members who can cause great damage by filing law suits for millions of dollars. The author’s example gives strong imagery to convey the importance of unity — if the insignificant husk is removed, the rice will not grow.

*iti vicitya pakṣiṇaḥ sarve jālam ādāya utpatitāḥ anantaraṁ ca vyādhaḥ sudūrāj jālāpahārakāṁs tān avalokya paścād dhāvito 'cintayat —*

**Thinking like this, the birds flew away with the net. From afar, the hunter saw the pigeons flying away and ran after them thinking....**

### Text 37

*saṁhatās tu haranty ete mama jālaṁ vihaṅgamāḥ*  
*yadā tu nipatiṣyanti vaśam eṣyanti me tadā*

**Перевод:**

**United they carry off my net, but when they land they will come under my control.**

**Комментарий:**

*tatas teṣu cakṣur viṣayam atikrānteṣu pakṣiṣu sa vyādho nivṛttaḥ atha lubdhakaṁ nivṛttaṁ dṛṣṭvā kapotā ūcuḥ-svāmin! kim idānīṁ kartum ucitam? citragrīva uvāca —*

**When the birds flew out of sight, the hunter stopped his pursuit. Losing sight of him, the pigeons said, “O master, what should we do now?” Citragriva said...**

The pigeons first solved the most imminent danger, being caught by the hunter. Now they will try to free themselves from the net. When one’s life is in danger, the best policy is to somehow create delay. When Kamsa drew his sword to kill Devaki after hearing the pronouncement that her eighth son would kill him, Vasudeva averted the danger by promising to give each son to Kamsa as he was born. Thus Devaki's life was saved and the danger was avoided. The purport is that one should not give up hope like the traveler caught by the tiger. Where there is a will there is a way.

Another point to be gained from this story is that the positive side of calamity is unity. The pigeons were divided while pondering whether or not to eat the rice, but as calamity struck they all surrendered to Citragriva. Sometimes the Lord creates a miserable situation for His devotees in order to unite them. When the gopis were proud of their good fortune of dancing with Kṛṣṇa, this could have caused a faction. So Kṛṣṇa suddenly disappeared from their sight and they all united for Him.

In a country suffering internal strife, the government sometimes declares war on another country in order to unite the citizens in a common cause. Thus we see that in calamity, people naturally unite against the common enemy.

### Text 38

*mātā mitraṁ pitā ceti svabhāvāt tritayaṁ hitam*  
*kārya-kāraṇataś cānye bhavanti hita-buddhayaḥ*

**Перевод:**

**These three are natural well-wishers: the mother, friend, and father. Others are well-disposed because of some motive.**

**Комментарий:**

Parents are the natural well-wishers of their children, as parental affection is generally unmotivated. The child can never understand the love within the heart of his mother, which is the purest form of love to be found in the material world. In the eyes of the mother, her son or daughter always remains a child. It is said, kuputro jayeta kadacin mata kumata na bhavati, a son may be disobedient and evil, but a mother is never bad. The father also has natural attachment for the child who is like his bodily expansion. It is said: atma vai putra utpannah, one takes birth as one’s son (Bhag. 10. 78. 36)

An actual friend is an unmotivated well-wisher and is very rarely found. Yet the scriptures describe such friendships as common in Vedic times. At present, owing to greed for money and desire for sense pleasure, most friendships are tinged with vested interest. To understand true friendship, we should hear about the exchanges between Kṛṣṇa and His friends like Sudama and Arjuna as narrated in the Vedic literature.

*tan me mitraṁ hiraṇyako nāma mūṣika-rājo gaṇḍakī-tīre citra-vane nivasati so 'smākaṁ pāśāṁś chetsyati ity ālocya sarve hiraṇyaka-vivara-samīpaṁ gatāḥ hiraṇyakaś ca sarvadā apāya-śaṅkayā śata-dvāraṁ vivaraṁ kṛtvā nivasati*

**“My friend Hiranyaka Maharaja, the king of mice, lives in Citravana on the bank of the Gandaki river. He will cut our bonds.” Thinking in this way, they approached the residence of Hiranyaka Maharaja, who was ever apprehensive and thus lived in a hole with hundreds of passages.**

*tato hiraṇyakaḥ kapotāvapāta-bhayāc cakitaḥ tūṣṇīṁ sthitaḥ citragrīva uvāca-sakhe hiraṇyaka! katham asmān na sambhāṣase?tato hiraṇyakas tad-vacanaṁ pratyabhijñāya sa-sambhramaṁ bahir niḥsṛtya abravīt-āḥ! puṇyavān asmi priya-suhṛn me citragrīvaḥ samāyātaḥ — ”*

**Thereafter Hiranyaka, startled by the sound of the pigeons descending, quietly remained in his hole. Citragriva asked, “Hiranyaka my friend, why don’t you speak to us?”**  
**Recognizing the voice of his friend, Hiranyaka rushed out and respectfully said, “O, how fortunate I am, My dear friend Citragriva is here.”**

### Text 39

*yasya mitreṇa sambhāṣo yasya mitreṇa saṁsthitiḥ*  
*yasya mitreṇa saṁlāpas tato nāstīha puṇyavān*

**Перевод:**

**There is no one more fortunate than he who converses with his friend, who lives with his friend, and who has confidential dialogues with his friend.**

**Комментарий:**

Man has invented innumerable gadgets in a vain attempt to lord it over material nature and people are mad to get them. Yet they are frustrated even after obtaining them, for dead objects can never satisfy living beings. On the other hand, one feels immense pleasure in loving relationships which are free from vested interests.

Unfortunately, for many people TV is their best friend. Spending hours in front of it, absorbed in the fantasies of others, they try to fill their vacant lives, to the neglect of personal relationships. In fact, when the TV is broken, they are at a loss what to do with themselves. In such a depersonalized society, how can anyone be happy? Unable to experience the joy of loving personal relationships, they seek satisfaction in unhealthy ways — aberrant sex practices, drugs, alcohol, gambling, or never-ending rounds of entertainment or forms of thrill-seeking, just to distract themselves from the vacuum in their lives.

Neglecting their spiritual nature, people forget that eternal happiness is only possible by establishing a relationship with Lord Kṛṣṇa and His devotees. A human devoid of personal loving relationships is as good as dead, because no amount of striving for sense gratification will bring the happiness we seek; material existence is an unnatural condition of the soul.

When ignorant persons are frustrated in the quest for happiness through sensual enjoyments, they generally opt to renounce the world and seek salvation through impersonal realization. Frustrated to the point of despair with the world of variety, they now decide to annihilate themselves in the nonvariegated impersonal existence. This cannot satisfy the soul’s thirst for happiness, but it generally takes these souls a long time to find out, spanning millions more births in the material world. If, however, they have the good fortune to meet a devotee of the Lord who is elevated enough to plant the seed of bhakti in their hearts, they can change their course once and for all.

Considering all this, to find such a pure devotee in this world, who is engaged in personal loving service to the Supreme Lord, is very rare and most auspicious; it is the height of good fortune.

*atha pāśa-baddhāṁś caitān dṛṣṭvā sa-vismayaḥ kṣaṇaṁ sthitvā uvāca-sakhe! kim etat? citragrīva uvāca-sakhe! asmākaṁ prāktana-janma-karmaṇaḥ phalam etat*

**Seeing the pigeons caught in the net, Hiranyaka was momentarily astonished and said, “My dear friend Citragriva, how did this happen?” Citragriva said, “Dear friend, this is the result of my past sinful activities.”**

When distress comes to a devotee, he takes it as the mercy of the Lord, thinking himself only worthy of more trouble due to his past misdeeds. He does not blame anyone for his suffering; he is free from attachment and detachment and is not angry when his plans are unsuccessful. Once a man angrily rebuked Srila Prabhupada, who tolerated the man and even treated him with kindness. Later, the devotees asked about the man and Prabhupada said that he must have committed some offense to this man in a previous life and now he was getting the reaction.

A devotee understands that nothing can happen without the sanction of the Lord. Therefore he does not hold others directly responsible for actions they take against him. Always expecting the mercy of the Lord, tat te ‘nukampan su-samiksamano, he patiently tolerates the reactions to his past misdeeds and goes on serving Kṛṣṇa in any condition of life with his body, mind, and words. This steadfast service to the Lord despite unfavorable conditions is the real victory over one’s rivals, because their real aim is to distract one from the Lord’s service.

Therefore, steadfast discharge of devotional service is the best course of action in any condition of life. For a devotee who has grasped the message of bhakti, whether Kṛṣṇa’s mercy comes as happiness or distress, makes no difference; it is all. an impetus for advancement in his service to Kṛṣṇa. Of course, this does not mean that he is irresponsible or negligent. He takes steps to alleviate any problem that may arise, for a devotee of the Lord is not impractical or a wishful thinker. A devotee is prajna, an intelligent person, a pragmatist. A realist.

### Text 40

*yasmāc ca yena ca yathā ca yadā ca yac ca*  
*yāvac ca yatra ca śubhāśubham ātma-karma*  
*tasmāc ca tena ca tathā ca tadā ca tac ca*  
*tāvac ca tatra ca vidhātṛ-vaśād upaiti*

**Перевод:**

**Good and bad deeds performed for whatever cause, by whichever means, in whatever way, at whatever time, in whatever amount, and at whatever place, create, by the will of providence, corresponding reactions by that same cause, that same means, that same time, in the same amount, and at that particular place.**

**Комментарий:**

This is the law of nature. In the modern age, people are largely ignorant of the law of karmic retribution, but ignorance of the law does not mean its effect will not act. For example, for thousands of years the law of gravity had not been formulated. Nevertheless apples continued to observe the law by falling to the ground. Indeed, men took advantage of the law, despite not having put it into words and neatly labeling it, because they used to drop boulders on their enemies from the ramparts of forts or castles.

Similarly, the law of karma may not have been sufficiently “proven” to the gross materialists, but the law works inexorably, as described in this verse.

### Text 41

*rāga-śoka-parītāpa-bandhana-vyasanāni ca*  
*ātmāparādha-vṛkṣāṇāṁ phalāny etāni dehinām*

**Перевод:**

**These are the results of man’s evil actions: disease, lamentation, misery, bondage, and troubles.**

**Комментарий:**

One has to suffer or enjoy the results of one’s karma. The Brahma Vaivarta Purana (Prakrti 37. 16) states:

*nābhuktaṁ kṣīyate karma kalpa-koṭi-śatair api*  
*avaśyam eva bhoktavyaṁ kṛtaṁ karma śubhāśubham*

**Karma is never lost or destroyed without delivering its fruits, though they may come after billions of years. Therefore everyone must enjoy or suffer the results of his pious and sinful deeds, since for every action there is a corresponding reaction. Karma never deteriorates or diminishes.**

Only a jivan-mukta, one who is liberated in this body and thus free from false ego, is free from karma. This does not mean, however, that the results of karma will no longer affect his body. The body itself is a product of karma. When we speak of the jivan-mukta being unaffected by karma, we mean that his response to the karmic reactions does not produce either pleasure or pain, a sense of loss or gain. Nor does he react to the karmic results by resorting to mundane activity to increase good or to nullify bad results; therefore no new karma is being generated by the liberated soul. He sees all such actions impacting on his life as the mercy of Kṛṣṇa.

*etac chrutvā hiraṇyakaś citragrīvasya bandhanaṁ chettuṁ satvaram upasarpati tatra citragrīva uvāca-mitra! mā maivaṁ kuru prathamam asmad-āśritānām eteṣāṁ tāvat pāśāṁś chindhi mama pāśaṁ paścāc chetsyasi hiraṇyako 'py āha-aham alpa-śaktiḥ dantāś ca me komalāḥ tad eteṣāṁ pāśāṁś chettuṁ kathaṁ samartho bhavāmi? tat yāvan me dantā na truṭyanti, tāvat tava pāśaṁ chinadmi tad-anantaram apy eteṣāṁ bandhanaṁ yāvat śakyaṁ chetsyāmi citragrīva uvāca-astv evam tathāpi yathā-śakti bandhanam eteṣāṁ khaṇḍaya hiraṇyakenoktam-ātma-parityāgena yadāśritānāṁ parirakṣaṇaṁ tan na nīti-vedināṁ sammatam yataḥ — ”*

**Hearing this, Hiranyaka immediately began cutting the bonds of Citragriva, who said, “Stop, my dear friend. First cut the bonds of the pigeons under my care, then free me.”**  
**Hiranyaka replied, “I am weak, and my teeth are tender. How will I be able to cut the bonds of all these pigeons? Let me free you before my teeth wear out. Later as I am able, I shall free the others.”**  
**Citragriva said, “What you say is fine, but still, first free as many of them as possible.”**  
**Hiranyaka said, “Protection of dependents at the cost of one’s life, is not recommended by moralists, for...”**

Here are the qualities of a true leader. The code of the sea is that in an emergency the ship’s captain must be the last to abandon ship. He must first look to the welfare of all his passengers and crew, who are his dependents. If a ship’s captain abandons ship prematurely, that is a great disgrace for him and he cannot get employed as a captain again, for he is stigmatized for life.

Actually, this verse is informing us that the code of the sea should be the code of leadership in all spheres of human endeavor where there is a leader and dependents. Leaders who let their subordinates take the blame for their blunders, or who leave them in peril are not fit to be called leaders. Here Citragriva was not even directly responsible for the decision that got the pigeons trapped in the hunter’s net, but as we shall see, he’s firm in accepting responsibility and fulfilling the code of leadership. Of course, in the age of quarrel and hypocrisy, Kaliyuga, leaders of this caliber are almost impossible to find.

Although all of the pigeons are in trouble, Citragriva asks Hiranyaka to free his subordinates first, though he himself may remain bound. Hiranyaka will test Citragriva’s integrity by citing verses from artha- sastra, which justify saving himself first. Citragriva will counter with verses from dharma-sastra, which is superior to artha-sastra.

### Text 42

*āpad-arthe dhanaṁ rakṣed dārān rakṣed dhanair api*  
*ātmānaṁ satataṁ rakṣed dārair api dhanair api*

**Перевод:**

**One should keep wealth for an emergency, but should protect one’s wife at the expense of wealth. One should save oneself, however, at the expense of wife and wealth.**

**Комментарий:**

Hiranyaka is testing Citragriva; thus this verse is purva paksa (antithesis). A householder must save money for unexpected calamities. The real foundation of household life, however, is the wife. Na grham grham ity ahur grhini grham ity ucyate, it is not the house, but the wife who is called home. A wife leaves her parents, brothers, and other family members to join her husband, hence it’s his duty to protect her. At marriage, the husband accepts the responsibility to maintain and protect his wife; therefore he is called bharta or pad. It is wise to protect oneself at the expense of wealth, but not at the expense of one’s wife. Lord Rama fought for Sita, and the Pandavas fought for Draupadi.

King Sibi set a good example of how one should protect one’s dependents even at the risk of one’s own life. Once a pigeon, being chased by a hawk, took shelter of Sibi, who assured it protection. As the pigeon was its natural food, the hawk demanded it, saying that he was also under the king’s protection. Sibi refused the hawk and instead offered it flesh equal to the pigeon’s weight from his own body. The king cut flesh from his thigh to equal its weight. The pigeon was heavier, so he cut more flesh and put it on the scale. But no matter how much he cut, it did not equal the weight of the pigeon. Finally, Sibi sat on the scale, offering his whole body to the hawk, rather than withdrawing his protection from the pigeon. At this, the pigeon and hawk revealed their true identities as Dharma and Indra, who had come to test King Sibi, and both lauded his good character.

If Sibi Maharaja could offer his life for protecting a pigeon, then how can one ethically sacrifice his dear wife for the sake of his life? Dependents must be protected even at the cost of one’s own life. Lord Rama promises to protect His surrendered devotees at all costs (Ramayana 6. 10. 33), sakṛd eva prapannāya tavāsmīti ca yācate abhayaṁ sarva-bhūtebhyo dadāmy etad vrataṁ mama. “If a person surrenders to Me just once, by saying ‘I am Yours, ’ I give him protection from all beings. This is my vow.”

Anyac ca — Moreover...

### Text 43

*dharmārtha-kāma-mokṣāṇāṁ prāṇāḥ saṁsthita-hetavaḥ*  
*tān nighnatā kiṁ na hataṁ rakṣatā kiṁ na rakṣitam*

**Перевод:**

**Moreover, life is the basis of religion, economic development, sense gratification and liberation. Therefore if life is lost, what is not lost? But if life is protected, then everything is protected.**

**Комментарий:**

Life is most precious because one can attain everything with it, including liberation. Thus one must protect one’s life at all cost. Yet, for the welfare of others, one can give up his life, as Dadhici Muni gave his body to benefit Indra. Also, for a ksatriya it is glorious to lay down his life on the battlefield, rather than run away. According to the ksatriya code, the infamy of cowardice is worse than death.

On the other hand, if one sacrifices one’s family members to protect himself, he may temporarily gain some artha and kama, but not dharma or moksa. And ultimately, that artha and kama will be lost, and infamy will remain for having abandoned his family. One may, however, renounce his family for the sake of rendering devotional service to the Lord, which is the ultimate goal of human life and is rarely attained. In the realm of artha and kama-niti one can never leave his dependents in peril, for how can one enjoy life by sacrificing his family members?

Arjuna posed the same question, “How could we be happy by killing our own kinsmen?”(Bg 1. 36). Of course in his case, the Kauravas deserved to be killed as they had acted impiously. Thus, before one makes decisions regarding protecting or sacrificing one’s own life or that of a relative, both the motive and the end result must be carefully weighed. However, we shall see that in terms of dharma-niti, morality is determined according to life’s ultimate purpose and not on the basis of temporary gain from artha or kama-niti.

*citragrīva uvāca-sakhe! nītis tāvad īdṛśy eva, kintv aham asmad-āśritānāṁ duḥkhaṁ soḍhuṁ sarvathāsamarthas tenedaṁ bravīmi yataḥ — "*

**Citragriva said, “My dear friend, you have indeed spoken the truth according to polity, but I am unable to tolerate the pain of those who have taken shelter of me. I speak like this, for...”**

Citragriva represents a truly magnanimous leader. Being unselfish, he was not influenced by the arguments of his friend, though they are based on niti. He understands that principles of religion are superior to the moral codes presented by Hiranyaka. Moral codes related to artha and kama aim to fulfill selfish interests for immediate benefit, but dharma-niti aims at the ultimate good of everyone. Now the king of pigeons will speak on the higher principles of niti.

### Text 44

*dhanāni jīvitaṁ caiva parārthe prājña utsṛjet*  
*sannimitte varaṁ tyāgo vināśe niyate sati*

**Перевод:**

**Realizing that destruction of life and wealth is inevitable, an intelligent person offers such assets for the benefit of others.**

**Комментарий:**

Since life and wealth are temporary, the wise use them to achieve permanent benefit. This is a sign of intelligence. Srimad-Bhagavatam explains, pranair arthair dhiya vaca, “It is the duty of every living being to perform welfare activities for the benefit of others with his life, wealth, intelligence and words.” (Bhag. 10. 22. 35)

Being attached to his own wealth and life, one who exploits the lives and wealth of others for selfish gain, without performing welfare work for society, is a fool.

*ayam aparaś cāsādhāraṇo hetuḥ —*

**There is another special reason...**

### Text 45

*jāti-dravya-balānāṁ ca sāmyam eṣāṁ mayā saha*  
*mat-prabhutva-phalaṁ brūhi kadā kiṁ tad bhaviṣyati*

**Перевод:**

**These pigeons are equal to me in class, wealth, and power. Tell me, if I do not protect them, what good is my leadership?**

**Комментарий:**

In terms of species, appearance, physical strength, and bodily needs, Citragriva is equal to the other pigeons. But as their leader, he possesses the exceptional quality of magnanimous character. A qualified leader protects his subjects at the expense of his own life, unlike today’s leaders who promise protection, but only exploit their subjects. Modern leaders amply protect themselves with innumerable bodyguards, yet sacrifice others on the front lines. In Vedic times, the king led the soldiers on the battlefield himself, and would either return victorious or sacrifice his life in battle.

When King Pariksit left his kingdom to fast on the bank of the Ganges, the question was raised, “How can the emperor, whose life is meant for others, sacrifice everything?” In other words, the emperor’s life was not his own, but belonged to the people, since he lived only for their benefit. This is the quality Citragriva is exhibiting.

Anyac ca — Moreover...

### Text 46

*vinā vartanam evaite na tyajanti mamāntikam*  
*tan me prāṇa-vyayenāpi jīvayaitān mamāśritān*

**Перевод:**

**These pigeons receive no reward, yet never leave my company. As they have taken shelter of me, please protect them even at the cost of my life.**

**Комментарий:**

Here Citragriva indicates that he feels even more obligated to these dependents because they have voluntarily taken shelter of him. Not that he has a contract with them and money is involved, and therefore he is obligated. Of course, he would be obligated that way as well, but the point here is that these volunteers had endeared themselves to him. This virtue is not as easy to display when one is part of a larger social system, which by nature of size is more impersonal. Nevertheless the lesson is there that the volunteer worker is not expendable in times of crisis. When the ordinary members of society see the individual is attended to, the society or organization endears itself to the hearts of all. When they see an impersonal dynamic, they know that the negative fate of a colleague today could be their fate tomorrow, and that subtly sets them on the path of closing themselves to the society or organization. Leaders therefore must be careful to keep “the big picture.” The secret to success in this area is to count oneself a servant of those whom one leads. It is the failsafe paradigm for leadership.

kiṁ ca — Moreover...

### Text 47

*māṁsa-mūtra-purīṣāsthi-pūrite'tra kalevare*  
*vinaśvare vihāyāsthāṁ yaśaḥ pālaya mitra me*

**Перевод:**

**My dear friend, disregard this material body which is full of flesh, urine, stool, and bones, and save my reputation by protecting these pigeons.**

**Комментарий:**

In Text 32 it was said that great souls have a taste for fame and here Citragriva is exhibiting that quality. He does not want to become infamous by abandoning his dependents and is willing to lay down his life for the sake of his reputation. This is the laudable character of a leader.

aparaṁ ca paśya — Furthermore...

### Text 48

*yadi nityam anityena nirmalaṁ mala-vāhinā*  
*yaśaḥ kāyena labhyeta tan na labdhaṁ bhaven nu kim*

**Перевод:**

**If permanent unsullied fame is achieved by sacrificing this temporary body, which is filled with stool, then what is not achieved? (i. e. everything is achieved)**

**Комментарий:**

Yatah — For...

### Text 49

*śarīrasya guṇānāṁ ca dūram atyantam antaram*  
*śarīraṁ kṣaṇa-vidhvaṁsi kalpānta-sthāyino guṇāḥ*

**Перевод:**

**There is a gulf of difference between this body and one’s good qualities. The body can be destroyed in a moment, but good qualities exist until the great annihilation.**

**Комментарий:**

The last three verses explained that since the body is temporary, one should not be unnecessarily attached to it. Better to sacrifice it for some good cause. One can degrade himself by misusing the body for enjoyment, or uplift himself by using the body in the service of others. It is true that even materially speaking, if someone uses their life, wealth, intelligence, and words in the service of others, that person is remembered for all posterity. There are numerous statesmen, for example, that made great sacrifices on behalf of their nation, and are honored by generations for their service.

Still more wonderful and glorious is using one’s life, wealth, intelligence, and words to serve the Lord and His devotees, for it yields eternal benefit. This is the most amazing feat. If one perfects devotional service in this life, he acquires all good qualities and his good character will be remembered by posterity. Therefore we see that religious figures are honored more in history than other servants of mankind. The Srimad-Bhagavatam says (Bhag. 7. 6. 1), durlabhaṁ mānuṣaṁ janma tad apy adhruvam arthadam, “The human body is most rarely achieved, and although temporary, it is meaningful because in human life one can perform devotional service. Even a slight amount of sincere devotional service can give one complete perfection.”

*ity ākarṇya hiraṇyakaḥ prahṛṣṭa-manāḥ pulakitaḥ san abravīt-sādhu mitra! sādhu anenāśrita-vātsalyena trailokyasyāpi prabhutvaṁ tvayi yujyate evam uktvā tena sarveṣāṁ kapotānāṁ bandhanāni chinnāni tato hiraṇyakaḥ sarvān sādaraṁ sampūjya āha-sakhe citragrīva! sarvathātra jāla-bandhana-vidhau sati doṣam āśaṅkya ātmani avajñā na kartavyā yataḥ — ”*

**Delighted by these words, with hair standing on end, Hiranyaka said, “Bravo! Well said my friend, well said! Due to your love for your surrendered subjects you deserve sovereignty over the three worlds.” Saying this, he cut the bonds of all the pigeons. Thereafter, Hiranyaka worshiped them with great respect and said, “My dear friend Citragriva, don’t blame yourself for this misfortune, thinking it was caused by your making a wrong decision. Because...”**

### Text 50

*yo 'dhikād yojana-śatān paśyatīhāmiṣaṁ khagaḥ*  
*sa eva prāpta-kālas tu pāśa-bandhaṁ na paśyati*

**Перевод:**

**A vulture can see a carcass eight hundred miles off, but when his life is about to end, he cannot see the snare in front of him.**

**Комментарий:**

Aparam ca — Moreover....

### Text 51

*śaśi-divākarayor graha-pīḍanaṁ*  
*gaja-bhujaṅgamayor api bandhanam*  
*matimatāṁ ca vilokya daridratāṁ*  
*vidhir aho balavān iti me matiḥ*

**Перевод:**

**Alas, seeing the sun and moon eclipsed by Rahu, the elephant and snake ensnared, and the learned impoverished, I consider fate most powerful.**

**Комментарий:**

Although the sun and moon illuminate the whole universe and harm no one, they become eclipsed for no fault of their own. The elephant is strong and the snake deadly, yet they are captured and bound by man. Many learned men live in utter poverty, while many uneducated men have great wealth. As all of the above suffer through no fault of their own, Citragriva should not be morose for having suffered a calamity, because destiny cannot be avoided. When its time has come, despite having incomparable vision, the vulture fails to see the trap before him. Everyone is in the hand of fate. In this way, Hiranyaka, as a true friend, tries to console Citragriva during a time of calamity.

aparaṁ ca — Moreover....

### Text 52

*vyomaikānta-vihāriṇo 'pi vihagāḥ samprāpnuvanty āpadaṁ*  
*badhyante nipuṇair agādha-salilān matsyāḥ samudrād api*  
*durnītaṁ kim ihāsti kiṁ sucaritaṁ kaḥ sthāna-lābhe guṇaḥ*  
*kālo hi vyasana-prasārita-karo gṛhṇāti dūrād api*

**Перевод:**

**Innocent birds high in the sky meet with misery, and fish deep in the ocean are caught by expert men. So what is just and unjust in this material world? And what is the real advantage of being in a safe place? Death stretches its hand of calamity to seize things from afar.**

**Комментарий:**

The last three verses are meant for the despondent. Dejection kills creativity and enthusiasm. Thus it is said that a depressed man is no better than a dead man. Despondency checks one’s spiritual and material progress and fosters physical and psychological diseases. The exalted sage Valmiki says;

*śoko nāśayate dhairyaṁ śoko nāśayate śrutam*  
*śoko nāśayate sarvaṁ nāsti śokasamo ripuḥ*

**Lamentation destroys fortitude and vanquishes knowledge. Indeed it destroys everything. Thus there is no enemy like lamentation.**

One can destroy despondency arising from lamentation only by being fixed in clear understanding. Then one is free from doubt and delusion, and can realize his full capacity for achievement, up to attaining the association of the Lord, sa canantyaya kalpate. Yet, lacking this realization, many become despondent and suffer. Lord Kṛṣṇa spoke the whole Bhagavad-gita just to shake off Arjuna’s despondency. Thus a true friend is one who enlightens us.

Hiranyaka consoles his friend Citragriva who did not err, yet was prone to take the blame, being a good leader. The mouse typically blames fate, which never complains and can withstand any amount of criticism.

*iti prabodhya ātithyaṁ kṛtvā āliṅgya ca tena sampreṣitaś citragrīvo 'pi saparivāro yatheṣṭa-deśān yayau, hiraṇyako 'pi sva-vivaraṁ praviṣṭaḥ.*

**In this way, Hiranyaka enlightened Citragriva, hosted him, and after embracing him, bade him farewell. Citragriva along with his retinue left for their destination and Hiranyaka returned to his hole.**

### Text 53

*yāni kāni ca mitrāṇi kartavyāni śatāni ca*  
*paśya mūṣika-mitreṇa kapotā mukta-bandhanāḥ*

**Перевод:**

**One should make hundreds of friends, great and small. Just see how the pigeons were freed by the help of their friend, the little mouse.**

**Комментарий:**

This chapter entitled Mitralabha, Acquisition of Friends, shows the importance of keeping good friends, even if insignificant. One is indeed fortunate if he finds one good friend who can be counted on in a calamity. One should, therefore, select friends carefully and understand that even if insignificant, they are valuable. This is the theme of this and the next story.

*atha laghu-patanaka-nāmā kākaḥ sarva-vṛttānta-darśī sāścaryam idam āha-aho hiraṇyaka! ślāghyo 'si, ato 'ham api tvayā saha maitrīṁ kartum icchāmi atas tvaṁ māṁ maitryeṇānugrahītum arhasi etac chrutvā hiraṇyako 'pi vivarābhyantarād āha-kas tvam? sa brūte-laghupatanaka-nāmā vāyaso 'ham hiraṇyako vihasyāha-kā tvayā saha maitrī? yataḥ — ”*

**Thereafter, the crow named Laghupatanaka (Swift Flyer), who witnessed the whole episode, spoke with great wonder, “O Hiranyaka, you are praiseworthy. I also want to be your friend. Therefore, be merciful and accept me as your friend.”**  
**Hearing this, Hiranyaka said from within his hole, “Who arc you?” The crow said, “I am the crow, Laghupatanaka.”**  
**Hiranyaka laughingly said, “How can we be friends? Because...”**

### Text 54

*yad yena yujyate loke budhas tat tena yojayet*  
*aham annaṁ bhavān bhoktā kathaṁ prītir bhaviṣyati*

**Перевод:**

**The intelligent combine only those things fit to be united. I am food for you, so how can love exist between us?**

**Комментарий:**

Union or friendship between beings with a common interest is expected. Mice, however, naturally fear crows, who prey on them. Friendship between them, therefore, is not expected. The next verse explains that such friendships end in distress.

Aparam ca — Moreover....

### Text 55

*bhakṣya-bhakṣayoḥ prītir vipatteḥ kāraṇaṁ matam*  
*śṛgālāt pāśabaddho 'sau mṛgaḥ kākena rakṣitaḥ*

**Перевод:**

**Moreover, friendship between the prey and predator is the cause of distress. The deer that was caught in the net because of a jackal, was protected by a crow (his friend).**

**Комментарий:**

*vāyaso 'bravīt--katham etat? hiraṇyakaḥ kathayati —*

**The crow said, “How was that?” Hiranyaka tells the story.**

## Story 2 - The Deer, Crow, and Cheating Jackal

*asti magadha-deśe campakavatī nāma araṇyānī tasyāṁ cirāt mahatā snehena mṛga-kākau nivasataḥ sa ca mṛgaḥ svecchayā bhrāmyan hṛṣṭa-puṣṭāṅgaḥ kenacit śṛgālenāvalokitaḥ taṁ dṛṣṭvā śṛgālo 'cintayat-āḥ! katham etan-māṁsaṁ sulalitaṁ bhakṣayāmi? bhavatu, viśvāsaṁ tāvad utpādayāmi ity ālocya upasṛtyābravīt-mitra! kuśalaṁ te? mṛgeṇoktam-kas tvam? sa brūte-kṣudra-buddhi-nāmā jambuko 'ham atrāraṇye bandhu-hīno mṛtavat ekākī nivasāmi idānīṁ tvāṁ mitram āsādya punaḥ sa-bandhur jīva-lokaṁ praviṣṭo 'smi adhunā tavānucareṇa mayā sarvathā bhavitavyam iti mṛgeṇoktam-evam astu*

**Перевод:**

**In Magadha there was a forest, Campakavati, where a deer and crow lived in great affection for a long time. While wandering carelessely, a jackal spotted the strong, healthy deer and thought, “Oh! How can I eat his wonderful flesh? Let me first take him into my confidence.” The jackal approached the deer and said, “My dear friend, how do you fare?”**  
**“Who are you?” asked the deer.**  
**“I am the jackal, Ksudrabuddhi (Less-intelligent). I live alone in this forest as if dead, being bereft of friends. Now that I have found a friend and relative in you, I have regained my life. By serving you, I will remain happy.”**  
**“So be it,” the deer said.**

*tataḥ paścād astaṁ gate savitari bhagavati marīci-mālini tau mṛgasya vāsa-bhūmiṁ gatau tatra campaka-vṛkṣa-śākhāyāṁ subuddhi-nāmā kāko mṛgasya cira-mitraṁ nivasati tau dṛṣṭvā kāko 'vadat-sakhe citrāṅga! ko 'yaṁ dvitīyaḥ? mṛgo brūte-mitra! akasmād āgantunā saha maitrī na yuktā tan na bhadram ācaritam tathā coktam — ”*

**Перевод:**

**When the sun, wearing a garland of rays, had set, they went to the deer’s residence. On the branch of a Campaka tree there lived a crow named Subuddhi (Genius), who was an old friend of the deer. Upon seeing them, the crow said, “O Citranga (one with beautiful limbs), who is this?”**  
**“He is a jackal who wants to be our friend.”**  
**The crow said, “Dear friend, it is not proper to make friendship with strangers. You have acted improperly. For it is said — ”**

### Text 56

*ajñāta-kula-śīlasya vāso deyo na kasyacit*  
*mārjārasya hi doṣeṇa hato gṛdhro jarad-gavaḥ*

**Перевод:**

**One should not offer residence to a person whose family and character are unknown; by the fault of the cat, the blind vulture Jaradgava was killed.**

**Комментарий:**

Previously the crow advised the deer that one should not make friends with strangers and now he is more clearly defining himself — that one should not offer them shelter. The Vedas enjoin that guests should be treated like God, atithi-devo bhava; yet one must examine the guest’s character. If one discovers that his guest is a thief, he should not be welcomed. To behave religiously toward a cheater is a sign of foolishness, vrajanti te mudha-dhiyah parabhavam bhavanti mayavisu ye na mayinah. Acting irreligiously toward an irreligious person is not irreligious. In this regard, Sri Vidura defines religion (Vidura-niti 5. 7):

*yasminyathā vartate yo manuṣyas*  
*tasmiṁstathā vartitavyaṁ sa dharmaḥ*  
*māyācāro māyayā vartitavyaḥ*  
*sādhvācāraḥ sādhunā pratyudeyaḥ*

**One should behave with a person as the other person behaves with him. This is dharma. With a cheater one should behave like a cheater and with a gentleman, like a gentleman.**

Although Bhisma took a vow to observe lifelong celibacy, he abducted the three daughters of the King of Kasi for his stepbrother to marry. Of the three, Amba had already decided to marry Salva, so Bhisma sent her to Salva’s capital. But since Amba had been touched by Bhisma during her abduction, Salva refused to accept her. She then proposed marriage to Bhisma, who refused because of his vow. As a result, she approached his guru, Parasurama, and persuaded Him to order Bhisma to marry her. Bhisma respectfully explained the details of his vow to Parasurama, who nevertheless insisted he marry Amba. When Bhisma still refused, the enraged Parasurama challenged him to a duel. Though it was improper for Bhisma to fight with his martial arts guru, he accepted the challenge on the grounds that his guru was encouraging him to act irreligiously. He told Parasurama (MB. Udyoga Parva 178. 53):

*yo yatha vartate yasmims tasminneva pravartayan*  
*nadharmam samavapnoti na casreyas ca vindati*

**One neither commits sin nor achieves undesirable ends by reciprocating the actions of others.**

Of course, this should not be taken as the highest principle. A person in the renounced order of life does not behave in this manner. Also, one surrendered to a bona fide spiritual master will do anything to please him, even if it involves breaking a principle of dharma. Of course, the guru must be qualified. In Kali-yuga, a bona fide guru is difficult to find.

*tau āhatuḥ--katham etat? kākaḥ kathayati-.*

**Both of them said, “How is that?”**  
**The crow began...**

## Story 3

*asti bhāgīrathī-tīre gṛdhrakūṭa-nāmni parvate mahān parkaṭī-vṛkṣaḥ tasya koṭare daiva-durvipākāt galita-nakha-nayano jaradgava-nāmā gṛdhraḥ prativasati atha kṛpayā taj-jīvanāya tad-vṛkṣa-vāsinaḥ pakṣiṇaḥ svāhārāt kiṁcit kiṁcid uddhṛtya tasmai dadati, tenāsau jīvati, teṣāṁ śāvaka-rakṣāṁ ca karoti atha kadācit dīrghakarṇa-nāmā mārjāraḥ pakṣi-śāvakān bhakṣayituṁ tatrāgataḥ tatas tam āyāntaṁ dṛṣṭvā pakṣi-śāvakair bhayārtaiḥ kolāhalaḥ kṛtaḥ tac chrutvā jaradgavena uktam-ko 'yam āyāti? dīrghakarṇo gṛdhram avalokya sa-bhayam āha-hā hato 'smi yato 'yaṁ māṁ vyāpādayiṣyati*

**Перевод:**

**On Grdhrakuta mountain, on the bank of the Ganges, there was a big parkati tree. In its hollow lived a vulture, Jaradgava, who, owing to misfortune, had no claws or eyes. Out of mercy, the birds residing in that tree saved a portion of their food for him. In exchange, Jaradgava guarded the young birds.**  
**Once a cat named Dirghakarna (Long-eared) came to eat the fledglings. Seeing him approaching, the baby birds cried out in fear. Jaradgava called out, “Who goes there?”**  
**Seeing the vulture, Dirghakarna became fearful and thought, “Oh, now I am dead. He will tear me into pieces.”**

### Text 57

*tāvad bhayasya bhetavyaṁ yāvad bhayam anāgatam*  
*āgataṁ tu bhayaṁ vīkṣya naraḥ kuryād yathocitam*

**Перевод:**

**One should dread danger before it comes, but once it arrives, one should counter it with fitting action.**

**Комментарий:**

Before embarking on a venture, one should determine if danger is imminent. Then one should either plan to counter it, or abandon the venture. Fear in the face of danger gives strength to the enemy. Therefore, with bold resolve one should calmly devise a means to overcome the danger. When the cat realized the vulture could kill him, he cleverly sought a solution, rather than resign himself to fate. In contrast, when the tiger approached the traveler, he accepted his fate and died.

*adhunātisannidhāne palāyitum akṣamaḥ tad yathā bhavitavyaṁ tathā bhavatu, tāvat viśvāsam utpādyāsya samīpam upagacchāmīty ālocya tam upasṛtyābravīt-ārya! tvām abhivande gṛdhro 'vadat-kas tvam? so 'vadat-mārjāro 'ham gṛdhro brūte-dūram apasara no cet hantavyo 'si mayā mārjāro 'vadat-śrūyatāṁ tāvat mad-vacanam tato yady ahaṁ vadhyas tadā hantavyaḥ yataḥ*

**“As I am too close to him, I cannot flee. So let me gain his confidence and go near him. Let whatever happens, happen.” Thinking like this, the cat approached him and said, “Respected sir, I salute you.”**  
**“Who are you?” asked the vulture.**  
**“I am a cat,”**  
**“Then be gone or you’ll be dead.”**  
**“Please hear my words and then if I deserve to be killed, you can kill me, for...”**

### Text 58

*jāti-mātreṇa kiṁ kaścid vadhyate pūjyate kvacit*  
*vyavahāraṁ parijñāya vadhyaḥ pūjyo 'thavā bhavet*

**Перевод:**

**Is one killed or worshiped merely because he belongs to a particular species or caste? One’s conduct alone should determine whether one is to be honored or killed.**

**Комментарий:**

Although there is truth in this statement, one should know that ultimately, one’s real nature will prevail. Good behavior can be feigned. Duplicitous men may cite scriptures or act religiously to exploit simple- hearted people, especially in holy places. A common man has practically no understanding of sastra. Thus his religious sentiment can easily be exploited by unscrupulous men.

Therefore it is the first duty of the sincere devotees of the Lord to expose unscrupulous rascals, who use the sastra to exploit the people in general. Learned devotees must also teach the public to discriminate for themselves between the truth and illusion. To instruct the general populace in the correct conclusions of the sastra, is the highest welfare work, para upkara. The human form of life is meant for such welfare activities. The Bhagavatam states:

*etāvaj janma-sāphalyaṁ*  
*dehinām iha dehiṣu*  
*prāṇair arthair dhiyā vācā*  
*śreya-ācaraṇaṁ sadā*

**It is the duty of every living being to perform welfare activities for the benefit of others with his life, wealth, intelligence, and words.**

Kali-yuga is the age of hypocrisy. One must be especially careful to look out for the sly species. This verse of the Bhagavatam clearly directs the sincere devotees to preach pure Kṛṣṇa consciousness selflessly and live their lives as offerings in the service of others. The best service is to enable others to distinguish reality from illusion. This unscrupulous cat, Dirghakarna, will do all in his power to deceive the blind vulture, who represents the gullible public, so it can fulfill its evil intentions.

*gṛdhro brūte-brūhi kim artham āgato 'si? so 'vadat-aham atra gaṅgā-tīre nitya-snāyī nirāmiṣāśī brahmacārī cāndrāyaṇa-vratam ācaraṁs tiṣṭhāmi yuṣmān dharma-jñāna-ratāḥ prema-viśvāsa-bhūmayaḥ iti pakṣiṇaḥ sarve sarvadā mamāgre prastuvanti, ato bhavadbhyo vidyāvayo-vṛddhebhyo dharmaṁ śrotum ihāgataḥ bhavantaś caitādṛśā dharmajñāḥ, yan mām atithiṁ hantum udyatāḥ? gṛhastha-dharmaś ca eṣaḥ — ”*

**“What brings you here?”the vulture asked.**  
**“I live on the bank of the Ganges, where I bathe daily. I am vegetarian, observe celibacy, and follow the candrayana-vrata. All birds inform me that you are a very religious, knowledgeable, loving, and trustworthy saint. Thus I have come here to hear religious principles from you, who are advanced in knowledge and years. What kind of religious scholar are you that you are ready to kill a guest? Householders should follow these principles....”**

At first the vulture was enraged at the cat, but his heart softened upon hearing the cat’s eloquent words. A gentleman does not boast of his good character, nor find fault with others, unless they are his dependents. But a cheater does just the opposite; he advertises his good character and wonderful deeds and points out defects in others. He shows expertise in lecturing on religious principles, regardless of the interest and qualification of the audience. This is not done to enlighten the audience, but to impress them with himself so he can bring them under his sway.

Here is the catch in the cat’s presentation: Those who do not follow religious principles have no right to instruct others; and even if qualified to give discourses, one should not do so unless asked. And why should the cat, who is a guest, preach to his host? The cat has offered no proof of his being vegetarian and so on, yet he has quickly exposed the vulture’s violation of etiquette in receiving guests. On the other hand, he flatters the vulture, portraying him as a learned sage, exploiting the bird’s weakness for praise. It is said that even the demigods are pleased by hearing praise, then what to speak of humans, stutya vasam yanti deva hy anjasa kim punar narah (Sukra Niti 3. 305).

Seasoned cheaters capitalize on this weakness. One of the signs of a true gentleman is that as soon as he is praised inappropriately he will rectify the speaker. When Lord Caitanya, in the mood of a devotee, was being praised as the Supreme Lord, he covered his ears. Similarly, when Narada glorified Lord Brahma as the supreme, the creator of the universe, he immediately corrected him.

Though the vulture knows he is not a scholar, he is quite pleased if someone takes him as one. This is the cheating propensity. Ironically, this attachment to flattery becomes the weakness through which he can be manipulated. Lord Caitanya Mahaprabhu indicated four things that one must not become attached to in this world — money, followers, women, and praise. One can be controlled either by these attachments or through them. Jaradgava should have sincerely chastised the cat for speaking flowery words, because overpraise is another form of criticism, atistutirninda.

After hailing the vulture as a learned authority on religion, the cat speaks sarcastically. To avoid embarassment, the vulture will receive the cat nicely, then forget his duty to protect the fledgings, and thus fall right into Dirghakarna’s plan.

### Text 59

*arāv apy ucitaṁ kāryam ātithyaṁ gṛham āgate*  
*chettum apy āgate chāyāṁ nopasaṁharate drumaḥ*

**Перевод:**

**Even if an enemy comes to one’s home, he should be treated as a guest; trees do not refuse shade to the woodcutter.**

**Комментарий:**

It is a householders duty to host a guest even if he is an enemy, as long as he behaves like a guest. If an enemy intends to kill the host, it is foolish to treat him as a guest. Such a guest is called atatayi and should be killed without a second thought. Atatayinam ayantam hanyad evavicarayan (MB Bhisma Parva 92. 26). If one spares the enemy, his fate may be like that of the tree who shades the woodcutter.

Being cheated by a rogue is not a symptom of religiosity, but of utter foolishness. Although unqualified, cheaters expertly use the scriptures to dupe pious men who are supposedly learned. Since they quote Niti-sastra to achieve their ends, saintly people conclude that niti is only for politicians and rogues, and not for them. Lord Kṛṣṇa expertly demonstrated how to use niti properly, by marrying Subhadra to Arjuna without causing a rift in the family. He says, nitir asmi jigisatam, “Of those desiring victory, I am morality.” Therefore saintly persons should not shun niti, especially when dealing with cheaters. Although cheaters may misuse niti, it is an indispensable tool to deal with the abundant cheaters in Kali-yuga.

*kiṁ ca-yadi annaṁ nāsti, tadā suprītenāpi vacasā tāvad atithiḥ pūjya eva.*

**Moreover, if one does not have food in the house, he should at least receive a guest with sweet words, for...**

### Text 60

*tṛṇāni bhūmir udakaṁ vāk caturthī ca sūnṛtā*  
*etāny api satāṁ gehe nocchidyante kadācana*

**Перевод:**

**These four arc always available in the homes of saints: a seat made of grass, a sitting place, water, and sweet words.**

**Комментарий:**

Kinca — Moreover,

### Text 61

*bālo vā yadi vā vṛddho yuvā vā gṛham āgataḥ*  
*tasya pūjā vidhātavyā sarvasyābhyāgato guruḥ*

**Перевод:**

**If a child, an old man, or a youth comes to one’s home, he should be worshiped; a guest must be honored by all.**

**Комментарий:**

aparaṁ ca — Also...

### Text 62

*nirguṇeṣv api sattveṣu dayāṁ kurvanti sādhavaḥ*  
*na hi saṁharate jyotsnāṁ candraś cāṇḍāla-veśmanaḥ*

**Перевод:**
