**Just as the moon does not withhold its light from the house of a dog eater, saintly persons bestow their mercy even on those who lack good qualities.**

**Комментарий:**

Anyac ca — Again...

### Text 63

*atithir yasya bhagnāśo gṛhāt pratinivartate*  
*sa dattvā duṣkṛtaṁ tasmai puṇyam ādāya gacchati*

**Перевод:**

**If a guest leaves a home dissatisfied, he takes with him the host’s piety and leaves his sinful reactions behind.**

**Комментарий:**

anyac ca — Furthermore,

### Text 64

*uttamasyāpi varṇasya nīco 'pi gṛham āgataḥ*  
*pūjanīyo yathā-yogyaṁ sarva-deva-mayo 'tithiḥ*

**Перевод:**

**Even if one of a lower class visits the home of a higher class person, he should be received appropriately, for a guest is the embodiment of all the demigods.**

**Комментарий:**

By citing six Puranic verses, the clever Dirghakarna has authoritatively established the importance of receiving a guest. Earlier he insured Jaradgava’s compliance by praising his piety and expertise in scriptural knowledge. If the old vulture disagrees or raises a doubt, his ignorance may be exposed. Out of insincerity, therefore, he remained silent, indicating that he is conversant with the principles of hosting guests.

Yet the cat may think, “With your great learning, why did you mistreat me in the first place?” To clarify this doubt and maintain the cat’s faith in him, the vulture will claim that he was being dutiful by threatening the cat. By making the vulture feel embarassed, the cat has gained the upper hand.

*gṛdhro 'vadat-mārjāro hi māṁsa-ruciḥ pakṣi-śāvakāś cātra nivasanti tenāham eva bravīmi tac chrutvā mārjāro bhūmiṁ spṛṣṭvā karṇau spṛśati, brūte ca-mayā dharma-śāstraṁ śrutvā vīta-rāgenedaṁ duṣkaraṁ vrataṁ cāndrāyaṇam adhyavasitam yataḥ parasparaṁ vivadamānānām api dharma-śāstrāṇām ahiṁsā paramo dharmaḥ ity atraikamatyam yataḥ*

**The vulture said, “Cats eat flesh and as there are many fledglings here, I spoke in that way.”**  
**Hearing this, the cat touched the ground and then his ears (a gesture to indicate his innocence) and said, “I have heard religious scriptures, overcome passion, and followed the most difficult vow of candrayana. Though various scriptures present different opinions on religious principles, they unanimously accept that nonviolence is the supreme religion. For — ”**

Candrayana is a difficult vow prescribed in Smrti Sastras wherein one controls his diet according to the waxing and waning of the moon. On the full moon day one eats fifteen mouthfuls of food and then decreases the amount by one mouthful each day as the moon wanes, until the dark moon day when one fasts completely. As the moon waxes, he increases his daily intake by one mouthful until he again eats fifteen on the full moon day. In this way, the vow is completed in one month. As this vrata requires one to be vegetarian, the cat has proved his superiority to the vulture, who has neither performed candrayana vrata, nor is vegetarian. Besides, the vulture has committed an offense by speaking harshly to a saintly guest, who has come to take holy association. Actually, the cat’s real motive is to eat the fledglings. Thus he lectured on the importance of receiving guests, and next will establish the importance of nonviolence.

In this way, imposters remove doubts in those they want to exploit. All religions have their differences, but also share some common principles such as nonviolence. The cat indicates that since he is religious and a vegetarian, nonviolence should be assumed. Thus the vulture need not fear for the lives of the young birds. Jaradgava’s reasoning is like this: “If the cat was irreligious, why would he even come to associate with such a religious person as me? And, how could he speak on religious principles?”

The vulture’s psychology is very simple. His blindness symbolizes the gullibility of the public to be swindled by the plausible lies and flattery of the unscrupulous. People who are intellectually blind will reason simplistically like Jaradgava and get cheated wholesale. But the same people will believe that this simplicity is high thinking. No doubt Dirghakarna will prove a better psychologist than Jaradgava.

Although the vulture cannot verify the cat’s claims, out of fear of committing an offense to the “saintly” cat, and fear of being revealed as a fool, he goes along with the cat’s reasoning. Incrementally, the cat wins his confidence and that is usually how seasoned deceivers work.

### Text 65

*sarva-hiṁsā-nivṛttā ye narāḥ sarva-sahāś ca ye*  
*sarvasyāśraya-bhūtāś ca te narāḥ svarga-gāminaḥ*

**Перевод:**

**Those who are free from violence, who arc fully tolerant, who offer shelter to everyone, will certainly attain the heavens.**

**Комментарий:**

Anyac ca — Furthermore.

### Text 66

*eka eva suhṛd dharmo nidhane'py anuyāti yaḥ*  
*śarīreṇa samaṁ nāśaṁ sarvam anyad hi gacchati*

**Перевод:**

**The results of religious deeds are the true friends; they follow one even after death. All else is destroyed along with the body.**

**Комментарий:**

Anyac ca — Moreover,

### Text 67

*yo 'tti yasya yadā māṁsam ubhayoḥ paśyatāntaram*  
*ekasya kṣaṇikā prītir anyaḥ prāṇair vimucyate*

**Перевод:**

**Just see the great difference between predator and prey. The predator momentarily enjoys flesh, while the other loses his life.**

**Комментарий:**

Api ca — Moreover

### Text 68

*martavyam iti yad duḥkhaṁ puruṣasyopajāyate*  
*śakyas tenānumānena paro 'pi parirakṣitum*

**Перевод:**

**As one feels agony anticipating imminent death, one should empathize with the agony of others and refrain from killing.**

**Комментарий:**

śṛṇu punaḥ — Listen again:

### Text 69

*svacchanda-vana-jātena śākenāpi prapūryate*  
*asya dagdhodarasyārthe kaḥ kuryāt pātakaṁ mahat*

**Перевод:**

**One can fill his belly with grass and leaves found naturally in the forest. What need is there to commit sin just to stuff the wretched stomach?**

**Комментарий:**

The preceding verses depict how sly persons use scriptural knowledge to fulfill some ulterior motive. Even the devil can quote scripture, but it is always to serve his own ends. While citing verses stressing vegetarianism to dispel the vulture’s doubts, he intends to devour the flesh of the young birds. The moral is that one’s character cannot be accurately assessed simply by hearing his speech, as even actors devoid of saintly character can imitate religious speech. One must carefully note a saint’s conduct.

Deceived by pious-sounding speech, both the traveler in the earlier story and the vulture in this story made the same mistake. In the first instance, the cheating tiger took the role of a bogus guru to entice fools into his trap, and in the second instance, the sly cat takes on the role of a cheating disciple, making a fool out of the unqualified person posing as guru.

Unqualified gurus sometimes understand that a disciple is smarter than they are, and the clever disciple knows this. Still, the disciple behaves in such a way that his guru can continue making a display. Feeling apprehensive to instruct and guide such a disciple, the guru praises him and gives him a free hand, so the disciple does not reject him. Taking advantage of his guru’s weakness, the clever disciple cheats the guru and exploits other less intelligent disciples. The guru, like the blind vulture, cannot protect his disciples, who are like the dependent baby birds. Although such a guru may be happy hearing insincere praise, such flattery leads to the guru’s downfall.

*evaṁ viśvāsya sa mārjāras taru-koṭare sthitaḥ tato dineṣu gacchatsu asau pakṣi-śāvakān ākramya sva-koṭaram ānīya pratyahaṁ khādati atha yeṣām apatyāni khāditāni taiḥ śokārtair vilapadbhir itas tato jijñāsā samārabdhā tat parijñāya mārjāraḥ koṭarān niḥsṛtya bahiḥ palāyitaḥ paścāt pakṣibhir itas tato nirūpayadbhis tatra taru-koṭare śāvakāḥ khāditā iti sarvaiḥ pakṣibhir niścitya ca gṛdhro vyāpāditaḥ ato 'haṁ bravīmi-ajñāta-kula-śīlasya ity ādi*

**In this way, having won the vulture's confidence, the cat began living in the hollow of the tree. Daily he brought a few baby birds to his den and ate them. Thus the parent birds lamented and began searching for their offspring. Knowing the search was on, the cat fled. The parent birds' search led them to find the bones of their young in the vulture’s hollow. Concluding that the vulture had eaten their babies, they killed him. Therefore I say, ajnata-kula-silasya. etc.**

The intricacies of religious principles are very difficult to understand. Even greatly learned people become bewildered during trying situations. Kim karma kim akarmeti kavayo’py atra mohitah. Those who try to understand religion and philosophy by mundane logic, knowingly or unknowingly, will always remain bewildered. Different sages speak differently, nasav rsir yasya matam na bhinnam, and we may even find seeming contradictions in the words of one saintly person. Furthermore, different Puranas seem to glorify different deities as supreme.

With all these seeming contradictions, the only way to understand the scriptures properly is to approach a bona fide spiritual master, tad viddhi pranipatena pariprasnena sevaya. There is absolutely no alternative to this process. A bona fide guru is one who has been a bona fide disciple studying under and serving a bona fide guru. He not only preaches, but practices the principles.

If one chooses an unqualified guru, then he is doomed. In this regard Sri Sukadeva gives the following invaluable advice:

*teṣāṁ kupatha-deṣṭṝṇāṁ*  
*patatāṁ tamasi hy adhaḥ*  
*ye śraddadhyur vacas te vai*  
*majjanty aśma-plavā iva*

**Leaders who have fallen into ignorance and who mislead people by directing them to the path of destruction are, in effect, boarding a stone boat, and so too are those who blindly follow them. A stone boat would be unable to float and would sink in the water with its passengers. Similarly, those who mislead people go to hell, and their followers go with them. (Bhag. 6. 7. 14)**

When one is attracted to the spiritual path, he invariably hears about the importance of accepting a bona fide guru, without the least idea what “bona fide” means. Feeling the pressure to surrender to a guru, he is easy prey for those with vested interests posing as well-wishers. Just as a girl’s husband should be carefully chosen, one must be extremely careful in accepting a guru. If one surrenders under pressure or is allured by some material benefit, his spiritual life is ruined. Better no guru than an unqualified one. Like the tiger and the cat, there are many gurus lacking faith and realization of the sastra, yet are expert in preaching to others. As stated later, “To be expert in giving instruction to others is easy for anyone, but only a great soul can actually follow his prescribed duties.” (Hitopadesa 107).

*ity ākarṇya sa jambukaḥ sa-kopam āha-mṛgasya prathama-darśana-dine bhavān api ajñāta-kula-śīla eva āsīt tat kathaṁ bhavatā saha etasya snehānuvṛttir uttarottaraṁ vardhate?”*

**Hearing this, the jackal angrily spoke (to the crow), “Upon your first meeting with the deer, you too were a person of unknown family and disposition. How then has his affection toward you increased day by day?”**

Now we return to the story of the deer, crow, and jackal. The crow advised that one not give shelter to a person of unknown family and character, using the narration of the cat and vulture. Now the sly jackal points out that when the deer and crow first met they were unknown to each other, but nevertheless they became good friends, so why can’t the jackal and deer be friends?

The jackal has twisted the crow’s statement, which was that one not give shelter or live with an unknown person, to one should not make friends with an unknown person (Text 56). The crow is not opposed to two persons becoming friends; he is opposed to persons taking up residence together before they know each other. In nyaya, the jackal’s defective maneuver is called arthantara-nigraha sthana, which means to digress from the topic being discussed, and is grounds for defeat. Although the deer and crow were unknown to each other at first meeting, they didn’t begin living together until after some time. Moreover, they are not natural enemies, whereas the jackal and deer are.

### Text 70

*yatra vidvaj-jano nāsti ślāghyas tatrālpadhīr api*  
*nirasta-pādape deśe eraṇḍo 'pi drumāyate*

**Перевод:**

**Just as in a land of no trees, the castor oil plant is considered great, so in a place devoid of scholars, a man of petty knowledge is lauded.**

**Комментарий:**

Though the crow has offered sound advice, the jackal is insinuating that he is not wise. In the same way, men of little knowledge criticize real scholars. The modern jackals pass for pure devotees or incarnations and they refer to real saints as bewildered fools. And the masses, lacking the ability to discriminate properly, cannot tell the real from the fake. They cannot understand things on their own merit. They rely on others to direct them what to think. Their sole measure of “truth” is to see who has the best social standing or reputation. The fact that their standing or reputation may be irrelevant, coming from some other field, does not concern the ordinary person. Therefore the views of rock stars, for example, are sought on a wide variety of issues, not because the musicians are knowledgeable or competent in those fields, but because they are celebrities. Every society has its equivalent of this kind of unsound reasoning in Kali-yuga. One has to understand the principle and apply it in all fields of activity.

Anyac ca — Again,

### Text 71

*ayaṁ nijaḥ paro veti gaṇanā laghu-cetasām*  
*udāra-caritānāṁ tu vasudhaiva kuṭumbakam*

**Перевод:**

**Petty minded people say, “This is mine and that belongs to another,” but to the generous, the whole world is his family.**

**Комментарий:**

Here the jackal effectively prevents the crow from speaking, by showing that the crow can pass as a scholar only among fools. Having already gained the confidence of the deer by flattery and false humility, he dares rebuke the deer’s old friend. He’s sowing the seed of division.

Selfish persons are attached to the gross body, kith and kin. The more self-centered a person is, the more dangerous he is for society and himself as well. His whole world is made up of friends and enemies. To serve his selfish schemes he has to divide and conquer, as the jackal is attempting to do here.

In contrast to the selfish, are the selfless saintly persons. They see all as belonging to one family, as children of God. They work not to divide humanity, but to unite it. The magnanimous work not only to uplift their kinsmen, but to benefit the whole world. Because such God conscious persons do not have a separatist mentality, they help bring peace in society.

After marriage it sometimes happens that the love between the newlywed son and his parents, brothers, and friends diminishes, because the man gets attached to the sweet words of his new wife. Similarly, when persons on the spiritual path become famous, being surrounded by many followers, they tend to forget their old friends, who may not have attained such a position. Being attached to position, fame and praise, they sometimes expect their own godbrothers to worship them as their disciples. Preferring to surround themselves with sycophants who praise and worship them, they can never understand the reality of their position. This drama is being played out here in the story of the jackal, crow, and deer.

Although the sycophant jackal appears to be service-motivated and submissive, his real motive is to exploit, but the deer thinks the jackal more concerned about him than his long-standing well-wisher, the crow. The jackal has achieved this by flattering the deer and posing as one who has mastered the scriptures. He has expertly misused scripture to achieve his personal ambition. He is inferring that the crow is not generous, and thus the deer should not listen to his advice.

*“yathā cāyaṁ mṛgo mama bandhus tathā bhavān api mṛgo 'bravīt kamanena uttarottareṇa? sarvair ekatra viśrambhālāpaiḥ sukham anubhavadbhiḥ sthīyatām yataḥ — ”*

**The jackal continued: “Just as this deer is my friend, so are you.”**  
**The deer said, “Why continue arguing and counter-arguing? Let us sit together and enjoy talking, for..**

By accepting the crow as his friend, the clever jackal prevents the crow from refuting his statements. Previously he said that magnanimous souls consider everyone a family member. If the crow now rejects the jackal’s friendship, he will appear narrow-minded, petty, and paranoid. Knowing this and fearful of creating dissension with the deer, who was being foolishly sentimental, the crow did not protest. He knew that when people become attached to a misconception, it is difficult to disillusion them for their own benefit, even with the voice of an angel. Invariably, they must learn the hard way. The deer thought that if the crow refutes the jackal it would be embarrassing, therefore he did not give a chance for the crow to speak. He is convinced that the jackal is right and the crow is being paranoid. To support the jackal he speaks the next verse.

### Text 72

*na kaścit kasyacin mitraṁ na kaścit kasyacid ripuḥ*  
*vyavahāreṇa mitrāṇi jāyante ripavas tathā*

**Перевод:**

**No one is another’s friend or enemy. Friends or enemies are created by conduct.**

**Комментарий:**

It is common sense that a person’s conduct determines whether he is a friend or enemy. There may be seeming exceptions to this rule, but they can be traced to a person’s conduct in previous lives. Sometimes we see that people have either a natural affinity or repulsion for one another, without previously having any mutual dealings. For example, Kamsa and Sisupala hated Lord Kṛṣṇa, though He had never harmed them. People may also have a natural liking or attraction for each other even as they are first introduced. In such cases, we must attribute these tendencies to relationships from previous lives. Thus the verse is not contradicted.

*kākena uktam-evam astu atha prātaḥ sarve yathābhimata-deśaṁ gatāḥ ekadā nibhṛtaṁ śṛgālo brūte-sakhe mṛga! etasminn eva vanaika-deśe sasya-pūrṇaṁ kṣetram asti tad ahaṁ tvāṁ tatra nītvā darśayāmi tathā kṛte sati mṛgaḥ pratyahaṁ tatra gatvā sasyaṁ khādati tato dina-katipayena kṣetra-patinā tad dṛṣṭvā pāśās tatra yojitāḥ anantaraṁ punar āgato mṛgaḥ tatra caran pāśair baddho 'cintayat-ko mām itaḥ kāla-pāśād iva vyādha-pāśāt trātuṁ mitrād anyaḥ samarthaḥ? atrāntare jambukas tatrāgatya upasthito 'cintayat-phalitas tāvad asmākaṁ kapaṭa-prabandhaḥ manoratha-siddhir api bāhulyān me bhaviṣyati yataḥ etasya uktṛtyamānasya māṁsāsṛg-liptāni asthīni mayā avaśyaṁ prāptavyāni tāni ca bāhulyena mama bhojanāni bhaviṣyanti sa ca mṛgas taṁ dṛṣṭvā ullāsito brūte-sakhe! chindhi tāvan mama bandhanam satvaraṁ trāyasva mām yataḥ —*

**The crow said, “So be it.” In the morning they went their ways.**

**In a solitary place, the jackal said to the deer, “My dear friend, in a certain part of the forest is a field full of crops. Let me take you there.”**

**Henceforth, the deer frequented the field daily. The owner of the field laid a net to catch him. When the deer got caught, he thought, “Who but my friend is capable of delivering me from this hunter’s net, which is like death’s snare?”**

**The jackal soon appeared and thought, “Today my cunning plot has borne fruit. I will definitely snatch his bones smeared with blood and meat after they are cut. Thus I will have plenty of food.”**

**The deer, seeing the jackal, said with delight, “My friend, quickly cut my bonds and protect me, for..**

### Text 73

*āpatsu mitraṁ jānīyād raṇe śūraṁ ṛṇe śucim*  
*bhāryāṁ kṣīṇeṣu vitteṣu vyasaneṣu ca bāndhavān*

**Перевод:**

**A friend is tested in adversity, a hero in war, an honest man when in debt, a wife in times of poverty, and relatives in times of distress.**

**Комментарий:**

"Prosperity makes friends; adversity tests them.” When a person enjoys wealth or high position, many claim to be his friend, but in adversity, only a true friend comes forward to help. When poverty enters the house, love and friendship flee. Thus a true friend is known only in times of need. The maxim, “A friend in need is a friend indeed” attests to this. Similarly, wives and relatives are tested in difficult times. Therefore, adversity is a good time for understanding who is a real friend or relative.

Aparan ca — Also...

### Text 74

*utsave vyasane prāpte durbhikṣe śatru-saṅkaṭe*  
*rāja-dvāre śmaśāne ca yas tiṣṭhati sa bāndhavaḥ*

**Перевод:**

**One who accompanies another during festivals, in misery, in famine, in national calamity, in court, and in the crematorium, is a friend.**

**Комментарий:**

A true relative is favorable through thick and thin. Sometimes people show displeasure with their friends by not attending a festival. If one has to go and humbly beg them to attend, then such people are not true friends or relatives.

Raja-dvara, or court, refers to being implicated in a court case. Smasana indicates death in the family; durbhikse means poverty and bankruptcy in business; utsava indicates a festival like a marriage.

*jambukaḥ pāśaṁ muhur muhur vilokyācintayat-dṛḍhas tāvad ayaṁ bandhaḥ brūte ca-sakhe! snāyu-nirmitāḥ pāśāḥ, tad adya bhaṭṭāraka-vāre katham etān dantaiḥ spṛśāmi? mitra! yadi citte na anyathā manyase, tadā prabhāte yat tvayā vaktavyaṁ tat kartavyam iti anantaraṁ sa kākaḥ pradoṣakā mṛgamanāgatam avalokya itas tato 'nviṣyan tathāvidhaṁ taṁ dṛṣṭvā uvāca-sakhe! kim etat? mṛgeṇoktam-avadhīrita-suhṛd-vākyasya phalam etat tathā coktam — ”*

**After carefully inspecting the net, the jackal understood the deer could not escape. He said: “My dear friend, this net is made of intestines, and today is Sunday, so how can I touch my teeth to it? (This is against religious principles. ) Dear friend, please, do not misunderstand me. Tomorrow morning I will do as you say.”**  
**That evening the crow noticed the deer had not returned and searched for him. Finding him in that situation he asked, “Friend, what is this?”**  
**“This is the result of not listening to the words of a friend. Thus it is said...”**

### Text 75

*suhṛdāṁ hita-kāmānāṁ yaḥ śṛṇoti na bhāṣitam*  
*vipat sannihitā tasya sa naraḥ śatrunandanaḥ*

**Перевод:**

**Adversity befalls those who don’t listen to the words of well-wishing friends. Such a person delights his enemy.**

**Комментарий:**

When the deer heard the jackal’s cunning excuse, he realized his mistake in accepting him as a friend. Allowing himself to be blinded by false praise, he had failed to understand the deception behind the jackal’s false humility the first time, though warned by his true friend, the crow. True humility is very rare, and when feigned it is a powerful strategy to melt peoples’ hearts and make them vulnerable. A devotee having true humility can attract the Supreme Lord, who attracts everyone. False humility attracts only those who want to be cheated. They want to be deceived, so the Lord fulfills their desire, just as He with equal expertise fulfills the desire of the sincere soul.

The jackal is so shrewd that he does not reveal his heart even when the deer is caught in the snare. Therefore, we must not wait for another to show his hand or heart to us; we must venture to look beyond the mask presented to us and see the character — or the absence of it — behind the mask. In this way, we can become practiced at discriminating between true humility and swindle. Our fundamental orientation should be not to deceive and not to be deceived. This is not a guarantee that will be always foolproof, but the lesson of this story is that it is the safest of outlooks.

*kāko brūte-sa vañcakaḥ kvāste?*  
*mṛgeṇoktaṁ-man-māṁsārthī tiṣṭhaty atraiva*  
*kāko brūte-mitra! uktam eva mayā pūrvam*

**The crow asked, “Where is the traitor?”**  
**“He is hiding somewhere nearby,” the deer replied, “waiting to eat my flesh.”**  
**The crow said, “My friend, I have already told you that...”**

### Text 76

*aparādho na me'stīti naitad viśvāsa-kāraṇam*  
*vidyate hi nṛśaṁsebhyo bhayaṁ guṇavatām api*

**Перевод:**

**Because one is faultless, the wicked will not spare one. Even saintly persons are victims of the cruel.**

**Комментарий:**

One must protect oneself from the evil or ill-intended, who enjoy harming others without cause. Just as a vehicle can hit an oblivious pedestrian, the evil harm innocent persons. Fish, birds, and animals are innocent, yet those driven by blood-lust prey on them. One with an evil or demoniac mentality makes no exception for human beings. In the. Bhagavad-gita (16. 13-15) the Supreme Lord sums up the demoniac mentality:

*idam adya mayā labdham*  
*imaṁ prāpsye manoratham*  
*idam astīdam api me*  
*bhaviṣyati punar dhanam*  
*asau mayā hataḥ śatrur*  
*haniṣye cāparān api*  
*īśvaro 'ham ahaṁ bhogī*  
*siddho 'haṁ balavān sukhī*  
*āḍhyo 'bhijanavān asmi*  
*ko 'nyo 'sti sadṛśo mayā*  
*yakṣye dāsyāmi modiṣya*  
*ity ajñāna-vimohitāḥ*

**The demoniac person thinks: “So much wealth do I have today, and I will gain more according to my schemes. So much is mine now, and it will increase in the future, more and more. He is my enemy, and I have killed him, and my other enemies will also be killed. I am the lord of everything. I am the enjoyer. I am perfect, powerful and happy. I am the richest man, surrounded by aristocratic relatives. There is none so powerful and happy as I am. I shall perform sacrifices, I shall give some charity, and thus I shall rejoice.” In this way, such persons are deluded by ignorance.**

The deer represents innocence and immaturity. He naively assumed that since he had never harmed the jackal, he was not in danger from him. He is thinking, “I have no ulterior motive in myself, so others must also be free of ulterior motive.” It can be difficult for those devoid of crookedness to see crookedness, just as it is difficult for the crooked to see virtue in others. Therefore it is essential that we use our imaginations to practice discrimination. Just as an actor develops the skill to don another’s character, keen powers of discrimination call for the ability to get inside another’s skin and temporarily gaze on the world from his eyes.

The deer represents one who wants to see only the good, which is regarded as a rare virtue in all cultures of the world. The author of Hitopadesa knows, however, that the virtue of this outlook is not practical and if overly stressed makes one vulnerable to deception. He wants to teach the young princes not to be gullible, that one must see both sides or as many sides as possible to an issue. We must not get taken in by how others present themselves, but must observe their character and motives and adjust ourselves accordingly. This is the wisdom of niti.

### Text 77

*dīpa-nirvāṇa-gandhaṁ ca suhṛd-vākyam arundhatīm*  
*na jighranti na śṛṇvanti na pśyanti gatāyuṣaḥ*

**Перевод:**

**Those about to die cannot detect the odor of an extinguished ghee lamp; they ignore the advice of friends; and are unable to see the star Arundhati.**

**Комментарий:**

The Ayurveda lists certain symptoms of one who is approaching death, some of which are described here, but applied in a slightly different sense. Although ignoring the advice of friends is not included as a symptom in Ayurveda, it appears here to show that in keeping one’s appointment with fate, one heeds none of the warning signs. One loses his good sense, vinasa-kale viprita-buddhih.

The smoke emitting from a doused ghee lamp is so acrid, it is impossible for the nose to miss, but even such a blatant sign is missed by one who is destined to encounter calamity. The star Arundhati is mentioned for a similar reason. Arundhati, or Betelgeuse, as she is known in Western astronomy, is the wife of sage Vasistha, the daughter of Kardama and Devahuti. She is seen in the sky next to the Saptarsi stars, or the Great Bear (ursa major). Arundhati is mentioned here because she is easily distinguished from the other stars owing to her red color. But just as persons hurtling towards calamity cannot smell the smoke of a doused ghee lamp, so they cannot spot a red star in the heavens.

Regarding not seeing Arundhati, the Susruta Samhita, a text on Ayurveda, says:

*paśyati na paśyati sanakṣatrāṁ yaśca devīmarundhatīm*  
*dhruvamākāśagaṅgāṁ vā taṁ vadanti gatāyuṣam*

**A person who is unable to see the star Arundhati along with the seven stars, the pole star, and the Milky Way, is at the end of his life, gatayusa.**

### Text 78

*parokṣe kārya-hantāraṁ pratyakṣe priya-vādinam*  
*varjayet tādṛśaṁ mitraṁ viṣa-kumbhaṁ payomukham*

**Перевод:**

**One who works against a friend in his absence, but who speaks sweetly In his presence, should be abandoned; for he is like a pot of poison topped with milk.**

**Комментарий:**

Such a so-called friend is more dangerous than an enemy. In dealing with an enemy we are on guard, but to an enemy who poses as a friend we are vulnerable. He can get confidential information to use against us. He is, therefore, compared to poison hidden in nourishing milk. As soon as one drinks it, the poison takes effect.

*tataḥ kāko dīrghaṁ niḥśvasya uvāca-are vañcaka! kiṁ tvayā pāpa-karmaṇā kṛtam yataḥ —*

**The crow sighed heavily and said, “O cheater, (jackal), what sinful act have you done? For..**

### Text 79

*saṁlāpitānāṁ madhurair vacobhir*  
*mithyopacāraiś ca vaśīkṛtānām*  
*āśāvatāṁ śraddadhatāṁ ca loke*  
*kim arthināṁ vañcayitavyam asti*

**Перевод:**

**What glory is there in deceiving those who are convinced by sweet words, controlled by cheating ploys, and who are faithful and full of hopes?**

**Комментарий:**

If we cheat persons who have placed their faith in us, it is as sinful as strangling a child asleep in one’s lap. Samlapita means to allure someone by sweet talks. Mithyopacara means deceitful behavior. Asavatam refers to those hoping to gain something and sraddadhata means those who have faith in a person. Exploiting such a trusting person is ignoble.

### Text 80

*upakāriṇi viśrabdhe śuddha-matau yaḥ samācarati pāpam*  
*taṁ janam asatya-sandhaṁ bhagavati vasudhe kathaṁ vahasi*

**Перевод:**

**O Goddess Earth, how can you bear the weight of those who betray their promise, who act sinfully towards benevolent trustworthy, pure- minded persons?**

**Комментарий:**

When Sukracarya asked Bali Maharaja to deny the promised charity to Lord Vamanadeva, Bali replied, “How can I withdraw my promise because of greed for money when I have already said that I shall give this land? How can I behave like an ordinary cheater, especially toward a brahmana? There is nothing more sinful than untruthfulness. Because of this, mother earth once said, T can bear any heavy thing except a person who is a liar. ’ I do not fear hell, poverty, an ocean of distress, falldown from my position, or even death itself as much as I fear cheating a brahmana.” (Bhag. 8. 20. 3-5)

### Text 81

***durjanena samaṁ sakhyaṁ vairaṁ cāpi na kārayet***  
***uṣṇo dahati cāṅgāraḥ śītaḥ kṛṣṇāyate karam***

**Перевод:**

**One should neither bc a friend or foe to the wicked. They are just like coal, which burns when it is hot, and blackens the hand when cold.**

**Комментарий:**

The wicked have one focus in life, themselves. Their desires and actions are always calculated to serve their self-interest. Here we are advised to be neither the friend or foe of the evil-minded. The outcome of friendship with the wicked is degradation and so is the outcome of enmity. The best course is to avoid them as much as possible.

It is better to have a wise and saintly enemy than a wicked enemy, who is unpredictable and irreligious. The former will plan carefully and act according to the scriptures, whereas the latter’s conduct is not under any rules or codes. To the wicked, a good code of conduct is whatever course of action gets his desired result. The Garuda Purana advises us about the wicked:

*sadbhir asita satatam sadbhih kurvita sangatim*  
*sadbhir vivadam maitri ca nasadbhih kincid acaret*

**One should always reside with saints. If that is not possible, one should associate with them, befriend them, and even if one wants to quarrel, he should do so with saints. Indeed one should have no dealings with the wicked.**

This verse is not advising one to pick a quarrel with saintly people, but it warns that one should have nothing to do with rogues — not even quarrel.

*athavā sthitir iyaṁ durjanānām —*

**Or the situation of a vile person is as given below...**

### Text 82

*prāk pādayoḥ patati khādati pṛṣṭha-māṁsaṁ*  
*karṇe phalaṁ kim api rauti śanair vicitram*  
*chidraṁ nirūpya sahasā praviśaty aśaṅkaḥ*  
*sarvaṁ khalasya caritaṁ maśakaḥ karoti*

**Перевод:**

**A mosquito has the characteristics of a wicked person. He falls at one’s feet and then bites one’s back. He hums a tune in one’s ear, but as soon as he finds a hole (a weak spot), he fearlessly enters to take advantage.**

**Комментарий:**

Dogs wag their tails not so much out of love for their master, but because they anticipate getting food. Similarly, the flattery of a wicked man is performed for self-interest. Praise is the most potent tool in a wicked person’s toolkit. Disciples who think of the guru as a facility for sense gratification know well how to be lavish in their praises in their attempt to manipulate him. Although externally, the destitute jackal humbly begged the friendship of the deer, his heart’s desire was to eat his flesh.

An enlightened leader is always wary of such servile flatterers, knowing that hearing praise of oneself is an intoxicant that increases pride, and that one who is attached to praise becomes the plaything of his most eloquent flatterer. It is like drinking a sweet poison that slowly kills one.

Those who fear the intoxicating power of flattery accept as truth that one who praises me is my enemy, one who criticizes me is my friend. Although this is pregnant with meaning, it is difficult to practice. Ultimately, the conditioned soul’s passion for honor is a tighter bond than sex desire.

In this verse, mosquitoes swarming around the feet are compared to rogues feigning respect while seeking an advantage. As the mosquito's real intention, is to suck blood, the rogue’s intention is to exploit his victim. When one is covered by a cloth while sleeping, mosquitoes look for a hole (chidra) to enter. The Sanskrit word chidra means “hole” as well as a defect or weakness. Similarly, a rogue carefully searches for a defect or weakness in his victim and attacks. It is best to abandon such bloodthirsty mosquito-like association.

### Text 83

*durjanaḥ priya-vādī ca naitad viśvāsa-kāraṇam*  
*madhu tiṣṭhati jihvāgre hṛdi hālāhalaṁ viṣam*

**Перевод:**

**Though a vile man may speak sweetly, there is no reason to put faith in him; while the tip of his tongue is sweet, his heart is full of deadly poison.**

**Комментарий:**

A vile person is like a sword covered by a beautiful sheath of velvet studded with gems. Distracted by its external beauty, one forgets its destructive nature. Similarly, a vile person hides his malicious nature behind sweet, melodious words. His heart is compared to halahala, the most deadly poison. Placing trust in the flattering words of vile people can only lead to misfortune.

*atha prabhāte sa kṣetra-patir laguḍa-hastas taṁ pradeśam āgacchan kākenāvalokitaḥ tam avalokya kākenoktam-sakhe mṛga! tvam ātmānaṁ mṛtavat sandarśya vātenodaraṁ pūrayitvā pādān stabdhīkṛtya tiṣṭha ahaṁ tava cakṣuṣī cañcvā kim api vilikhāmi, yadāhaṁ śabdaṁ karomi, tadā tvam utthāya satvaraṁ palāyiṣyase mṛgas tathaiva kāka-vacanena sthitaḥ tataḥ kṣetra-patinā harṣotphulla-locanena tathāvidho mṛga ālokitaḥ athāsau-āḥ! svayaṁ mṛto 'si? ity uktvā mṛgaṁ bandhanāt mocayitvā pāśān saṁvarītuṁ satvaro babhūva tataḥ kiyad dūre antarite kṣetra-patau sa mṛgaḥ kākasya śabdaṁ śrutvā satvaram utthāya palāyitaḥ tam uddiśya tena kṣetra-patinā prakopāt kṣiptena laguḍena śṛgālo vyāpāditaḥ tathā coktam —*

**The next morning the crow saw the owner approaching the field carrying a stick. The crow said to the deer, “My dear friend, now pretend to be dead. Fill your belly with air, stiffen your legs, and lie perfectly still. I will pretend to peck at your eyes, and when I give a call, swiftly get up and flee.”**  
**The deer agreed, and followed the crow’s advice. Then the man cast his happy eyes on the deer and exclaimed, “Oh! He has died by himself! ”**  
**He gathered the net and began folding it up. When the man was at a safe distance from the deer, the crow called and the deer took off. The man threw his stick in anger at the deer, but it hit the jackal and killed him. Therefore it is said...**

### Text 84

*tribhir varṣais tribhir māsais tribhiḥ pakṣais tribhir dinaiḥ*  
*atyutkaṭaiḥ pāpa-puṇyair ihaiva phalam aśnute*

**Перевод:**

**The results of powerful sins or pious acts are reaped in this very life within three years, three months, three fortnights, or within three days.**

**Комментарий:**

*ato 'haṁ bravīmi-bhakṣya-bhakṣyakayoḥ prītir ity ādi.*

**Therefore, I (Hiranyaka) say, “Friendship between the prey and the predator and so on..**

*iti mṛga-vāyasa-śṛgāla-kathā*

**Thus Ends the Story of the Deer, the Crow and the Jackal.**

*kākaḥ punar āha —*

**The crow (Laghupatanaka) again said (to Hiranyaka, the mouse)**

### Text 85

*bhakṣitenāpi bhavatā nāhāro mama puṣkalaḥ*  
*tvayi jīvati jīvāmi citragrīva ivānagha*

**Перевод:**

**O pious Hiranyaka, even if I ate you I would not be satisfied; but while you live, I will be happy like Citragriva.**

**Комментарий:**

Anyac ca — Again...

### Text 86

*tiraścām api viśvāso dṛṣṭaḥ puṇyaika-karmaṇām*  
*satāṁ hi sādhu-śīlatvāt svabhāvo na nivartate*

**Перевод:**

**Even animals that arc inclined toward pious deeds should be trusted; persons with saintly dispositions never deviate from good behavior.**

**Комментарий:**

One should study the nature of others by observing consistency in their behavior. A lowborn man need not be inherently bad, nor is a learned or highborn person automatically good. It is by observing character that one tells the good from the bad, and one can distinguish between a faithful friend and a manipulative flatterer. Otherwise one will be cheated at every step. In Kali-yuga this danger is multiplied because it is the age of hypocrisy.

By observing others, one learns about one’s self and it is imperative for one to understand his own nature (svabhava) so one may soar with one’s strengths. Just as observing others helps us understand ourselves, understanding ourselves helps us to understand the natures of others.

kiṁ ca — Moreover,

### Text 87

*sādhoḥ prakopitasyāpi mano nāyāti vikriyām*  
*na hi tāpayituṁ śakyaṁ sāgarāmbhas tṛṇolkayā*

**Перевод:**

**Just as the temperature of the ocean is not affected by burning a handful of straw, the heart of a saintly person is never changed, even by anger.**

**Комментарий:**

A sadhu is not malicious by nature. Although he may sometimes be irritated or angry, he never thinks of harming or destroying anyone. Even his anger is beneficial for others, as shown by Narada Muni’s curse on the two sons of Kuvera. A saintly person’s curse is a blessing.

*hiraṇyako brūte-capalas tvam capalena saha snehaḥ sarvathā na kartavyaḥ tathā coktam —*

**Hiranyaka said, “You are fickle, and one should not make friends with the fickle-minded. ’’**

### Text 88

*mārjāro mahiṣo meṣaḥ kākaḥ kāpuruṣas tathā*  
*viśvāsāt prabhavanty ete viśvāsas tatra no hitaḥ*

**Перевод:**

**One should never place confidence in a cat, buffalo, ram, crow, or a vile man, as that increases their ability to cause harm.**

**Комментарий:**

The purpose of this verse is to teach one not to trust or befriend a vile person, because sooner or later they will betray your trust. These animals mentioned are noted for their fickleness, or stubbornness. They have an indomitable will to follow their own counsel; thus to put confidence in them is foolish. A vile man is no better. He may pretend to be worthy of our confidence, but as in a few verses back, we had the mosquito example, the vile man is always on the lookout for an opening.

*kiṁ cānyat-śatru-pakṣo bhavān asmākam śatruṇā sandhir na vidheyam*

**Moreover, you are my natural enemy and one should never make truce with an enemy.**

### Text 89

*śatruṇā na hi sandadhyāt saṁśliṣṭenāpi sandhinā*  
*sutaptam api pānīyaṁ śamayaty eva pāvakam*

**Перевод:**

**One should never befriend an enemy even after forming a truce; boiling water still extinguishes fire.**

**Комментарий:**

Water is the natural enemy of fire, and even if by boiling it has acquired one quality of fire, still it does not change its basic nature; it will extinguish fire. Similarly, mice are the natural prey for crows; therefore it would be a fatal mistake for Hiranyaka to befriend Laghupatanaka.

### Text 90

***durjanaḥ parihartavyo vidyayālaṅkṛto 'pi san***  
*maṇinā bhūṣitaḥ sarpaḥ kim asau na bhayaṅkaraḥ*

**Перевод:**

**Even though scholarly, a wicked man should be shunned; though its hood be decorated with a jewel, a snake is still dangerous.**

**Комментарий:**

Education and bad character go ill together. Giving knowledge to a wicked man is like supplying weapons to an enemy, because he will surely use it to exploit the innocent. A wicked man is compared to a snake and education is compared to a jewel. A gem on the hood of a snake will only distract us, making us more vulnerable to its sting. Educating a wicked man is also compared to feeding a snake milk, which only increases its poison, payah panam bhujanganam kevalam visa-vardhanam.

The goal of education should be to create men of character. Character development is far more important than mere education. The purpose of modern education is to produce businessmen and technicians, people narrowly focused on their skills, whereas the purpose of Vedic education is to impart wisdom and to build good character. One's skill is intrinsic to one’s nature and while it requires training to perfect, competence in a skill alone is not an education. Vedic education seeks to produce independently thoughtful men, who live principle-centered lives. Immoral students are considered unfit to be taught. Education without morality is like a body without a soul.

### Text 91

*yad aśakyaṁ na tac chaktyaṁ yac chaktyaṁ śakyam eva tat*  
*nodake śakaṭaṁ yāti na ca naur gacchati*

**Перевод:**

**What is impossible can never be possible; and what is possible can surely be accomplished. A chariot cannot move on water, nor can a boat move on land.**

**Комментарий:**

In the second line of the verse the author gives two examples of how the impossible cannot be possible. These examples also indicate that what is possible can be achieved, i. e. the chariot can run on the land and the boat can ply on the water. The author cites this verse to establish that a vile man will not change his ways any more than a chariot can run on water or a boat run on land. Thus one should not be misled by his cunning words and deceptive behavior.

### Text 92

*mahatāpy artha-sāreṇa yo viśvasiti śatruṣu*  
*bhāryāsu ca viraktāsu tad-antaṁ tasya jīvanam*

**Перевод:**

**If one trusts an enemy for the sake of wealth, or a wife devoid of love, he has surely reached the end of his life.**

**Комментарий:**

Placing trust in an enemy is like inviting death. Even if the enemy has been appeased by huge amounts of wealth, one should never trust him. If a wife has no affection for her husband, she will not be faithful, even if the husband satisfies all her needs. A woman can leave everything to join a paramour, even if he is a pauper. In Delhi the wife of a wealthy man became attached to a mendicant and conspired to kill her husband. She left the back door unlocked so hired killers could enter and kill her husband. After they fled, she scattered her jewelry about and called the police to report that dacoits had robbed her and killed her husband. It took the police a year to unravel the conspiracy.

Of course, sometimes it is the man who conspires to kill his wife. The air is so full of mistrust in Kali-yuga that even though we have so- called love marriages in the West, the spouses now make prenuptial agreements, so when the marriage is dissolved the interest of both sides will be protected. The trust is absent from the very beginning.

*laghu-patanako brūte-śrutaṁ mayā sarvaṁ, tathāpi mamaitāvan eva saṅkalpaḥ yat tvayā saha sauhṛdyam avaśyaṁ karaṇīyam iti anyathā anāhāreṇātmānaṁ tava dvāri vyāpādayiṣyāmīti tathā hi — ”*

**Laghupatanaka said, “I have heard all this, yet still I am determined to gain your friendship. Otherwise, I will fast until death at your door, for...”**

### Text 93

*mṛd-ghaṭavat sukha-bhedyo duḥsandhānaś ca durjano bhavati*  
*sujanas tu kanaka-ghaṭavad durbhedyaś cāśu sandheyaḥ*

**Перевод:**

**Friendship with the wicked is compared to a clay pot — easily broken and impossible to mend; whereas friendship with a gentleman is compared to a gold pot — difficult to break, yet easy to mend.**

**Комментарий:**

The wicked establish friendships to satisfy ulterior motives, but as soon as their needs are met, or the relationship proves troublesome, it ends. Wicked or selfish people try to use others to serve their own agenda, and those they cannot involve, they deem useless. They cannot respect a human being merely because he or she is a human being; rather they see people as things, objects to use, and if not useful, to be discarded. Just being another human being — without necessarily some further definition or criterion of value — is not enough to win the respect of the wicked and selfish. This narcissism is ultimately the root of evil.

In contrast, the saintly are unselfish and unmotivated. Their friendship is steadfast. If discord arises, it can easily be reconciled by reasonable discussion. Thus Sunderpandya, an authority on niti, says (Niti-dvisastika 64):

*pasanesv iva rekha murkhe vairam drdham ciram bhavati jala-madhyesv iva rekha prajne na ciram bhavati vairam*

**Enmity among fools is deep and steadfast, like a groove cut in stone; but a wise man’s hostility is like a line drawn on water; it remains only for an instant.**

Kinca — And also,

### Text 94

*dravatvāt sarva-lohānāṁ nimittād mṛga-pakṣiṇām*  
*bhayāl lobhāc ca mūrkhāṇāṁ saṅgataḥ darśanāt satām*

**Перевод:**

**Metals arc combined by melting; birds and animals unite for some common purpose; fools make friendship out of fear or greed; but the saintly unite by seeing each other.**

**Комментарий:**

This verse shows that metals combine by losing their individual identities, birds and animals unite to serve some personal motive (as dogs unite to combat pigs or monkeys), fools unite out of fear or greed, but saintly persons have no ulterior motive for uniting.

In any profession we find varying degrees of skill. Similarly, among saintly persons there are also varying degrees of accomplishment. One saintly person may be purer than others, but regardless of the level of attainment, one common trait runs among all saintly persons: they do not have an ulterior motive.

No ulterior motive means that a saintly person is never interested in exploiting another living being, either human or animal. Therefore, just by seeing each other they become jubilant.

### Text 95

*nārikela-samākārā dṛśyante hi suhṛjjanāḥ*  
*anye badarikākārā bahir eva manoharāḥ*

**Перевод:**

**Friendship among the pure-hearted is like the coconut — hard and rough on the outside, but sweet and soft on the inside; others are like the jujube — soft and attractive on the outside but sour and full of worms on the inside.**

**Комментарий:**

This verse is very significant. In many cultures there are social conventions that require one to pretend to be sweet, charming, and adorable, regardless of one’s real feelings. This is false friendship, and yet because of convention, one has to play along. Sometimes it is difficult to tell whether someone is truly a friend or not, because they play the game of pretending so well.

Real friends, on the other hand, sometimes appear rough or harsh with each other, but their mutual friendship runs very deep. It is sweet on the inside. This verse suggests we put more stock in straigthforward dealings with straightforward people and avoid superficial relationships. Pretence drains one’s energy for no good cause. Honesty is the best policy. While honesty may be difficult at times, overall, it helps us live more freely, openly, and the practice of honesty exercises our courage and makes us stout-hearted.

Those who live a life of false smiles, false relationships and social or subconscious rationalizations slowly twist their personalities. This action may be subtle and imperceptible, and eventually will cause neurotic or psychosomatic symptoms to develop. The cardinal rule of human nature is that one cannot go against one’s conscience without distorting one’s being. Thus slowly one loses touch with one’s conscience.

*Ata eva satam sangatir isyate*

**Therefore, I desire friendship with the saintly.**

### Text 96

*sneha-cchede'pi sādhūnāṁ guṇā nāyānti vikriyām*  
*bhaṅge'pi hi mṛṇālānām anubadhnanti tantavaḥ*

**Перевод:**

**Even if friendship with a saintly person is broken, his character does not change; the broken stem of the lotus remains attached by the fibers.**

**Комментарий:**

After the Pandavas were banished to the forest, Duryodhana wanted to exhibit his opulence to humiliate them. In his attempt he was captured by some Gandharvas, and this gave Bhima great joy. But the saintly Yudhisthira ordered Bhima and Arjuna to rescue Duryodhana. Although their friendship had been broken, Yudhisthira would not act maliciously toward Duryodhana, as it was against his nature.

Among ordinary people we see that enmity generally results when friendship ends. Indeed former intimate friends can become the most dangerous enemies, owing to their awareness of the other’s weaknesses and secrets. Naturally, their alliance will be sought by those who intend to harm the former friend.

A saintly person, however, may forsake a friendship owing to the other’s disqualification, but it is not his nature to act maliciously, seeking revenge or acting from some similar motive. A saintly person understands that every act motivated by malice, envy, or hatred destroys his own heart.

### Text 97

*śucitvam tyāgitā śauryaṁ sāmānyaṁ sukha-duḥkhayoḥ*  
*dākṣiṇyaṁ cānuraktiś ca satyatā ca suhṛd-guṇāḥ*

**Перевод:**

**These are the qualities of a true friend: Purity, generosity, chivalry, being composed in happiness and distress, politeness, affection, and truthfulness.**

**Комментарий:**

The human tendency is to try and find a friend with these qualities, without endeavoring to cultivate them in one’s own self. It is better to embody these qualities in one’s self. Then one’s purity, generosity and so forth will attract like-minded persons. It is similar to the predicament about love. Most people try to make themselves lovable in some way, like a commodity on the market, in the hope that someone will come along and love them. The real art, however, is not how to be lovable, but how to be loving. It is better to give (love or friendship) than to receive. Of course, if we are successful in giving, we receive many times more than we gave. This is one of the natural laws, like the law of gravity. On this basis, Vaisnavas, devotees of the Lord, live by the principle of doing good for others.

*“etair guṇair upeto bhavad=anyo mayā kaḥ suhṛt prāptavyaḥ? ity ādi tad-vacanam ākarṇya hiraṇyako bahiḥ niḥsṛtyāha-āpyāyito 'haṁ bhavatām etena vacanāmṛtena tathā coktam — "*

**Besides you, where will I find a friend with these qualities?**  
**Upon hearing this, Hiranyaka replied, “My heart has been melted by your nectarean words. For it is said — ”**

### Text 98

*gharmārtaṁ na tathā suśītala-jalaiḥ snānaṁ na muktāvalī*  
*na śrīkhaṇḍa-vilepanam sukhayati pratyaṅgam apy arpitam*  
*prītyai sajjana-bhāṣitaṁ prabhavati prāyo yathā cetasaḥ*  
*sad-yuktyā ca pariṣkṛtaṁ sukṛtinām ākṛṣṭi-mantropamam*

**Перевод:**

**A person disturbed by heat is not as pleased at heart by bathing in cool water, wearing a necklace of pearls, or smearing sandalwood all over his body, as he is upon hearing the affectionate and wise words of a saintly person, which act like an enthralling charm.**

**Комментарий:**

The material world is a place of duality. Even those not educated in philosophy understand this instinctively. Happiness is always followed by distress, heat by cold, loss by gain, and so forth. The result of this duality is that the living being is never secure. Even in long periods of relative happiness there is the niggling fear that it will end — that distress will come without warning. Human life means one is always looking for relief from dualities, in some form or other. This verse says that the insightful counsel of the saintly is the best balm to soothe the suffering of living beings, because they have understood how to live in this world without being controlled by the dualities. They know how to be aloof from it. Therefore they are called transcendentalists.

Saints wander the face of the earth in search of those sufficiently frustrated by the dualities, and who are eager to get out of material consciousness. No words can give greater solace to such suffering individuals than those of the genuine saint who has received knowledge in the parampara system and has become a via medium for such knowledge. Human life is an opportunity to seek the shelter of such saintly persons.

Anyac ca — Again,

### Text 99

***rahasya-bhedo yācñā ca naiṣṭhuryaṁ cala-cittayā***  
***krodho niḥsatyatā dyūtam etan mitrasya dūṣaṇam***

**Перевод:**

**These are defects in a friend: Divulging a secret, asking for money, cruelty, ficklc-mindedness, anger, deceitfulness, and gambling.**

**Комментарий:**

Borrowing money from friends is one of the notorious means of killing a friendship, because if the money is returned late or not at all, it ruins the relationship. A less frequent problem, but one with potential to embarrass, is when one friend is unable to fulfill the request for a loan. Such dealings, however, will not harm relations among true friends, but a true friend is a rarity.

Fickle-mindedness is a defect in friends because fickle-minded persons are unreliable, and their behavior is invariably agitating. It may require excessive energy to maintain such relationships. Since most people find fickle-minded friends draining, they often regret the relationship, and thus it is mentioned here as a defect. The threat to friendship that the other items stand for is obvious.

*anena vacana-krameṇa tat ekam api dūṣaṇaṁ tvayi na lakṣyate yataḥ —*

**According to this, ou have not even a single defect. Because...**

### Text 100

*paṭutvaṁ satyavāditvaṁ kathā-yogena buddhyate*  
*astabdhatvam acāpalyaṁ pratyakṣenāvagamyate*

**Перевод:**

**Shrewdness and truthfulness are understood during conversation, while courage and gravity are understood by direct experience.**

**Комментарий:**

An expert can understand many things by observing bodily movements, facial expressions, and by attentive listening. It is said:

*ākāra-riṅgatair gatyā ceṣṭayā bhāṣaṇena ca*  
*netra-vaktra-vikāreṇa lakṣyate'ntargataṁ manaḥ*

**A person’s heart can be understood by studying his bodily features, movements, walk, actions, speech, eyes, and facial expressions. (Hitopadesa 2. 50).**

Without being enamored by the crow’s praise, Hiranyaka could understand Laghupatanaka’s sincerity, whereas in the earlier stories the traveler, vulture, and deer were all deceived by flatterers. It is a rare achievement when a person is not influenced by flattery. The safe response to being flattered is to immediately suspect the motives of the flatterer. Yet most times this warning is not heeded, and the sweet, intoxicating words of praise cloud the judgment of the person being praised. Hiranyaka had the wisdom and experience to be above this quality.

He had argued that the natural enmity between crows and mice inhibited their proposed friendship, but Laghupatanaka begged him to reconsider. A cheater posing as an aspiring friend may also beg reconsideration, but an expert, by observing his facial expressions and bodily movements, can sense the uneasiness stemming from his fear of being exposed, or can sense the cheater’s lust, greed or envy behind his disguise. The primary qualification for developing this sensing ability is to be free of the motive to exploit others.

Hiranyaka represents an experienced person well-versed in scripture, and free from personal ambition; he has the qualities of a bona fide guru. The crow represents a sincere disciple, who is determined to be accepted by the guru, or fast until death. The guru must test the sincerity of a prospective disciple as Hiranyaka has done.

These stories imply that providence arranges for the cheaters and the cheated to meet, but also arranges for sincere seekers to find real saints. In this age of hypocrisy, there is great demand for bogus gurus and self-proclaimed gods who promise instant ecstasy, liberation, or even to become God. Being conditioned to instant gratification, people expect the same in spiritual life also. Unfortunately, after being exploited, people remain unfulfilled, and the promised ecstasy and liberation are as elusive as ever. And most of them cannot even understand that they are being exploited. This is because they are deeply insincere.

aparaṁ ca — Moreover —

### Text 101

*anyathaiva hi sauhārdaṁ bhavet svacchāntarātmanaḥ*  
*pravartate'nyathā vāṇī śāṭhyopahata-cetasaḥ*

**Перевод:**

**Friendship and speech of the pure hearted is of one type, whereas words uttered by a deceitful person are of another.**

**Комментарий:**

A mother feeds her child differently than a maidservant. One is motivated by loving feelings and the other does it for a salary, yet the external actions of mother and servant are the same, even to the point that the maidservant may express greater affection for her employer’s baby; however, if she gets a better job, her service and affection ends. The point is that despite the visual similarity between the actions of the mother and the servant, careful observation of their speech, eye movements, and so on, reveals their diverse inner feelings. Similarly by careful observation such as Hiranyaka displays, one can distinguish between a genuine friend and a deceitful person, for the facial expressions and the mannerisms of a loving person are unlike those of a cheat, though their words and actions may be similar.

### Text 102

*manasy anyad vacasy anyat karmaṇy anyad durātmanām*  
*manasy ekaṁ vacasy ekaṁ karmaṇy ekaṁ mahātmanām*

**Перевод:**

**The wicked think in one way, speak in another, yet act differently; but the saintly say what they think, and do what they say.**

**Комментарий:**

Hiranyaka accepted the crow as saintly, because he was truthful. Vedic literature is meant to bring humanity to this level of behavior and for this purpose the Lord appears in the material world again and again. Truthfulness is the last leg of dharma. Once truthfulness is gone, there is no more dharma in human society. Whatever rites and rituals remain become the servant of untruth. The basis of truthfulness is integrity. One has to make a decision to be a person of integrity. Once that decision is made, one practices truthfulness with relative ease.

The character of Sukracarya and his disciple Bali Maharaja, respectively, demonstrate the duplicity and straightforwardness depicted in this verse. By citing moral codes, Sukracarya advised Bali Maharaja to deny the charity promised to Lord Visnu. Bali Maharaja, on the other hand, made his promise in keeping with his inner desire to worship Visnu, and acted accordingly, despite the temporary hardships it caused. Bali showed integrity by keeping his word, whereas Sukracarya was duplicitous, because despite citing moral codes, he had an ulterior motive. He wanted to keep Bali Maharaja under his control, so he advised him not to surrender to Lord Visnu, which was in Bali’s best interest. For his personal ambition, the guru advised the disciple against his spiritual progress. If Bali had been a blind follower of his guru, he would have made the most grievous mistake of his life.

In today’s society, people generally don't take into consideration a person’s character. If one has a Ph. D or other impressive credentials, he is accepted as an authority irregardless of his character. This practice is opposed to brahminical culture, which awards positions of authority based on merit, and the essence of merit is ideal character.

*tad bhavatu bhavataḥ abhimatam eva ity uktvā hiraṇyako maitryaṁ vidhāya bhojana-viśeṣair vāyasaṁ santoṣya vivaraṁ praviṣṭaḥ vāyaso 'pi sva-sthānaṁ gataḥ tataḥ-prabhṛti tayoḥ anyo 'nyāhāra-pradānena kuśala-praśnaiḥ viśrambhālāpaiś ca kiyat-kālo 'tivartane ekadā laghu-patanako hiraṇyakam āha-sakhe! vāyasasya kaṣṭataralabhyāhāram idaṁ sthānam tad etat parityajya sthānāntaraṁ gantum icchāmi* *hiraṇyako brūte*

**Saying, “As you desire,” Hiranyaka befriended the crow and satisfied him with nice food. They then returned to their respective residences. From that day, they passed their time offering each other food, inquiring about each other’s welfare, and speaking confidentially.**  
**Once Laghupatanaka told Hiranyaka, “O friend, it is difficult for me to find food here, so I wish to give up this place for another.” Hiranyaka said...**

### Text 103

*sthāna-bhraṣṭā na śobhante dantāḥ keśā nakhā narāḥ*  
*iti vijñāya matimān sva-sthānaṁ na parityajet*

**Перевод:**

**Teeth, hair, nails, and human beings lose their beauty when they fall from their respective positions. Understanding this, a wise man should never leave his designated place.**

**Комментарий:**

Every society has its conventional wisdom. Quite often conventional wisdom is a social rationalization, a plausible lie, that a particular community practices. This verse is an example of such a rationalization that passes as wisdom. In this story, the crow represents wisdom, because crows are reputed for their long life, and wisdom is always associated with longevity. The clearest indication of a social rationalization is that it is invariably disempowering rather than empowering. Laghupatanaka wants to relocate to better his position, but Hiranyaka cites conventional wisdom, which, if heeded, will prevent the crow from bettering his lot. The verse is correct with respect to teeth, hair, and nails which are indeed destestable when removed from their positions, but a person can give up his post for a better opportunity Otherwise no one would ever progress. Thus the crow’s reply...

*kāko brūte-mitra! kāpuruṣasya vacanam etat.”*

**The crow said, “These are the words of a coward.”**

### Text 104

*sthānam utsṛjya gacchanti siṁhāḥ sat-puruṣā gajāḥ*  
*tatraiva nidhanaṁ yānti kākāḥ kāpuruṣā mṛgāḥ*

**Перевод:**

**Lions, saints, and elephants give up their respective places in time of need and go elsewhere; but crows, cowards, and deer perish in their designated places.**

**Комментарий:**

Human beings are generally attached to comfortable positions and will not risk losing them even for better opportunities. To justify this attachment, they develop both personal rationalizations or social ones. Without risk there is no gain, yet most humans want to live risk-free, although that is impossible in the treacherous material world where danger lurks in others, in nature, and in our own selves. Of course, decisions regarding change should be made with careful deliberation, considering the advantages and disadvantages of the situation, but the fundamental point is that growth means change, and change means risk. You can’t have one without the other.

### Text 105

*ko vīrasya manasvinaḥ sva-viṣayaḥ ko vā videśaḥ smṛtaḥ*  
*yaṁ deśaṁ śrayate tam eva kurute bāhu-pratāpārjitam*  
*yad daṁṣṭrānakha-lāṅgula-praharaṇaḥ siṁho vanaṁ gāhate*  
*tasminn eva hata-dvipendra-rudhirais tṛṣṇāṁ chinnatty ātmanaḥ*

**Перевод:**

**The lion, whose weapons are his jaw, claws, and tail, kills elephants in any forest he enters, and thus satisfies his thirst for blood. Similarly, for a broad-minded hero, there is no difference between his native and a foreign land, for he conquers each with his mighty arms.**

**Комментарий:**

*hiraṇyako brūte-mitra kva gantavyam?*

**Hiranyaka said — “Friend, where will you go?”**

### Text 106

*calaty ekena pādena tiṣṭhaty ekena buddhimān*  
*nāsamīkṣya paraṁ sthānaṁ pūrvam āyatanaṁ tyajet*

**Перевод:**

**A wise man walks with one foot and stands with the other; thus one should not leave one place without carefully scrutinizing the next.**

**Комментарий:**

Wisdom lies in making the best use of the available facility or situation. A wise man does not act without proper deliberation. He is attached to progress, not to a particular place or post. Walking with one foot and standing with the other means that he does not act rashly and lose whatever he possesses in the vain hope of acquiring a better situation. In this regard Srimad-Bhagavatam gives the example of a caterpillar which does not leave one leaf until it has firmly grasped the next one.

*vāyaso brūte-mitra! asti sunirūpitaṁ sthānam hiraṇyako 'vadat-kiṁ tat? vāyasaḥ kathayati-asti daṇḍakāraṇye karpūragaurābhidhānaṁ saraḥ tatra cira-kālopārjitaḥ priya-suhṛn me mantharābhidhānaḥ kūrmaḥ sahaja-dhārmikaḥ prativasati paśya mitra -*

**The crow said, “Friend, there is a nice place well-known to me.” “Where is it?” Hiranyaka asked.**  
**“In the Dandakaranya forest is a lake called Karpura-gaura where my very old and dear friend Manthara the tortoise resides. He is very religious. My dear friend, just hear... (about the importance of religious activities performed without motive. )”**

### Text 107

*paropadeśe pāṇḍityaṁ sarveṣāṁ sukaraṁ nṛṇām*  
*dharme svīyam anuṣṭhānaṁ kasyacit tu mahātmanaḥ*

**Перевод:**

**To be expert in instructing others is very easy, but one who actually practices what he preaches is a great soul and very rare.**

**Комментарий:**

Teaching (pracara) should be preceded and accompanied by exemplary behavior (acara). Actions speak louder than words. By steadfastly following religious principles, one gains potency and creates faith in those who witness such excellent behavior. Lord Kṛṣṇa re-establishes religion not so much by sermonizing, but by setting the example. Though He is the Supreme Lord. He follows religious duties to set the standard for others. His preaching strategy is clear: (Bg. 3. 21-26, 29):

Whatever action a great man performs, common men follow. And whatever standards he sets by exemplary acts, all the world pursues.

O son of Prtha, there is no work prescribed for me within all the three planetary system. Nor am I in want of anything, nor have I a need to obtain anything — and yet I am engaged in prescribed duties. For if I ever failed to engage in carefully performing prescribed duties, O Partha, certainly all men would follow My path.

As the ignorant perform their duties with attachment to results, the learned may similarly act, but without attachment, for the sake of leading people on the right path.

So as not to disrupt the minds of ignorant men attached to the fruitive results of prescribed duties, a learned person should not induce them to stop work. Rather, by working in the spirit of devotion, he should engage them in all sorts of activities \[for the gradual development of Kṛṣṇa consciousness\].

Bewildered by the modes of material nature, the ignorant fully engage themselves in material activities and become attached.

But the wise should not unsettle them, although these duties are inferior due to the performers’ lack of knowledge.

Example is better than precept. That does not mean precept is unnecessary. Precept must explain systematically and logically the reasons and benefits for particular codes of conduct. But precept alone will not be enough. A saintly person’s teachings may convince us to embark on the spiritual path, but display of proper conduct inspires a change of heart, which leads to a permanent change in behavior, from the inside out. This is the essence of the above verses. As the clichī goes, “Talk is cheap.” Therefore one is recognized as a saint primarily by his conduct and secondarily by his words which must be based on sastra, otherwise a good thief is also an example for other thieves.

There is no meaning to the word acarya if there is no acara. At present there arc many charlatans posing as acaryas or jagad-gurus, who secretly engage in abominable activities, or simply lack the full realization of the function of teacher. For such unscrupulous persons, preaching is simply a means to amass wealth. Wealth is meant to be used for religion, but such people utilize religion to gain wealth. Laksmi is meant to serve Kṛṣṇa — not that we use Kṛṣṇa to get Laksmi. Srimad- Bhagavatam (Bhag. 1. 2. 9) prohibits such misuse of religion, dharmasya hy āpavargyasya nārtho 'rthāyopakalpate.

*sa ca bhojana-viśeṣair māṁ saṁvardhayiṣyati hiraṇyako 'py āha-tat kim atrāvasthāya mayā kartavyam? yataḥ-*

**He will nourish me by supplying choice foods.**  
**Hiranyaka said, “Then what will I do by remaining here? For...”**

### Text 108

*yasmin deśe na sammāno na vṛttir na ca bāndhavaḥ*  
*na ca vidyāgamaḥ kaścit taṁ deśaṁ parivarjayet*

**Перевод:**

**One should abandon that country where one is not respected, where one has no means of livelihood, no relatives, and no opportunity to acquire knowledge.**

### Text 109

*dhanikaḥ śrotriyo rājā nadī vaidyas tu pañcamaḥ*  
*pañca yatra na vidyante tatra vāsaṁ na kārayet*

**Перевод:**

**One should not reside in a country where these five do not exist: a rich man to give credit, a brahmana, a king, a river, and a doctor.**

**Комментарий:**

aparaṁ ca — Also —

### Text 110

*loka-yātrā bhayaṁ lajjā dākṣiṇyaṁ tyāga-śīlatā*  
*pañca yatra na vidyante na kuryāt tatra saṁsthitim*

**Перевод:**

**One should not live where these five are lacking: a means of livelihood, fear of the king, a sense of shame, politeness, and generosity.**

### Text 111

*tatra mitra! na vastavyaṁ yatra nāsti catuṣṭayam*  
*ṛṇa-dātā ca vaidyaś ca śrotriyaḥ sajalā nadī*

**Перевод:**

**My dear friend, one should not reside in a place where these four do not exist: a lender of money, a doctor, a learned brahmana, and a plentiful river.**

**Комментарий:**

The last four verses describe an unsuitable residence. Basically, this means one should not live where people are discourteous; where one cannot make a comfortable living; where there is shortage of water or good doctors, good schools, and good association. As to the importance of giving up bad or wicked association, the twelfth century philosopher Moses Maimonides made this drastic recommendation: “If you live in a country whose inhabitants are evil, avoid their company. If they try to force you to associate with them, leave the country, even if it means going to the desert.”

*ato mām api tatra naya vāyaso 'vadat-evam astu atha vāyasas tena mitreṇa saha vicitrālāpa-sukhena tasya sarasaḥ samīpaṁ yayau tato mantharo dūrād eva laghu-patanakam avalokya utthāya yathocitam ātithyaṁ vidhāya mūṣikasyāpy atithi-satkāraṁ cakāra yataḥ-*

**“Therefore, take me there also.”**  
**The crow said, “So be it.”**  
**Thereafter, the crow while happily speaking on diverse subjects with his friend, went to the lake. From afar, the tortoise Manthara saw Laghupatanaka and stood up to greet him properly. He also received the mouse with respect befitting a guest. Because...**

According to Vedic etiquette, the friends of one’s friend should be treated warmly; thus Manthara treated Hiranyaka as if he knew him. A loving wife not only serves the husband, but also his parents and brothers. Similarly, one who engages in service to the Lord must not neglect to serve His devotees, who are very dear to Him. In fact, Lord Kṛṣṇa told Arjuna, “Those who claim to be My devotees are not My devotees, but those who are the servants of My devotees are My devotees” (Adi Purana).

### Text 112

*bālo vā yadi vā vṛddho yuvā vā gṛham āgataḥ*  
*tasya pūjā vidhātavyā sarvatrābhyāgato guruḥ*

**Перевод:**

**Whether a guest is a child, an old man, or a youth, he should be honored, because a guest is always worshipable.**

### Text 113

*gurur agnir dvijātīnāṁ varṇānāṁ brāhmaṇo guruḥ*  
*patir eko guruḥ strīṇāṁ sarvatrābhyāgato guruḥ*

**Перевод:**

**The firegod is worshipable for brahmanas, ksatriyas, and vaisyas; the brahmana is respectable for all four varnas; the husband is most worshipable for the wife, and a guest is worshipable for everyone.**

**Комментарий:**

Agni, or fire, is worshiped as it is considered the mouth of Lord Visnu. When a couple begins grhastha life, a sacrificial fire is lit in the home and oblations are daily offered into it. The brahmanas, who are the gurus of society, are worshiped by all members of the varnasrama system as they protect the sastra and distribute knowledge.

The pati, or husband, is worshipable by the wife. At the time of marriage, a father performs kanya-dana, or giving his virgin daughter in charity to her prospective husband. The husband must protect, care for, and maintain her, and in return, she must render service and regard him as her main object of love and respect.

A guest, however, is to be respected by all. To illustrate this point Pancatantra relates a story. In a particular tree in the forest lived a pigeon and his wife. Once a hunter captured the female pigeon as she was out searching for food. That evening while the hunter was returning home, a heavy storm suddenly erupted, showering rain and hail, and as darkness fell, the hunter lost his way. Shivering with cold, the drenched hunter took shelter under the very tree where the pigeon couple lived. While sitting there, the hunter cried out, “I take shelter of whoever lives in this tree.”

The husband of the captured pigeon was roosting atop the tree, thinking about his wife and speaking to himself about her chastity. Hearing him from within the hunter’s cage, she called out, “My dear husband, please do not lament. By the will of fate this hunter has captured me and has taken shelter of you. As he is your guest, it is your duty to mitigate his hunger and cold, even at the expense of your own life. Please do not be envious of him for having captured me. "

Hearing this, the pigeon approached the hunter with folded hands and said, “Welcome, my dear sir. Tel! me how I can serve you.”

The hunter replied, “O pigeon, I am cold and hungry, so please do something to relieve this misery.”

The pigeon fetched some wood and dry leaves in his beak, then, retrieving a burning splinter from a nearby village, started a fire. While deliberating on how to feed his guest, the pigeon realized he had nothing to offer, so he offered himself by jumping into the fire. At this, the hunter was moved and pondered, “Just see what a sinner I am. Even though I hold his wile captive, this pigeon has sacrificed his life to feed me.”

Inspired by the pigeon’s self-sacrifice, he decided to give up his abominable profession and lead a saintly life. He broke open the cage and released the female pigeon, who was lamenting the loss of her husband. Not being able to bear his separation, she jumped into the fire to join him. At that moment, a celestial plane arrived and carried them both to the heavenly planets.

### Text 114

*uttamasyāpi varṇasya nīco 'pi gṛham āgataḥ*  
*pūjanīyo yathā-yogyaṁ sarva-deva-mayo 'tithiḥ*

**Перевод:**

**Even if a low class man visits the house of a superior, he should be well-received, because a guest is the embodiment of all the demigods.**

**Комментарий:**

In this connection Srimad-Bhagavatam narrates the story of king Rantideva. He was so devoted to his guests that he would satisfy their requests, no matter the amount of personal inconvenience or suffering. One morning, after fasting for forty-eight days, Rantideva was brought some water and food prepared with milk and ghee, but just as he and his family were about to eat, a brahmana guest arrived. Because Rantideva perceived the presence of the Supreme Godhead everywhere, and in every living entity, he received the guest with faith and respect and gave him a share of the food. The brahmana ate his share and left.

Thereafter, having divided the remaining food with his relatives, Rantideva was just about to eat his own share when a sudra arrived. Seeing the sudra in relationship with the Supreme Personality of Godhead, Rantideva gave his guest a share of the food. After the sudra went away, another guest arrived, surrounded by dogs, and said, “O king, I and my company of dogs are very hungry. Please give us something to eat.”

With great respect, Rantideva offered the balance of food to his guests — the dogs and the master of the dogs. The king offered them all respects and obeisances.

Now only enough water for one person was left of the king’s meal. Just as he was about to drink it, a candala appeared and said, “O King, although I am lowborn, kindly give me some drinking water.”

Aggrieved at hearing the pitiable words of the fatigued candala, Maharaja Rantideva spoke the following nectarean words. “I do not pray to the Supreme Personality of Godhead for the eight perfections of mystic yoga, nor for salvation from repeated birth and death. I want only to stay among all the living entities and suffer all distresses on their behalf, so that they may be freed from suffering. By offering my water to maintain the life of this candala, who is struggling to live, I have been freed from all hunger, thirst, fatigue, trembling of the body, moroseness, distress, lamentation, and illusion.”

Having spoken thus, Rantideva, although on the verge of death because of thirst, gave his own portion of water to the candala without hesitation, for the king was naturally very kind and sober. Demigods like Lord Brahma and Lord Siva, who can satisfy all materially ambitious men by giving them the rewards they desire, then manifested their own identities before Rantideva, for it was they who had presented themselves as the brahmana, sudra, candala and so on.

*vāyaso 'vadat-sakhe! manthara! sa-viśeṣa-pūjām asami vidhehi, yato 'yaṁ puṇya-karmaṇāṁ dhurīṇaḥ kāruṇya-ratnākaro hiraṇyaka-nāmā mūṣika-rājaḥ etasya guṇa-stutiṁ jihvā-sahasra-dvayenāpi yadi sarpa-rājaḥ kadācit kartuṁ samarthaḥ syāt ity uktvā citragrīvopākhyānaṁ varṇitavān tato mantharaḥ sādaraṁ hiraṇyakaṁ sampūjyāha-bhadra! ātmano nirjana-vanāgamana-kāraṇam ākhyātum arhasi? hiraṇyako 'vadat-kathayāmi, śrūyatām*

**The crow said, “My dear friend Manthara, please offer special honor to this king of mice Hiranyaka, as he is foremost among the pious and an ocean of mercy. The king of serpents cannot adequately glorify his good qualities even with his two thousand tongues.”**  
**He then narrated the episode about Citragriva. Then, after respectfully entertaining Hiranyaka, Manthara said, “May I know why you have come to this solitary forest?”**  
**Hiranyaka replied, “Please listen.”**

## Story 4

*asti campakābhidhānāyāṁ nagaryāṁ parivrājakāvasathaḥ tatra cūḍākarṇo nāma parivrājakaḥ prativasati sa ca bhojanāvaśiṣṭa-bhikṣānna-sahitaṁ bhikṣāpātraṁ nāgadantake'vasthāpya svapiti ahaṁ ca tad annam utplutya utplutya pratyahaṁ bhakṣayāmi anantaraṁ tasya priya-suhṛd vīṇākarṇo nāma parivrājakaḥ samāyātaḥ, tena saha nānā-kathā-prasaṅgāvasthito mama trāsārthaṁ jarjara-vaṁśa-khaṇḍena cūḍākarṇo bhūmim atāḍayat taṁ tathāvidhaṁ dṛṣṭvā vīṇākarṇa uvāca-sakhe! kim iti mama kathā-virakto 'nyāsakto bhavān?*

**Перевод:**
