**In an asrama in the city of Campaka lived a hermit named Cudakarna. He used to hang his begging bowl, with scraps of food, on a wooden peg before going to sleep. By jumping again and again, I used to eat that food every day. One day, his dear hermit friend Vinakarna came to visit. While conversing with him, Cudakarna tapped the ground with an old broken bamboo staff to drive me away. Vinakarna said, “My dear friend, what’s wrong? Aren’t you interested in what Pm saying? Your mind seems to be wandering elsewhere.”**

### Text 115

*mukhaṁ prasannaṁ vimalā ca dṛṣṭiḥ*  
*kathānurāgo madhurā ca vāṇī*  
*sneho 'dhikaḥ sambhrama-darśanaṁ ca*  
*sadānuraktasya janasya lakṣma*

**Перевод:**

**A smiling face, affectionate glances, attentive listening, a sweet voice, great affection, and repeated respectful gazing — these are the symptoms of a beloved.**

**Комментарий:**

The face is the index of the mind. By studying the movements of the eyes, expressions on the face, and the tone of voice, one can understand the mind of a person. This verse describes the symptoms of an attentive person, and the next verse, the symptoms of a disinterested person.

### Text 116

*adṛṣṭi-dānaṁ kṛta-pūrva-nāśanam*  
*ānanaṁ duścaritānukīrtanam*  
*kathā-prasaṅgena ca nāma-vismṛtir*  
*virakta-bhāvasya janasya lakṣaṇam*

**Перевод:**

**Avoiding eye contact, being ungrateful, being disrespectful, repeatedly pointing out ill-behavior, forgetting another’s name during conversation — these are the symptoms of one who is indifferent.**

**Комментарий:**

*cūḍākarṇenoktam-bhadra! nāhaṁ viraktaḥ, kintu paśya ayaṁ mūṣiko mamāpakārī sadā pātrasthaṁ bhikṣānnam utplutya bhakṣayati vīṇākarṇo nāgadantam avalokyāha-katham ayaṁ mūṣikaḥ svalpa-balo 'py etāvad dūram utpatati? tad atra kenāpi kāraṇena bhavitavyam ”*

**Cudakarna said, “My dear friend, I am not indifferent. But this mouse always works against me by jumping up to eat from my begging bowl.” Looking at the peg, Vinakarna said, “How is it that this weak mouse can jump so high? There must be some reason.”**

*kṣaṇaṁ vicintya parivrājakenoktam-kāraṇaṁ cātra dhana-bāhulyam eva pratibhāti "*

**After contemplating a moment, the Sannyasi said, “The reason appears to be an abundance of wealth, for...”**

### Text 117

*dhanavān balavān loke sarvaḥ sarvatra sarvadā*  
*prabhutvaṁ dhana-mūlaṁ hi rājñām apy upajāyate*

**Перевод:**

**Rich men are ever powerful in this world. Even a king’s authoritative strength springs from wealth.**

**Комментарий:**

The importance of wealth is explained in the next seven verses. It is said that beauty is potent, but wealth is omnipotent. Although money is not everything, its importance in the material world cannot be underestimated. The omnipotent Lord keeps Laksmi Devi, the embodiment of all opulence, on His chest. Even great demigods like Brahma await Laksmi’s merciful glance. Wealth is best used in the service of the Lord; otherwise it causes degradation. Lord Kṛṣṇa lists fifteen defects of wealth:

*arthasya sādhane siddhe*  
*utkarṣe rakṣaṇe vyaye*  
*nāśopabhoga āyāsas*  
*trāsaś cintā bhramo nṛṇām*  
*steyaṁ hiṁsānṛtaṁ dambhaḥ*  
*kāmaḥ krodhaḥ smayo madaḥ*  
*bhedo vairam aviśvāsaḥ*  
*saṁspardhā vyasanāni ca*  
*ete pañcadaśānarthā*  
*hy artha-mūlā matā nṛṇām*  
*tasmād anartham arthākhyaṁ*  
*śreyo-'rthī dūratas tyajet*

**In the earning, attainment, increase, protection, expense, loss, and enjoyment of wealth, all men experience great labor, fear, anxiety and delusion. Theft, violence, speaking lies, duplicity, lust, anger, perplexity, pride, quarreling, enmity, faithlessness, envy and the dangers caused by gambling, intoxication, and illicit sex are the fifteen undesirable qualities that contaminate men because of greed for wealth. Although these qualities are undesirable, men falsely ascribe value to them. One desiring to achieve the real benefit of life should therefore remain aloof from undesirable material wealth. (Bhag. 11. 23. 17-19)**

One who uses wealth for the Lord’s service without being unnecessarily attached will not be influenced by the defects of wealth. The Isopanisad offers good advice in this regard:

*isavasyam idam sarvam yat kinca jagatyam jagat*  
*tena tyaktena bhunjitha ma grdhah kasyasvid dhanam*

**Everything animate or inanimate that is within the universe is controlled and owned by the Lord. One should therefore accept only those things necessary for himself, which are set aside as his quota, and one should not accept other things, knowing well to whom they belong.**

Problems arise when one wants to enjoy Wealth independently of the Lord. An intelligent person, understanding that everything has both good and bad usage, does not reject objects because of their negative uses. Rather, he uses them in such a way that he is not polluted by them, as described in Isopanisad. Rejecting objects for fear of misuse shows weakness of the person who rejects them, while utilizing them properly shows his genius. Even though the Lord describes the defects of wealth, He never renounces it.

*tataḥ khanitram ādāya tena parivrājakena vivaraṁ khanitvā cira-sañcitaṁ mama dhanaṁ gṛhītam tataḥ prabhṛti pratyahaṁ nija-śakti-hīnaḥ sattvotsāha-rahitaḥ svāhāram apy utpādayitum akṣamaḥ sann āsaṁ mandaṁ mandam upasarpan cūḍākarṇenāvalokitaḥ tatas tenoktam —*

**The Sannyasi then dug out my residence with a shovel and confiscated the wealth I had carefully kept for so long. Thus I daily grew weaker, lost all enthusiasm, and became unable to even collect my own food. In that fearful state, I was slowly walking away when Cudakarna spotted me and said...**

### Text 118

*dhanena balavān loko dhanād bhavati paṇḍitaḥ*  
*paśyainaṁ mūṣikaṁ pāpaṁ svajāti-samatāṁ gatam*

**Перевод:**

**In this world wealth makes one powerful, and for that, one is even considered a scholar. Just sec how this vile mouse is now equal to those of his own kind.**

**Комментарий:**

Money is power. In an earlier commentary the power of money to turn bad qualities into good ones was shown, citing a passage from the works of Karl Marx. Since common men naturally respect the wealthy, it is said that a rich man’s joke is always funny. Sometimes the wealthy hire scholars to do research or write books in their name, to pass themselves off as scholars. Previously, many kings in India used to purchase the works of panditas and publish them under their own names.

All perfections take shelter of wealth. When Hiranyaka had wealth, he was powerful and superior to his fellow mice; but when his wealth was confiscated, he lost all vigor and was no longer able to reach the mendicant’s begging bowl. Wealth gives one a special type of temperament which can be understood only through experience, in the sense that it is impossible for one who has never known wealth to imagine how the truly wealthy view the world.

kiṁ ca — Moreover,

### Text 119

*arthena tu vihīnasya puruṣasyālpa-medhasaḥ*  
*kriyā sarvā vinaśyanti grīṣme kusarito yathā*

**Перевод:**

**Just like a small river that dries up in the summer, people lacking intelligence and wealth are unsuccessful in all endeavors.**

**Комментарий:**

Intelligence and wealth are the means to achieve one’s desired goals in this material world. Foolish and poverty-stricken persons are unfortunate and can never be successful. In India we see that in many spheres important work cannot be accomplished without paying bribes. Furthermore, laws can be amended to suit the fancy of the rich. Often one cannot get justice in court without money. Just as a river without water is useless, a person without wealth and intelligence is considered useless. Nirdhanata sarvapadam aspadam, poverty is the seat of all miseries. Indeed wealth is considered a sign of intelligence.

aparaṁ ca — Also,

### Text 120

*yasyārthās tasya mitrāṇi yasyārthās tasya bāndhavāḥ*  
*yasyārthāḥ sa pumān loke yasyārthāḥ sa hi paṇḍitaḥ*

**Перевод:**

**He who possesses wealth, has friends and relatives and is considered a great soul. Indeed, a rich man is considered a scholar.**

**Комментарий:**

Anyac ca — Moreover,

### Text 121

*aputrasya gṛhaṁ śūnyaṁ san-mitra-rahitasya ca*  
*mūrkhasya ca diśaḥ śūnyāḥ sarva-śūnyā daridratā*

**Перевод:**

**The home is void for one with no son, as it is for one who lacks good friends; the directions are void for the fool, and for the pauper, everything is void.**

### Text 122

*dāridryān maraṇād vāpi dāridryam avaraṁ smṛtam*  
*alpa-kleśena maraṇaṁ dāridryam atiduḥsaham*

**Перевод:**

**Of poverty and death, poverty is worse; death happens only one time, but from poverty one suffers always.**

**Комментарий:**

This verse depicts the mood of a person who has lost his wealth, like Hiranyaka. Otherwise this verse is not really true. One who is rich and one who is impoverished may think like this, but there are many thoroughly disturbed and miserable rich people. They are always in anxiety that someone may kidnap them or steal their money. The poor rarely fear thieves and dacoits and are often humble and open to receiving saintly persons. In contrast, the rich keep many dogs, servants, and fences to prevent people from approaching them. Moreover, if one survives, one may become rich. Death is not the solution to poverty.

Poverty tests one’s character as it generally nurtures bad qualities. There is no virtue that poverty does not destroy. On the other hand, it is not necessary that wealth makes one virtuous. In fact, only a few rich people are virtuous. Indeed people often get rich by dishonest means. As mentioned earlier, wealth also brings negative side effects such as pride, which is one of the greatest enemies on the spiritual path. Pride is like the shadow of opulence. Thus it is said yasyaham anugrhnami harisye tad dhanam sanaih, that when the Lord is especially mercifully inclined towards us, He takes away our wealth.

### Text 123

*tānīndriyāṇy avikalāni tad eva nāma*  
*sā buddhir apratihatā vacanaṁ tad eva*  
*arthoṣmaṇā virahitaḥ puruṣaḥ sa eva*  
*anyaḥ kṣaṇena bhavatīti vicitram etat*

**Перевод:**

**Although one’s sensory power, name, sharp intelligence, speech, and body do not change upon losing wealth, still, one becomes quite a different person. This is indeed strange.**

**Комментарий:**

Upon losing wealth, a person’s name, form, speech, mind, and intelligence remain the same as before, yet one’s attitude and behavior may change radically. When the sons of Kuvera were intoxicated by opulence and frolicking naked with heavenly damsels, Narada Muni’s appearance before them didn’t faze them. Yet when Narada cursed them to lose their wealth and heavenly position, their demeanor changed drastically. Narada says that while wealth blinds one, poverty restores his ability to see, asataḥ śrī-madāndhasya dāridryaṁ param añjanam (Bhag. 10. 10. 13). Thus when one’s pride is lost, his vision changes.

This principle applies, however, only to materialistic people. Since saintly persons are unattached, they can use wealth in Kṛṣṇa’s service without being corrupted by its defects, or they can be content to serve Kṛṣṇa in poverty. Without being genuinely detached, however, wealth becomes a hindrance to spiritual progress. Therefore, Caitanya Mahaprabhu advises that those who want to progress in pure bhakti should avoid wealth, followers, women, and praise. What he really means is that one should not be attached to these items. However, their physical absence is of no help if one hankers for them.

*etat sarvam ākarṇya mayālocitaṁ-mamānnāvasthānam ayuktam idānīm tathā coktam —*

**Hearing these words of Cudakarna, I thought it unwise to stay in that place (in a poverty stricken condition. ) As it is said...**

### Text 124

*atyanta-vimukhe daive vyarthe yatne ca pauruṣe*  
*manasvino daridrasya vanād anyat kutaḥ sukham*

**Перевод:**

**When valor and endeavor have failed, and fate is unfavorable, an honorable poor man Finds happiness only in the forest.**

### Text 125

*manasvī miryate kāmaṁ kārpaṇyaṁ na tu gacchati*  
*api nirvāṇam āyāti nānalo yāti śītatām*

**Перевод:**

**As fire naturally exhausts itself, but is never cool, so a man of honor would rather die willingly than act disgracefully.**

**Комментарий:**

An honorable person never behaves viciously even in adversity. He also prefers to tolerate poverty than to beg, regretting only his inability to help others in need. In this connection there was a famous and charitable Seventh Century poet named Magha, who wrote Sisupala- vadha. Because of his charitable disposition, brahmanas always begged from him.

Once some brahmanas requested alms from Magha, but he had nothing to offer them. So he composed a beautiful verse and sent it via his wife to the king, hoping for some reward. The king was pleased with the verse and rewarded Magha’s wife abundantly. On her way home some beggars requested charity, and being very generous, she gave it all to them. She arrived home empty-handed and related what happened. Magha was both happy and morose — happy that his wife acted generously, but morose at not having anything to give the brahmanas. Thus lamenting, he gave up his life, owing to his inability to help others.

### Text 126

*kusuma-stavakasyeva dve vṛttī tu manasvinaḥ*  
*sarveṣāṁ mūrdhni vā tiṣṭhed viśīryeta vane'thavā*

**Перевод:**

**A man of honor and a bouquet of flowers rest in only two places: either at the head of all, or withering away in the forest.**

**Комментарий:**

Lord Kṛṣṇa says that for a man of honor, dishonor is worse than death, sambhāvitasya cākīrtir

maraṇād atiricyate (Bg. 2. 34). Rather than accept a wretched position in society, an honorable man will prefer to live in a solitary place, keeping his honor intact.

*yac cānyasmai etad vṛttānta-kathanaṁ tad apy anucitam yataḥ —*

**Even to describe this to others is inapropriate. Because...**

### Text 127

*artha-nāśaṁ manas-tāpaṁ gṛhe duścaritāni ca*  
*vañcanaṁ cāpamānaṁ ca matimān na prakāśayet*

**Перевод:**

**An intelligent person should never reveal loss of wealth, mental distress, misbehavior in the family, or that he was deceived, or insulted.**

**Комментарий:**

These things should only be discussed with confidential associates to relieve tension, duhkham nivedya sukhino bhavanti. Revealing our misfortunes to common men is never beneficial and will subject us to ridicule. Real friends will try to help by empathizing and offering good advice.

*yac cātraiva yācñayā jīvanaṁ tad apy atīva-garhitam.*

**It is also abominable to live here by begging.**

### Text 128

*varaṁ vibhava-hīnena prāṇaiḥ santarpito 'nalaḥ*  
*nopacāra-paribhraṣṭaḥ kṛpaṇaḥ prārthyate janaḥ*

**Перевод:**

**It is better for a poor man to enter fire than to beg from misers possessing bad character.**

**Комментарий:**

Begging from misers is not recommended as they either offer insufficient renumeration or none at all. Sometimes they humiliate one by promising something and making one return several times while they delay keeping their promise. The Pancatantra (2. 75) states, dātā laghur api sevyo bhavati na kṛpaṇo mahān api samṛddhyā kūpo’ntaḥ-svādu-jalaḥ prītyai lokasya na samudraḥ, one should approach a benevolent person instead of a wealthy miser, for a well satisfies people with sweet water, but not the ocean. If the miser is vile, he will be insulting. Although he gives insignificantly, he will humiliate the receiver by advertizing it. Since the miser gives begrudgingly, such charity is in the mode of passion, which results in misery, rajasas tu phalam dukham. The poet Kalidas also advises (Meghaduta 6), yacna mogha varam adhigune nadhame labdha-kama, better to have a request rejected by the meritorious than to have it accepted by a wretch.

### Text 129

*dāridryād dhriyam eti hrī-parigataḥ sattvāt paribhraśyate*  
*niḥsattvaṁ paribhūyate paribhavān nirvedam āpadyate*  
*nirviṇṇaḥ śucam eti śoka-phihito buddhyā parityajyate*  
*nirbuddhiḥ kṣayam ety aho nidhanatā sarvāpadām āspadam*

**Перевод:**

**Owing to poverty one feels ashamed and thus loses courage; one who is discouraged is never honored; dishonor results in dejection, which leads to lamentation; lamentation destroys intelligence, and thus one’s life is destroyed. Alas, poverty is the seat of all misfortune.**

**Комментарий:**

Just as pride usually accompanies wealth, so dejection accompanies poverty. Poverty is certainly a curse, but not everyone is overwhelmed by it. Many poor people have reversed their circumstances and succeeded as writers, scientists, poets, and businessmen. For those who are honest and industrious, poverty is but an impetus to improve their lot.

kiṁ ca — Also

### Text 130

*varaṁ maunaṁ kāryaṁ na ca vacanam uktaṁ yad anṛtaṁ*  
*varaṁ klaibyaṁ puṁsāṁ na ca para-kalatrābhigamanam*  
*varaṁ prāṇa-tyāgo na ca piśuna-vākyeṣv abhirucir*  
*varaṁ bhikṣāśitvaṁ na ca para-dhanāsvādana-sukham*

**Перевод:**

**Silence is better than speaking untruth; impotence is better than adultery; death is better than delighting in evil words; and begging is better than enjoying another’s wealth.**

**Комментарий:**

This verse explains that it is better not to use a facility than to misuse it. The ability to speak is one of the greatest gifts human beings have and is a very powerful tool. Many leaders created spiritual or political revolutions by using eloquent speech. The voice, which is one of the working senses, should be used to transfer knowledge and remove ignorance, which is the seat of all evil. It is a tragedy to use the voice for telling lies or fault-finding.

### Text 131

*varaṁ śūnyā śālā na ca khalu varo duṣṭa-vṛṣabho*  
*varaṁ veśyā patnī na punar avinītā kula-vadhūḥ*  
*varaṁ vāso 'raṇye na punar avivekādhipa-pure*  
*varaṁ prāṇa-tyāgo na punar adhamānām upagamaḥ*

**Перевод:**

**Better an empty cowshed than one with an aggressive bull; better to wed a genteel prostitute than a well-bred shrew; better to live in the forest than the state of a foolish king; and better to die than to beseech low class people.**

**Комментарий:**

A prostitute of good character — well spoken, polite, considerate, and so forth — makes a better wife than an ill-behaved woman from a good family. Out of pride, the latter may be sharp-tongued, irritable, and difficult to please. Such association does not bring happiness in this life or in the next. The same holds for keeping company with low class people.

Api ca — Also.

### Text 132

*seveva mānam akhilaṁ jyotsneva tamo jareva lāvaṇyam*  
*hari-hara-katheva duritaṁ guṇa-śatam apy arthitā harati*

**Перевод:**

**Begging destroys hundreds of good qualities, service destroys honor, the moon destroys darkness, old age destroys beauty, and the pastimes of Lord Hari and Hara destroy sins.**

**Комментарий:**

To live by begging is demeaning, and a beggar cannot fit into society. However, in Vedic culture brahmacaris and sannyasis are allowed to beg, as they contribute to society’s welfare. Out of gratitude, people feel honored to support them.

In this world, those with many subordinates are honored, but those in subordinate roles are not. One’s prestige increases if he is subordinate to no one. Generally a loving relationship does not exist between servant and master, as the servant is motivated by a salary and may even scheme to usurp his master’s position. Thus everyone in the material world is striving to be master.

In the spiritual world, the opposite holds true — service is the highest goal. The more service one does, the more elevated he is. Even Lord Kṛṣṇa, the supreme object of service, takes great pleasure in offering menial service to His devotees, such as driving Arjuna’s chariot. Because of the loving relationship between servant and master, each one derives more pleasure from serving than being served.

*tat kim ahaṁ para-piṇḍena ātmānaṁ poṣayāmi? kaṣṭaṁ bhoḥ! tad api dvitīyaṁ mṛtyu-dvāram*

**What then? Shall I maintain myself with another’s food? Oh! How miserable! That is another gate to death.**

### Text 133

*rogī cira-pravāsī parānna-bhojī parāvasatha-śāyī*  
*yaj jīvati tan maraṇaṁ yan maraṇaṁ so 'sya viśrāmaḥ*

**Перевод:**

**These men are dead while living: A diseased man; one who stays away from home for a long time (owing to business); one who lives on another's food; and one who lives in another’s house. For them, dying is a relief.**

**Комментарий:**

*ity ālocyāpi lobhāt punar api tadīyam annaṁ grahītuṁ graham akaravam tathā coktam-*

**Even after considering this, owing to greed, the desire to steal food from the Sannyasi still sprang up. As it is said...**

### Text 134

*lobhena buddhiś calati lobho janayate tṛṣām*  
*tṛṣārto duḥkham āpnoti paratreha ca mānavaḥ*

**Перевод:**

**Intelligence is perturbed by greed, and from greed springs material desire. From material desire one suffers both in this world and in the next.**

**Комментарий:**

*tato 'haṁ mandaṁ mandam upasarpaṁs tena vīṇākarṇena jarjara-vaṁśa-khaṇḍena tāḍitaś cācintayam-lubdho hy asantuṣṭo niyatam ātma-drohī bhavati tathā ca — ”*

**While crawling away, Vinakarna beat me with his old bamboo stick. I thought, “A greedy man is always discontented and is thus a traitor to his own self, for...”**

### Text 135

*dhana-lubdho hy asantuṣṭo 'niyatātmājitendriyaḥ*  
*sarvā evāpadas tasya yasya tuṣṭaṁ na mānasam*

**Перевод:**

**One greedy for wealth is discontented, and cannot control his mind and senses. Such a dissatisfied person faces all sorts of miseries.**

**Комментарий:**

In Srimad-Bhagavatam Lord Vamana says: “O my dear King, even the entirety of whatever there may be within the three worlds to satisfy one’s senses cannot satisfy a person whose senses are uncontrolled. One should be satisfied with whatever he achieves by his previous destiny, for discontent can never bring happiness. A person who is not self- controlled will not be happy even with possessing the three worlds.” (8. 19. 21, 24)

Desire is the root of all suffering. Thus only a self-satisfied person is free from disturbance. This is illustrated in the story of Pingala:

*āśā hi paramaṁ duḥkhaṁ*  
*nairāśyaṁ paramaṁ sukham*  
*yathā sañchidya kāntāśāṁ*  
*sukhaṁ suṣvāpa piṅgalā*

**Material desire is undoubtedly the cause of the greatest unhappiness, and freedom from such desire is the cause of the greatest happiness. Therefore, completely cutting off her desire to enjoy so-called lovers, Pingala very happily went to sleep. (Bhag. 11. 8. 44)**

Pingala was a prostitute who lived in Mithila (East India). One night, after decorating herself provocatively, she stood at the doorway of her house, enticing men to enjoy with her for a fee. As each one approached, she felt hopeful, but after several hours of refusals, she became depressed. The whole night passed in this way, until she felt completely disgusted, and detached herself from all desires. Then she became peaceful and happy.

Kṛṣṇa instructed Arjuna, “For him who has conquered the mind, the mind is the best of friends; but for one who has failed to do so, his mind will remain the greatest enemy (Bg. 6. 6). While focusing on so many external enemies, we fail to recognize that our greatest enemy lies within. Indeed, the mind addicted to material hankering is our greatest enemy, yet we do little or nothing to control it.

Some people don’t realize they could control their mind if they would take responsibility for their thoughts. Taking the mind to be an incessant flow of thoughts, a stream of consciousness, they feel helplessly swept along by the flow. Such people experience life as victims and are perpetually in distress. Until they get the association of saintly devotees, they cannot imagine that the mind can be made peaceful. By seeing someone who is genuinely peaceful, they may become interested, and begin a new direction in their otherwise miserable life.

About controlling the mind, a doctor made the point this way: “If you don’t believe that you are the one who is responsible for your thoughts, then I say, ‘If it isn’t you who put those thoughts in your head, who did? What I want you to do is find that person and send him or her to me for therapy, and you'll get better. ’’ His point is that we have the power to choose what we allow into our minds. Even when a thought just “pops” into our minds we have the power to make it go away by focusing on thoughts we want. Therefore we can control and are responsible for our mental world.

In bhakti-yoga, the goal is not to stop the incessant flow of thoughts or desires, because the bhakta understands that as eternal souls we are eternally conscious; hence the stream of consciousness is inherent in us and attempting to stop all thought is futile. Even if attained, the success is only temporary, for the stream must flow again. The goal for devotees of the Lord, therefore, is to reorient their desires from the mundane sphere to the spiritual world. For such persons the mind is the best friend. Lord Kṛṣṇa hails them as the topmost transcendentalists (Bg. 6. 47).

### Text 136

*sarvāḥ sampattasyas tasya santuṣṭaṁ yasya mānasam*  
*upānad-gūḍha-pādasya nanu carmāvṛteva bhūḥ*

**Перевод:**

**A person whose mind is satisfied has won all opulence and happiness; the whole earth appears covered with leather to one who wears shoes.**

**Комментарий:**

The root cause of disturbance is the uncontrolled mind. Yet no modern school or college teaches how to control it. On the contrary, billions of dollars are spent yearly on advertisements to tantalize the mind, thus creating endless hankering that disturbs even peaceful men. Ironically, grand seminars and conferences are held to establish world peace without focusing on the cause of our disquiet. Peace of mind cannot be legislated; it also cannot be achieved by external arrangements. Peace of mind can be attained only by association with Vaisnavas and reading devotional literature. Then the orientation of our desires will change and our minds will become peaceful. Otherwise, peace will not come until the universe is annihilated; but that peace is also temporary for the universe will be created again and the conditioned souls begin again to live out their karma.

### Text 137

*santoṣāmṛta-tṛptānāṁ yat sukhaṁ śānta-cetasām*  
*kutas tad-dhana-lubdhānām itaś cetaś ca dhāvatām*

**Перевод:**

**The happiness enjoyed by the peaceful, who drink the nectar of satisfaction, cannot be had by greedy men, who run here and there amassing wealth.**

**Комментарий:**

Materialistic people are ignorant of how to be happy, so they travel the world amassing wealth, thinking that having things will make them happy. They don’t realize that their having orientation — whether it means possessing gross sense objects; titles, name, and fame; or other living beings — is the very root of their dissatisfaction. They don’t know that another orientation is available, one in which the nectar of satisfaction is possible, not by having but by being.

In Bhagavad-gita Lord Kṛṣṇa discourages the having mode and encourages the being mode when He says, “An intelligent person does not take part in the sources of misery, which are due to contact with the material senses. O son of Kunti, such pleasures have a beginning and an end, and so the wise man does not delight in them... One whose happiness is within, who is active within, who rejoices within, and is illumined within, is actually the perfect mystic” (Bg. 5. 22, 24). Beyond even the being mode is the giving mode. This is the topmost perfection of human life and acme of happiness. The unalloyed devotees of the Lord achieve the apex of giving orientation. This the Lord indicates when He says, “The steadily devoted soul attains unadulterated peace because he offers the results of all activities to Me; whereas a person who is not in union with Me, who is greedy for the fruits of his labor, becomes entangled” (Bg. 5. 12).

### Text 138

*tenādhītaṁ śrutaṁ tena tena sarvam anuṣṭhitam*  
*yenāśāḥ pṛṣṭhataḥ kṛtvā nairāśyam avalambitam*

**Перевод:**

**One who is free from all desire and thus content, has studied all the scriptures and heard all there is to be heard (from his guru). He alone has performed all religious practices.**

**Комментарий:**

The purpose of studying scripture, performing religious duties, and so on, is not to become desireless, as is commonly believed; it is to become free from material desires. Those free from selfish desires need not undergo these processes, as they have already attained the result. They experience contentment just by giving up material desires. When they serve the Lord with body, mind, and words they experience something more profound than contentment — they experience the bliss of devotional service, bhaktyananda, which surpasses all other states of happiness.

### Text 139

*aseviteśvara-dvāram adṛṣṭa-viraha-vyatham*  
*anukta-klība-vacanaṁ dhanyaṁ kasyāpi jīvanam*

**Перевод:**

**Fortunate indeed is that rare person who does not have to knock on the door of the rich; who does not have to suffer separation from his relatives; and who does not have to lament or speak petty words.**

### Text 140

*na yojana-śataṁ dūraṁ vāhyamānasya tṛṣṇayā*  
*santuṣṭasya kara-prāpte'py arthe bhavati nādaraḥ*

**Перевод:**

**A person who is troubled by material desires does not consider a thousand miles a great distance (to travel for fulfilling his desires), but one who is content does not even care for the wealth held in his hand.**

**Комментарий:**

Uncontrolled material desire, which impels people to travel great distances in a futile attempt to satisfy the senses, has transformed today’s world into a global village. Electronic gadgets, speedy communication and speedy travel have done nothing to bring peace into society; rather the lot of man has become more stressful. The solutions offered by science and technology, our substitutes for guru and God, simply complicate matters. Yet modern man has not understood that the heart of the problem is material desire (trsna), which make us restless and driven.

Material desires lie within the mind and are dispelled only when one develops attachment to Kṛṣṇa. Bhagavad-gita explains, “A person who is not disturbed by the incessant flow of desires... can alone achieve peace, and not the man who always strives to satisfy such desires” (Bg. 2. 70). The solution, as stated here, is to tolerate material desires, not entertain them. Kṛṣṇa also teaches that until the mind is steady, there is no possibility of peace (Bg. 2. 66). When the mind is controlled one becomes content and consequently disinterested in all material pleasures. This is confirmed in (Niti-dvisastika 35):

*kim dravyaih kim vibhavaih kim sarair vistarair nrnam tesam*  
*yair niyama-bhajana-stham santosa-rasayanam pitam*

**Of what avail are precious valuables, affluence, excellences, and progress to those who have drunk the nectar of contentment from the pot of self control?**

*tad atra avasthocita-kārya-paricchedaḥ śreyān*

**Thus it is advisable to act according to the situation.**

### Text 141

*ko dharmo bhūta-dayā kiṁ saukhyaṁ nityam aroginā jagati*  
*kaḥ snehaḥ sad-bhāvaḥ kiṁ pāṇḍityaṁ paricchedaḥ*

**Перевод:**

**What is religion in this world, but compassion for other living entities? What is happiness, but freedom from disease? What is affection, but benevolent feelings? What is scholarship, but discrimination?**

**Комментарий:**

Compassion is the cure for the disease of selfishness, the root of materialism. Therefore compassion is the essence of religion. Out of ignorance, the conditioned living entity mistakes the subtle and gross bodies for the self. This gives rise to the self-centered view that keeping the body young, having longevity, and making oneself appear the source of abundant knowledge and perfect qualities will make one happy. Since none of these are possible in the material body, which is temporary (asat), full of ignorance (acit), and naturally full of miseries (nirananda), the tendency to cheat comes to the forefront. This gives rise to evils such as exploitation, hoarding, cruelty, violence, hatred, envy, and so on. Thus the selfish living entity creates misery for himself and others, making the world a hellish place. To rectify this, all religions teach, as the essence of understanding, compassion for others. The more one gives up selfishness, the more one is compassionate, the more one’s real personality manifests.

The verse states that freedom from disease is real happiness. If health is ruined, one cannot be happy even if the whole world is at his command. Yet few appreciate how health is the basis of happiness. Pseudo transcendentalists especially do not understand that good health is the foundation of all human achievements, dharmartha-kama-dharmartha-kama moksanam arogyam mulam uttamam. Both materialists and spiritualists require good health to achieve their goals. Of course, until disease strikes, health is taken for granted. The Lord gave the medical science of Ayurveda specifically for spiritual practitioners, as disease can be a formidable obstacle to spiritual progress.

The third point of this verse — real affection is benevolent feelings — must be properly understood, otherwise there is a risk that sentimental or arbitrary feelings are taken as genuine affection. Genuine benevolence is to illuminate the path of elevation to pure devotional service for another. This may require encouragement, chastisement, praise, or even laughing at the person; however, as long as the purpose of such action is to elevate the person spiritually, one is being affectionate and benevolent. Any benevolent action motivated to other ends is not real benevolence nor affection, because it entangles both the giver and receiver in karma. Both good and bad karma entangles us in repeated birth and death.

Finally, knowledge has its value for guiding action. Knowledge without application does not have much utility. Generally it is seen that men of knowledge have a disinclination for acting, because the culture of knowledge and the life of action require different modes of being. Yet men of knowledge are the most qualified to act, for they can discriminate between right and wrong action. It is therefore said that “Men who lead have no time to read, yet only those who read are fit to lead.” Vedic culture solves this problem by having the men of words, the brahmanas, guide the men of action, ksatriyas. Then the function of scholarship and right action are reconciled.

In Kali-yuga, however, as materialistic society worsens, the measure of a man is in terms of material accumulation, thus men of words are undervalued. In America, for example, men of action pioneered and built the nation into one of the greatest on earth, but one result is that less value is placed on intellectual achievement. The passionate, achievement-oriented men of action see the men of words as a liability, as idle meditators, not contributing to society's goals. This is a serious mistake. Men of words are as vital to society as the head is vital to the physical body.

This undervaluing of men of words can also happen in spiritual communities. When men of action are at the top, organizational goals overshadow the quality of life in the organization. The men of action feel that the men of words must serve their agenda and they stress visible achievements. In contrast, men of words stress development of virtue, right conduct, and so forth. When achieving is stressed, this can cost the integrity or purity of the group organism. If, on the other hand, men of words dominate, then overcoming the individual's obstacles to enlightenment is stressed, which can cause laxness in pursuing organizational goals and the community can lose its vitality. Hence the ideal is to balance individual and organizational needs by cooperating. If one discriminates like this and teaches it to others that is real scholarship.

tathā ca — And,

### Text 142

*paricchedo hi pāṇḍityaṁ yadāpannā vipattayaḥ*  
*apariccheda-kartṝṇāṁ vipadaḥ syuḥ pade pade*

**Перевод:**

**When adversity calls, wisdom lies in making practical decisions. Those who act indiscriminately are miserable at every step.**

**Комментарий:**

Few people use good discrimination. In youth, when discrimination is lacking, one wastes energy in childish pursuits. By the time one gains experience in discriminating, old age has set in and one is plagued by lack of enthusiasm. This is life’s paradox, or the method by which material nature distracts us from spiritual life.

In youth we should learn discrimination from experienced persons. An intelligent person learns by hearing about the experiences of others; a fool learns by personal experience; and the mad never learn. Books like Hitopadesa increase our wisdom by teaching discrimination, which can save us trouble, time, money, and anguish.

tathā hi — For example,

### Text 143

*tyajed ekaṁ kulasyārthe grāmasyārthe kulaṁ tyajet*  
*grāmaṁ janapadasyārthe ātmārthe pṛthivīṁ tyajet*

**Перевод:**

**One should abandon a family member for the sake of the family, the family for the village, the village for the district — but one should abandon the whole world to save one’s own self.**

**Комментарий:**

When Duryodhana was born, Vidura detected inauspicious omens and advised Dhrtarastra that his son would cause the destruction of the whole dynasty. In keeping with the ethic of this verse, Vidura urged that Duryodhana be killed to save the Kuru family. Out of attachment, Dhrtarastra did not follow this advice and all the Kauravas were destroyed in the Battle of Kuruksetra.

One should protect oneself, even at the expense of one’s country. This does not mean, however, that one should forsake his duty. For a ksatriya, real glory is in fighting for religious principles, not in fleeing to save his body. In terms of spiritual versus material life, on the other hand, because the human form is a rare opportunity to attain life’s highest goal, prema, or love of God, the highest principle is to save one’s self.

The living being has two kinds of duties, termed as constitutional and conditional. Constitutional duties pertain to one’s spiritual elevation. Conditional duties pertain to those we do for our material body and extended body, such as family, society, and nation. When these duties converge or run parallel to each other we may do them both; however, if they diverge, we must apply the principle of this verse and save ourselves by sticking to our constitutional duties.

aparaṁ ca — Moreover,

### Text 144

*pānīyaṁ vā nirāyāsaṁ svādvannaṁ vā bhayottaram*  
*vicāryaṁ khalu paśyāmi tat sukhaṁ yatra nirvṛtiḥ*

**Перевод:**

**Of water obtained without effort, or delicious food which creates fear of disease or death — after consideration, I certainly take that which is attained with ease as conducive to happiness.**

**Комментарий:**

*ity ālocyāhaṁ nirjana-vanam āgataḥ yataḥ—*

**Thinking like this I have come to this lonely forest. Because...**

### Text 145

*varaṁ vanaṁ vyāghra-gajendra-sevitaṁ*  
*drumālayaḥ patra-phalāmbu-bhakṣitam*  
*tṛṇāni śayyā vasanaṁ ca valkalaṁ*  
*na bandhu-madhye dhana-hīna-jīvanam*

**Перевод:**

**Better to live under a tree in a forest infested with tigers and elephants, where there is ample water, fruits to eat, grass for a bed, and treebark for clothes, than to live penniless among family members.**

**Комментарий:**

*Tatah asmat-punyodayad anena mitrenaham snehanuvrttyanugrhitah. Adhuna ca punya-paramparaya bhavad-asrayah svarga eva maya praptah. Yatah—*

**Fortunately, upon arriving in this forest I was befriended by this crow; and now by further good fortune, I have received the heavenly shelter of your holiness, because...**

### Text 146

*saṁsāra-viṣaya-vṛkṣasya dve eva rasavat phale*  
*kāvyāmṛta-rasāsvādaḥ saṅgamaḥ sajjanaiḥ saha*

**Перевод:**

**The poisonous tree of the material world yields only two succulent fruits — nectarean literature and saintly association.**

**Комментарий:**

Visa means poison and visaya indicates an object for sense gratification. Sense gratification is like poison as it kills one’s progress in spiritual life. Thus, the material world, which is full of sense gratification (visaya), is like a poisonous tree (visa-vrksa). But fortunately it has two succulent fruits — the scriptures and the devotees. We should not drink the poison of sense gratification, nor should we starve; let us drink the immortal juice of the scriptures, so we can relish happiness even in this material world, and in the next life achieve the spiritual world.

To gain information about the next life, one must go to these two sources. The author refers to scriptural study as immortal juice, kavyamrta-rasasvadah, urging everyone to drink it. Similarly, Srila Vyasa urges pibata bhagavatam rasam, drink the nectar of Srimad-Bhagavatam, the ripened fruit of the tree of Vedic literature. This nectar is easily available in the association of the saintly, thus we are advised to seek their association.

A sane man earns sufficiently to provide for his future. As we work hard during youth to provide for our later years, so in this life we should work to provide happiness for our next life. The optimal goal is to go beyond birth and death. As the wise Vidura also advises (Vidura-niti 3. 69):

*pūrve vayasi tatkuryādyena vṛddhasukhaṁ vaset*  
*yāvajjīvena tatkuryādyena pretya sukhaṁ vaset*

**Those with foresight make long-term plans: those that fail to do so perish. Similarly, those with the vision of eternity plan beyond this life by studying Vedic literature and associating with saintly persons.**

Although the previous verses emphasize the importance of acquiring wealth and the disadvantages of poverty, this and the following verse present the conclusion.

### Text 147

*sat-saṅgaḥ keśave bhaktir gaṅgāmbhasi nimajjanam*  
*asāre khalu saṁsāre trīṇi sārāṇi bhāvayet*

**Перевод:**

**In the useless material world there are only three valuable things: saintly association, devotion to Lord Kṛṣṇa, and bathing in the Ganges.**

**Комментарий:**

Bhagavad-gita offers supreme instruction (paramam vacah), and here the author offers a conclusion consistent with the Bhagavad-gita. Although saintly association and study of scripture are the succulent fruits of the poison tree of this world, saintly association is mentioned again in this verse because the author wants to emphasize that it is even more succulent than study of scripture, because without holy association one cannot understand the real mission of human life, which is devotion to Kesava. It is rare that one understands scripture conclusively unless one is guided. The Vedic system, therefore, is that one approaches a spiritual master in parampara, disciplic succession, and by the combination of service and inquiry, one gradually gets a thorough understanding of the goal of scriptural knowledge.

Bathing in the Ganges is also connected to gaining saintly association. When one goes to bathe in the Ganges, he may gain the association of saints, who generally live in holy places. This is the real purpose of bathing in the Ganges — it represents bathing in transcendental knowledge. As spiritual knowledge is gained only in the association of Sadhus, the essence of life is to associate with them and study scriptures under their guidance.

The scriptures are the highest authority and sadhus are those who follow them. No living being or even an avatara, can transgress scriptural authority. Sometimes it appears that Lord Kṛṣṇa or Brahma transgresses the regulations of the scriptures, but in reality they do not. The purpose underlying their activities can be understood from saintly scholars. One should not imitate the activities of the Lord or great persons, but should follow their instructions, which are based on sastra.

manthara uvāca — Manthara said...

### Text 148

*arthāḥ pāda-rajopamā giri-nadī-vegopamaṁ yauvanam*  
*āyuṣyaṁ jala-bindu-lola-capalaṁ phenopamaṁ jīvanam*  
*dharmaṁ yo na karoti niścala-matiḥ svargārgalodghāṭanaṁ*  
*paścāt-tāpa-hato jarā-pariṇataḥ śokāgninā dahyate*

**Перевод:**

**Wealth is temporary, like specks of dust on the feet. Youth flows away as swiftly as a mountain river. Life is like a quivering bead of water on a lily leaf. This body is transitory like foam. Therefore, one who does not open the gates to heaven by religious deeds, will be overtaken by old age and remorse and thus burn in the fire of lamentation.**

**Комментарий:**

Wealth, youth, and the material body are all temporary, yet they can be used for permanent gain. Prahlada Maharaja says, durlabhaṁ mānuṣaṁ janma tad apy adhruvam arthadam, “Human birth is very rare, and although temporary, can take one to the ultimate goal” (Bhag. 7. 6. 1). The example is given that just as a thorn can be used to remove another thorn lodged in the body, so this body can be used to dislodge us from entanglement in material nature. Since the body is temporary, why take pride in wealth, which comes and goes like dust on the feet? To one with the vision of eternity, wealth is as worthless as the dust accumulated on one’s feet. Similarly, why should we be proud of our youth, which is quickly lost? Indeed this very life can end at any moment.

The human body is like a vehicle and our life span is like having limited fuel meant to last for a specific period. We can either waste this rare opportunity in idle pursuits, which sow the seeds of karma to be reaped in future births, or use it intelligently to make spiritual progress and get out of material entanglement altogether. It all depends on whether one takes the long view or the short view of the opportunity that human life affords. One way we measure intelligence is the person’s capacity to take the long view.

The long view in this context is to work toward a destination having a favorable atmosphere, affectionate people, ample food, no disease, nor old age, or death. If, however, one misuses the vehicle, his life will end in lamentation for having failed to reach the goal, and in the next life a different form of body may make it impossible to try again. It may take many lifetimes before the human form is awarded again. Hence this valuable human life should not be misused for serving the senses, as this will only lead to an animal birth. We should strive for our permanent destination, the spiritual world.

*yuṣmābhir atisañcayaḥ kṛtaḥ tasyāyaṁ doṣaḥ śṛṇu*

**You hoarded more than necessary, and this is the ill-effect. Please listen....**

### Text 149

*upārjitānāṁ vittānāṁ tyāga eva hi rakṣaṇam*  
*taḍāgodara-saṁsthānāṁ parīvāhaivāmbhasām*

**Перевод:**

**A pond is protected by an outlet that releases excess water, and wealth is protected by offering one’s earnings in charity.**

**Комментарий:**

The next eight verses explain the importance of giving charity. Wealth is more fleeting than the body, and is best used in the service of the Lord; otherwise one’s relatives may squander it, or it will be taken by thieves. If one is not charitable, he should at least use his savings for himself; otherwise he is compared to the honeybee, who hoards honey only so others can enjoy it.

anyac ca — Also,

### Text 150

*yad adho 'dhaḥ kṣitau vittaṁ nicakhāna mitampacaḥ*  
*tad-adho nilayaṁ gantuṁ cakre panthānam agrataḥ*

**Перевод:**

**A miser who digs deeper and deeper into the earth to hide his wealth paves the road to hell.**

**Комментарий:**

Before banks existed people used to bury their wealth. Here is it said that one who does so only prepares his path to hell. Since buried wealth lies untouched and hidden indefinitely, it too is as good as lost in the nether regions. Srimad-Bhagavatam states, “Generally, the wealth of misers never allows them any happiness. In this life it causes their selftorment, and when they die it sends them to hell. (11. 23. 15).” A miser cannot be promoted to heaven, as that requires a charitable disposition.

Anyac ca — Furthermore,

### Text 151

*nija-saukhyaṁ nirundhāno yo dhanārjanam icchati*  
*parārtha-bhāra-vāhīva sa kleśasyaiva bhājanam*

**Перевод:**

**Like an ass who carries loads for others, a person who desires to earn wealth even at the expense of his own peace and comfort, is an abode of misery.**

**Комментарий:**

Aparan ca — Moreover,

### Text 152

*dānopabhoga-hīnena dhanena dhanino yadi*  
*bhavāmaḥ kiṁ na tenaiva dhanena dhanino vayam*

**Перевод:**

**If a man who neither gives charity nor enjoys his wealth is considered rich, then why can’t we be called rich on account of the same wealth?**

**Комментарий:**

Some misers bank huge sums of money, but never use it. If such a person is considered rich, why can’t a poor man claim the same money and declare himself rich as well? In the practical sense, the miser and the poor man are equal, as neither uses the money. Actual wealth is that which is given in charity or used for personal maintenance, for what is hoarded in the bank is enjoyed by someone else. Many leaders, politicians, actors, swamis, and gurus keep Swiss Bank accounts which are never touched, and when they die, the money is claimed by the bank. What good is such wealth?

### Text 153

*dhanena kiṁ yo na dadāti nāśnute*  
*balena kiṁ yaś ca ripūn na yādhatte*  
*śrutena kiṁ yo na ca dharmam ācaret*  
*kim ātmanā yo na jitendriyo bhavet*

**Перевод:**

**What is the value of wealth, if one neither gives in charity, nor enjoys it? What is the value of strength, if one cannot conquer an enemy? What is the value of hearing scripture, if one does not follow his prescribed duty? What is the value of human life, if one cannot control his senses?**

### Text 154

*asambhogena sāmānyaṁ kṛpaṇasya dhanaṁ paraiḥ*  
*asyedam iti sambandho hānau duḥkhena gamyate*

**Перевод:**

**Since a miserly man does not use his wealth, it is like common property. The real owner is he who feels sorrow at its loss.**

**Комментарий:**

As previously explained, one can feel rich by considering the wealth of others as his own. But if that wealth is somehow lost, the miserly owner will alone lament, indicating that he is the real owner. The purport is that the miserly person’s wealth gives only pain. He earns it by hard labor, protects it with difficulty, and when it is lost, he feels miserable; but it is enjoyed only by others. Better to enjoy it. Better still is to give charity and earn piety. This is the only way to protect wealth.

### Text 155

***na devāya na viprāya na bandhubhyo na cātmane***  
***kṛpaṇasya dhanaṁ yāti vahni-taskara-pārthivaiḥ***

**Перевод:**

**The miser’s wealth is not used for the deity, brahmanas, relatives, nor for himself; it is either destroyed by fire, stolen, or taken away by the government.**

### Text 156

*dānaṁ priya-vāk-sahitaṁ*  
*jñānam agarvaṁ kṣamānvitaṁ sauryam*  
*tyāgaṁ sahitaṁ ca vittaṁ*  
*durlabham etac catur bhadram*

**Перевод:**

**These four beneficial combinations are very rare: charity accompanied by sweet words; knowledge without pride; valor with forbearance; and wealth with a charitable disposition.**

**Комментарий:**

Charity should be given respectfully to a suitable person. If given begrudgingly, it is considered charity in the mode of ignorance (Bg. 17. 22). When giving charity, the donor should understand the recipient to be doing him a favor by decreasing his attachment to wealth, and allowing him to accrue piety in return. The recipient becomes indebted, and must compensate for the donation in this life or the next. A charitable person feels happy when he finds a suitable recipient. Munificent donors are called danavira, or heroic in giving charity. Bali Maharaja, for example, was extremely pleased when Vamanadeva came to beg charity from him, and was disappointed when the Lord wanted only three steps of land. Bali said:

*māṁ vacobhiḥ samārādhya*  
*lokānām ekam īśvaram*  
*pada-trayaṁ vṛṇīte yo*  
*'buddhimān dvīpa-dāśuṣam*  
*na pumān mām upavrajya*  
*bhūyo yācitum arhati*  
*tasmād vṛttikarīṁ bhūmiṁ*  
*vaṭo kāmaṁ pratīccha me*

**I am able to give You an entire island because I am the proprietor of the three divisions of the universe. You have come to take something from me and have pleased me by Your sweet words, but You are asking only three paces of land. Therefore You are not very intelligent.**  
**O small boy, one who approaches me to beg something should not have to ask anything more, anywhere. Therefore, if You wish, You may ask from me as much land as will suffice to maintain You according to Your needs. (Bhag. 8. 19. 19, 20)**

It is hard for a common man to appreciate how rich persons can feel elated by giving away portions of their hard-earned wealth. Those who practice giving charity in the right spirit or out of the goodness of their heart know well the benefits of the saying “It is better to give than to receive.” Human life is an opportunity for conditioned souls to expand their being by serving others. The more we serve others, the more we grow in spirit, the more our heart softens. Of course, this is perfected when done in devotional service to the Lord. Giving charity to a qualified recipient and in the proper spirit blesses both the giver and the receiver. Whenever we extend ourselves on another’s behalf we grow, and the benefit to the giver surpasses that of getting charity or any service.

uktaṁ ca — It is also said:

*kartavyaḥ sañcayo nityaṁ na tu kāryo 'tisañcayaḥ \|*  
*atisañcaya-śīlo 'yaṁ dhanuṣā jambuko hataḥ*

**Перевод:**

**One should always accumulate wealth, but in excess it is dangerous; the jackal who hoarded was killed by the bow.**

**Комментарий:**

*tāv āhatuḥ-katham etat? mantharaḥ kathayati —*

**Both of them asked, “How was that?” Manthara relates the story.**

## Story 5

*āsīt kalyāṇa-kaṭaka-vāstavyo bhairavo nāma vyādhaḥ sa caikadā māṁsa-lubdho dhanur ādāya mṛgam anviṣyan vindhyāṭavī-madhyaṁ gataḥ tatra tena mṛga eko vyāpāditaḥ tato mṛgam ādāya gacchatā tena ghorākṛtiḥ śūkaro dṛṣṭaḥ tatas tena mṛgaṁ bhūmau nidhāya śūkaraḥ śareṇa hataḥ śūkareṇāpy āgatya pralaya-ghana-ghora-garjanaṁ kurvāṇena sa vyādho muṣka-deśe hataḥ chinna-druma iva papāta  tathā coktam —*

**Перевод:**

**In the country of Kalyanakataka lived a hunter named Bhairava. Avaricious for meat, he went to the Vindhya mountains, where he killed a deer with his bow. While carrying the deer on his back, he spotted a ferocious boar. He set the deer down and pierced the boar with an arrow. The boar rumbled like the cloud of devastation and charged the hunter, striking him in the groin, forcing him to the ground like a tree cut at the root. Therefore it is said...**

### Text 158

*jalam agnir viṣaṁ śastaṁ kṣud vyādhiḥ patanaṁ gireḥ \|*  
*nimittaṁ kiñcid āsādya dehī prāṇair vimucyate*

**Перевод:**

**The embodied soul gives up his body because of accidents caused by water, fire, poison, weapons, hunger, disease, or falling from a mountain.**

**Комментарий:**

*atha tayoḥ pādāsphālanena ekaḥ sarpo 'pi mṛtaḥ atrāntare dīrgharāvo nāma jambukaḥ paribhramanāhārārthā tān mṛtān mṛga-vyādha-sarpa-śūkarān apaśyat ālokyācintayac ca-aho bhāgyam! adya mahad bhojyaṁ me samupasthitam athavā*

**During this incident, a snake was trampled and killed. After some time, a jackal named Dirgharava (one who howls deeply), was wandering in search of food and came upon the dead deer, hunter, snake and boar. He thought, “Oh, what good fortune. Today I have come upon a big feast. On..”**

### Text 159

*acintitāni duḥkhāni yathaivāyānti dehinām \|*  
*sukhāny api tathā manye daivam atrātiricyate*

**Перевод:**

**Just as misfortune descends unexpectedly on living beings, so do happy occasions. In these matters, fate is certainly supreme.**

**Комментарий:**

Everyone strives for happiness and to avoid distress. No one consciously works to get misery, yet everyone gets their share. This proves that happiness and misery do not depend on our plans, but on destiny. This is confirmed by Prahlada Maharaja:

*sukham aindriyakaṁ daityā*  
*deha-yogena dehinām*  
*sarvatra labhyate daivād*  
*yathā duḥkham ayatnataḥ*

**My dear friends born of demoniac families, the happiness perceived with reference to the sense objects by contact with the body can be obtained in any form of life, according to one’s past fruitive activities. Such happiness is automatically obtained without endeavor, just as we obtain distress. (Bhag. 7. 6. 3)**

Therefore we should not waste our precious human life seeking material happiness, which will come automatically. Narada Muni advised Vyasadeva, “Persons who are actually intelligent and philosophically inclined should endeavor only for that purposeful end which is not obtainable even by wandering from the topmost planet \[Brahmaloka\] down to the lowest planet \[Patala\]. As far as happiness derived from sense enjoyment is concerned, it can be obtained automatically in course of time, just as in course of time we obtain miseries even though we do not desire them.” (Bhag. 1. 5. 18) An intelligent man should strive for the ultimate goal of life — love of God, which can be obtained by the association of a pure devotee.

*Bhavatu esam mamsaih masa-trayam samadhikam bhojanam me bhavisyati.*

**Very well. I shall feed on this meat for three months.**

### Text 160

*māsam ekaṁ naro yāti dvau māsau mṛga-śūkarau \|*  
*ahir ekaṁ dinaṁ yāti adya bhakṣyo dhanurguṇaḥ*

**Перевод:**

**The human’s flesh will last for one month; the deer and boar for two. The snake will be enough for a day, and today I will be content with eating the bow string.**

tataḥ prathama-bubhukṣāyām idaṁ niḥsvādu kodaṇḍa-lagnaṁ snāyu-bandhanaṁ khādāmi, ity uktvā tathākarot \| tataś chinne snāyu-bandhane drutam utpatitena dhanuṣā hṛdi nirbhinnaḥ sa dīrgharāvaḥ pañcatvaṁ gataḥ \| ato 'haṁ bravīmi kartavyaḥ sañcayo nityam ity ādi \| tathā ca—

*Therefore, when hunger strikes I will eat the insipid bow string made of sinew. Thus he began gnawing on it. When the string snapped, the bow pierced Dirgharava's heart and killed him. Therefore I say “One should accumulate....”*

**Комментарий:**

Moreover:

In decision-making one should carefully consider all pros and cons. Projects fail because planning is not pragmatic, or because of personal ambition to amass wealth, name, fame, or followers. In life the foolhardy, driven by selfish motives, take unnecessary risks; and the fearful, not wanting to make any mistakes, accomplish nothing at all. Between these two extremes is the discriminating or pragmatic person who takes calculated risks. Dirgharava was blinded by his greed and failed to take into account the danger of breaking the bow string. Just as one is attracted to the jewel on the head of a serpent and forgets the poisonous fangs, when greed covers the intelligence one cannot clearly see the disadvantages of an act.

### Text 161

*yad dadāti yad aśnāti tad eva dhanino dhanam \|*  
*anye mṛtasya krīḍanti dārair api dhanair api*

**Перевод:**

**Only that wealth one gives in charity or enjoys is considered his. Otherwise, after his death, his wealth and even his wife are enjoyed by others.**

**Комментарий:**

As indicated in the next verse, wealth given in charity, or used for personal enjoyment, is one’s real wealth. The rest is enjoyed by others. When death closes our eyes, all our possessions will remain here, but whatever we have given in charity goes with us in the form of pious credits. Thus in reality, our actual wealth is what we have donated in charity.

kiṁ ca - And

### Text 162

*yad dadāsi viśiṣṭebhyo yac cāśnāsi dine dine \|*  
*tat te vittam ahaṁ manye śeṣaṁ kasyāpi rakṣasi*

**Перевод:**

**Wealth given in charity to qualified people, and that which is enjoyed every day, actually belongs to you. The rest is protected for someone else.**

**Комментарий:**

*yātu, kim idānīm atikrāntopavarṇanena yataḥ—*

**Anyway, forget it. There is no use in digging up that which has been buried. Because...**

### Text 163

*nāprāyam abhivāñchanti naṣṭaṁ necchanti śocitum \|*  
*āpatsv api na muhyanti narāḥ paṇḍita-buddhayaḥ*

**Перевод:**

**The learned do not desire the unattainable and do not lament for what is lost; nor arc they bewildered by distress.**

**Комментарий:**

One who has wisdom is called a pandita. pandah sanjata asmin iti panditah. He is never bewildered and he accepts all situations in life with a peaceful mind. Lord Kṛṣṇa advises Arjuna to be equipoised in all circumstances as this is a sign of a man with fixed intelligence (Bg. 2. 56, 57):

*duḥkheṣv anudvigna-manāḥ*  
*sukheṣu vigata-spṛhaḥ*  
*vīta-rāga-bhaya-krodhaḥ*  
*sthita-dhīr munir ucyate*

**One who is not disturbed in mind even amidst the threefold miseries or elated when there is happiness, and who is free from attachment, fear and anger, is called a sage of steady mind.**

*yaḥ sarvatrānabhisnehas*  
*tat tat prāpya śubhāśubham*  
*nābhinandati na dveṣṭi*  
*tasya prajñā pratiṣṭhitā*

**In the material world, one who is unaffected by whatever good or evil he may obtain, neither praising it nor despising it, is firmly fixed in perfect knowledge.**

Such a person is qualified to taste immortality, so'mrtatvaya kalpate (Bg. 2. 15). On the contrary, one who laments is called a sudra, socati iti sudrah. He suffers in this world life after life.

*tat sakhe! sarvadā tvayā sotsāhena bhavitavyam, yataḥ—*

**Thus, my friend, always remain enthusiastic. Because.**

### Text 164

*śāstrāṇy adhītyāpi bhavanti mūrkhā*  
*yas tu kriyāvān puruṣaḥ sa vidvān \|*  
*sucintitaṁ cauṣadham āturāṇāṁ*  
*na nāma-mātreṇa karoty arogam*

**Перевод:**

**Just as the name of a medicine cannot cure a disease, so one who knows scripture but fails to act on it, is not considered learned. Such people remain foolish even after much study.**

**Комментарий:**

Like a parrot who recites verses or chants Hare Kṛṣṇa, some people don’t understand how to apply their acquired knowledge in adversity. When attacked by the cat, the parrot squawks uselessly. Similarly, one who laments in adverse circumstances cannot be called a pandita, or scholar. When Arjuna lamented on the battlefield, Lord Kṛṣṇa rebuked him for his feeble-mindedness and pseudo scholarship. In critical times a real scholar practices the teachings of sastra. A disease isn’t cured merely by hearing about the medicine; one must ingest it. Similarly, scriptures are not simply meant for hearing, but to be implemented. Otherwise knowledge becomes a burden.

Anyac ca — Also:

### Text 165

*na svalpam apy adhyavasāya-bhīroḥ*  
*karoti vijñāna-vidhir guṇaṁ hi \|*  
*andhasya kiṁ hasta-tala-sthito 'pi*  
*prakāśayaty artham iha pradīpaḥ*

**Перевод:**

**Just as a lamp in the hand of a blind man cannot help him see, so- scriptural instruction yields no fruit for he who does not act on it with determination.**

**Комментарий:**

Sastra is like a lamp, yet one who studies it without putting it to practical use, is compared to a blind man, because although the lamp sheds light, the blind man cannot take advantage. As a lamp only serves those who have the power to see, so the sastra enlightens those who understand it and have the intelligence, courage, and faith to put it into practice. The knowledge of one who fails to act is like a load of books on the back of an ass. To be fruitful, sastric knowledge must be put into practice.

*tad atra sakhe daśātiśeṣEṇa śāntiḥ karaṇīyā etad apy atikaṣṭaṁ tvayā na mantavyam. Yatah—*

**Thus my friend, you should reconcile the present situation and not let it overcome you, as...**

### Text 166

*sukham āpatitaṁ sevyaṁ duḥkham āpatitaṁ tathā \|*  
*cakravat parivartante duḥkhāni ca sukhāni ca*

**Перевод:**

**Happiness and distress revolve like a wheel. One should accept them, since they come of their own accord.**

**Комментарий:**

Happiness and distress turn like a wheel — one follows the other around and around. Since these changes result from one’s past deeds, why worry about the inevitable? As Lord Kṛṣṇa advises, tasmād aparihārye 'rthe na tvaṁ śocitum arhasi (Bg. 2. 27). Better to tolerate all situations without being disturbed. Such a tolerant person is called dhira, or one who acts with undisturbed intelligence.

### Text 167

*nipānam iva maṇḍūkāḥ saraḥ pūrṇam ivāṇḍajāḥ \|*  
*sodyogaṁ naram āyānti vivaśāḥ sarva-sampadaḥ*

**Перевод:**

**Just as frogs naturally approach a pond, and birds a full lake, similarly, ail opulences forcibly approach a man of enterprise.**

### Text 168

*utsāha-saṁpannam adīrgha-sūtraṁ kriyā-vidhijñaṁ vyasaneṣv asaktam \|*  
*śūraṁ kṛtajñaṁ dṛḍha-sauhṛdaṁ ca- lakṣmīḥ svayaṁ vāñchati vāsa-hetoḥ*

**Перевод:**

**Fortune resides with one full of enthusiasm, alacrity, expertise, who is free from vices, noble, grateful, and firm in friendship.**

**Комментарий:**

Those desiring success must acquire these qualities. The embodiment of fortune or success is the goddess Laksmi Devi, and it is known that She does not like defects. After Her birth from the ocean of milk, Laksmi Devi was allowed to select her consort from among all the demigods. Understanding Lord Visnu to be flawless, She chose Him as Her master and His chest as Her residence. It is understood from this verse that she resides with men in proportion to their having the qualities mentioned here. Women of good character are naturally attracted to men with the above qualities.

viśeṣataś ca — And particularly,

### Text 169

*vināpy arthair dhīraḥ spṛśati bahumānonnati-padaṁ*  
*samāyukto 'py arthaiḥ paribhava-padaṁ yāti kṛpaṇaḥ \|*  
*svabhāvād udbhūtāṁ guṇa-samudayāvāpti-viṣayāṁ*  
*dyutiṁ saiṁhīṁ śvā kiṁ dhṛta-kanaka-mālo 'pi labhate*

**Перевод:**

**Just as a dog, even if adorned with a golden necklace, cannot attain the natural gallantry of a lion, so a miser, although endowed with many riches, is defeated. A sober man, however, though devoid of riches, achieves a highly honorable position.**

### Text 170

*dhanavān iti hi madas te kiṁ gata-vibhavo viṣādam upayāsi \|*  
*kara-nihata-kanduka-samāḥ pātotpātā manuṣyāṇām*

**Перевод:**

**Why be proud when wealth appears, and why lament when it disappears? One experiences ups and downs, like a bouncing ball struck by the hand.**

**Комментарий:**

As mentioned before, the material world is a place of duality. Naturally, therefore, everything changes into its opposite — fortune into misfortune, summer into winter, youth into old age, heat into cold, happiness into distress. Those who become attached to either pole of duality never have peace or equilibrium. A learned person is one who tolerates these changes. The conception that this is good and this is bad is deep in the heart of the conditioned souls. It is difficult to rise above it. Yet as long as one thinks in this way, one remains a victim of duality. Therefore, Caitanya Mahaprabhu wanted us to become aware of the true nature of material existence (CC. Antya 4. 176):

*'dvaite' bhadrābhadra-jñāna, saba—'manodharma'*  
*'ei bhāla, ei manda, '—ei saba 'bhrama'*

**In the material world, conceptions of good and bad are all mental speculations. Therefore, saying, “This is good, and this is bad,” is all a mistake.**

From the spiritual point of view, it is all bad, because one becomes entangled in karma either by the so-called good or the so-called bad. The best course, therefore, is to tolerate the dualities and work on achieving life’s goal, loving devotional service to God, who is Kṛṣṇa. Then everything becomes good. As practical as this conclusion is, it is difficult to learn. Material nature is tricky; we become entangled from so many subtle directions. Even persons professing to be on the path of spiritual life have difficulty tolerating the dualities of material nature.

### Text 171

*vṛtty-arthaṁ nāticeṣṭate sā hi dhātraiva nirmitā \|*  
*garbhād utpatite jantau mātuḥ prasravataḥ stanau*

**Перевод:**

**Just as milk begins to flow from the mother’s breast as soon as the child is born, so one’s maintenance is already ordained by the Lord. Thus there is no need to overendeavor.**

**Комментарий:**

Economic development is not the goal of life. Rather, “Life’s desires should never be directed toward sense gratification. One should desire a healthy life, or self-preservation, since a human being is meant for inquiry about the Absolute Truth. Nothing else should be the goal of one’s works” (Bhag. 1. 2. 10). A Sannyasi especially should completely depend upon the Lord for his maintenance, keeping in mind the following verse.

api ca sakhe śṛṇu — Moreover, listen my friend:

### Text 172

*yena śuklī-kṛtā haṁsāḥ śukāś ca haritīkṛtāḥ \|*  
*mayūrāś citritā yena sa te vṛttiṁ vidhāsyati*

**Перевод:**

**That Lord who has made the swans white, parrots green, and peacocks multicolored, shall arrange for your maintenance.**

**Комментарий:**

The next eight verses are directed to those pursuing detachment on the path of jnana marga. However, those on the path of devotion do not care for such dry renunciation, though it may help those overly attached to wealth. In Bhakti-rasamrta-sindhu, Srila Rupa Gosvami advises:

*jñāna-vairāgyayor bhakti-praveśāyopayogitā*  
*īṣat prathamam eveti nāṅgatvam ucitaṁ tayoḥ*

**Knowledge and renunciation have some function for those entering bhakti, though they are not to be considered part of bhakti.**

Dry renunciation is antagonistic to devotional service; therefore, devotees of the Lord embrace yukta-vairagya, or balanced renunciation. They do not reject wealth, nor the earning of it, but use wealth with detachment for the pleasure of the Lord. This practice is superior to the dry renunciation described below, which is impractical. Since nothing belongs to us, what can we renounce?

Sri Isopanisad explains, “Everything animate and inanimate that is within the universe is controlled and owned by the Lord.” This understanding inspires the devotee to use everything in the service of the Lord and offers practical guidance on being detached. One need not renounce wealth, but must renounce attachment to it, for that is the source of problems. When the devotee understands that everything actually belongs to the Lord, he should be attached to protecting everything for His pleasure. This attachment is not harmful. Mother Yasoda stopped breast-feeding Kṛṣṇa to prevent the milk on the stove from boiling over. That milk was meant for Kṛṣṇa and she was attached to saving it for His pleasure. This type of attachment is never harmful; rather it is most beneficial.

If a *Sannyasi* is selfishly attached to his hut (or palace), he is no better than a king attached to his palace. Selfish attachment is detrimental, regardless of the size of the object. Therefore, those desiring to progress in life must break all selfish attachment by recognizing that everything belongs to the Lord and by using it all in His service.

*aparaṁ ca satāṁ rahasyaṁ śṛṇu, mitra!*

**And listen to another secret of great people...**

### Text 173

*janayanty arjane duḥkhaṁ tāpayanti vipattiṣu \|*

**Перевод:**

**Wealth is troublesome to earn, causes grief when it is lost, and in prosperity it bewilders. How can one say it is conducive to happiness?**

*dharmārdhaṁ yasya vittehā varaṁ tasya nirīhatā \|*

**Washing mud off one’s feet is good, but it is better not to get them muddy; similarly, desiring wealth for religious activities is good, but better not to desire wealth at all.**

**Комментарий:**

Often people do sinful activities, then to counter the sinful reactions they perform religious activities, for which they require wealth. Here the author recommends that it is better not to act sinfully. Then one need not desire wealth to free oneself of the reactions.

yataḥ — Because,

**Комментарий:**

*anyac ca bhrātaḥ śṛṇu —*

*dhanaṁ tāvad asulabhaṁ labdhaṁ kṛcchreṇa pālyate \|*  
*labdha-nāśo yathā mṛtyus tasmād etan na cintayet*

**Перевод:**

**Wealth is difficult to earn, then difficult to keep, and when lost, is like death. Better not to think of it.**

### Text 179

*sā tṛṣṇā cet parityaktā ko daridraḥ ka īśvaraḥ \|*  
*tasyāś cet prasaro datto dāsyaṁ ca śirasi sthitam*

**Перевод:**

**If desire is abandoned, then who is poor and who is wealthy? But if one harbors desire, he only invites slavery.**

**Комментарий:**

As soon as a person cherishes material desires, he accepts some form of slavery, because material desires are fulfilled only through sense objects, which are outside our body. To obtain these objects, we become dependent on others, even if they are servants, disciples, relatives, or friends. One who becomes free from material desire is not anybody’s hired servant. When a renunciate restricts his desires, he is respected even by the rich. Sukadeva says that hope or desire is the greatest misery, and freedom from it is the supreme bliss, āśā hi paramaṁ duḥkhaṁ nairāśyaṁ paramaṁ sukham.

aparaṁ ca— Furthermore,

### Text 180

*yad yad eva hi vāñcheta tato vāñchā pravartate \|*  
*prāpta evārthataḥ so 'rtho yato vāñchā nivartate*

**Перевод:**

**As one hankers for something, his desire for it increases (even after obtaining it). One actually obtains an object, if, after possessing it, he loses the desire for it.**

**Комментарий:**

A mirage never quenches one’s thirst; instead, one’s thirst increases while trying to reach it. Similarly, material desires do not satisfy the self; they only breed more desires. Once a desire is fulfilled, it opens new avenues for further desires, creating a never-ending, vicious cycle. According to Manu Smrti (2. 94),

*na jātu kāmaḥ kāmānām*  
*upabhogena śāṁyati*  
*haviṣā kṛṣṇa-vartmeva*  
*bhūya evābhivardhate*

**Desires are never satisfied by possessing sense objects; rather they get stronger. Just as pouring ghee on a blazing fire adds to the fire’s intensity, the desires of a lusty person are not satisfied by enjoying the sense objects. Rather, his desires increase.**

If, however, one desires to serve the Supreme Lord, then all his desires are fulfilled. Thus Sukadeva Gosvami says:

*akāmaḥ sarva-kāmo vā*  
*mokṣa-kāma udāra-dhīḥ*  
*tīvreṇa bhakti-yogena*  
*yajeta puruṣaṁ param*

**A person who has broader intelligence, whether he be full of all material desire, without any material desire, or desiring liberation, must by all means worship the supreme whole, the Personality of Godhead. (Bhag. 2. 3. 10):**

Lord Kṛṣṇa is called Kamadeva, the deity of desires. Since He is the source of everything, the possessor of all opulence, one who attains Him, satisfies all his desires. Everyone is looking for happiness in some form or other, forgetting that Lord Kṛṣṇa is anandamaya, the embodiment of bliss, and He is ananta, unlimited. If one perfectly meditates on Kṛṣṇa as Kamadeva, the fulfiller of all desires, he attains unlimited bliss, and has no more desires to fulfill.

*kiṁ bahunā, viśrambhālāpair mayaiva sahātra kālo nīyatām yataḥ —*

**What more can I say? Just spend your time in my company speaking confidentially, for...**

### Text 181

*āmraṇāntāḥ praṇayāḥ kopāś ca kṣaṇa-bhaṅgurāḥ \|*  
*parityāgāś ca niḥsaṅgā na bhavanti mahātmanām*

**Перевод:**

**The love of the saintly lasts until death, their anger is momentary, and they give charity without ulterior motive.**

**Комментарий:**

*iti śrutvā laghupatanako brūte-dhanyo 'si manthara! sarvathā āśrayaṇīyo 'si yataḥ — ”*

**Hearing this, Laghupatanaka exclaimed, “O Manthara, praises! You are worth taking shelter of, because..**

### Text 182

*santa eva satāṁ nityam āpad-uddharaṇa-kṣamāḥ \|*  
*gajānāṁ paṅka-magnānāṁ gajā eva dhurandharāḥ*

**Перевод:**

**Just as only elephants can pull another elephant out of a marsh, only a saint can save other saints from adversity.**

**Комментарий:**

When a saintly person has a problem, he seeks help from other saintly persons, not from materialists. In this regard Vidura advises (Vidura- niti 2. 46):

*gatirātmavatāṁ santaḥ santa eva satāṁ gatiḥ*  
*asatāṁ ca gatiḥ santo na tvasantaḥ satāṁ gatiḥ*

**The saintly give shelter to all living beings; indeed they are the shelter of other saints. They are also the shelter of rogues; but rogues can never be the shelter of saints.**

A rogue cannot appreciate the problems of a saint, what to speak of alleviating them. Saintly association, therefore, is the only panacea for all problems, and one must acquire it by all means. King Nimi has said, “I consider the association of saints who arc dear to Lord Kṛṣṇa rarer even than human birth” (Bhag. 11. 2. 29).

Earlier the author said that the poisonous tree of the material world has two succulent fruits — hearing scripture and associating with saints. Without saintly association, one cannot relish scriptures. Thus one is denied both desirable fruits. Only the poisonous fruits remain. To impede saintly association means to deny one the goal of life, and to supply poison. Victims of such an imposition are most unfortunate, as are those who give up saintly association for material benefit in the name of unity or spiritual progress.

### Text 183

*ślāghyaḥ sa eko bhuvi mānavānāṁ*  
*sa uttamaḥ sat-puruṣaḥ sa dhanyaḥ \|*  
*yasyārthino vā śaraṇāgatā vā*  
*nāśāvibhaṅgā vimukhāḥ prayānti*

**Перевод:**

**He who never disappoints beggars and others seeking his shelter, is saintly, glorious, the best, and is alone worthy of praise.**

**Комментарий:**

*tad evaṁ te svecchāhāra-vihāraṁ kurvāṇāḥ santuṣṭāḥ sukhaṁ nivasanti sma atha kadācit citrāṅga-nāmā mṛgaḥ kenāpi trāsitas tatrāgatya militaḥ tat-paścād āyāntaṁ bhaya-hetuṁ sambhāvya mantharo jalaṁ praviṣṭaḥ mūṣikaś ca vivaraṁ gataḥ, kāko 'pi uḍḍīya vṛkṣāgram ārūḍhaḥ tato laghupatanakena sudūraṁ nirūpya bhaya-hetur na ko 'py avalambitaḥ paścāt tad-vacanād āgatya punaḥ sarve militvā tatraivopaviṣṭāḥ manthareṇoktaṁ-bhadra mṛga! kuśalaṁ te? svecchayā udakādyāhāro 'nubhūyatām atrāvasthānena vanam idaṁ sanāthīkriyatām citrāṅgo brūte-lubdhaka-trāsito 'haṁ bhavatāṁ śaraṇam āgataḥ tataś ca, bhavadbhiḥ saha mitratvam icchāmi bhavantaś ca anukampayantu maitryeṇa yataḥ —*

**Thus feeling happy and satisfied, they sported and ate at will. Once a frightened deer named Citranga came to join them. Terrified, Manthara entered the water, the mouse into his hole, and the crow perched himself high in a tree. Though the crow, Laghupatanaka, surveyed a great distance, he could not detect the source of fear. Afterwards, hearing the crow’s words, they all came out and sat together.**  
**Manthara asked, “O gentle deer, are you well? Stay here and enjoy the grass and water at your will”.**  
**Citranga said, “Fearing a hunter I have come to take shelter of you. Please give me your friendship, because...”**

**Комментарий:**

A surrendered soul should be protected at all costs. Maharaja Sibi for example, protected the pigeon being chased by a hawk, even to the extent of offering flesh from his own body to appease the hawk. Similarly, Lord Kṛṣṇa protects His devotees, kaunteya pratijanihi na me bhaktah pranasyati.

*hiraṇyako 'py avadat-mitratvaṁ tāvad asmābhiḥ saha, ayatnena niṣpannaṁ bhavataḥ yataḥ — "*

**Hiranyaka replied, “You have secured our friendship without endeavor, because...**

### Text 185

*aurasaṁ kṛta-sambandhaṁ tathā vaṁśa-kramāgatam \|*
